#268. My hometown shows that high streets have to change or die

My hometown shows that high streets have to change or die

The empty building that used to be the home of Marks & Spencer in Aberdeen is one of the first things you see when you arrive off the train and come into the city centre. The sign has gone, though the shape of the lettering can still be made out; the windows are blacked out and the doors boarded up.

This is my hometown. As a teenager, I spent hours on Union Street, its main thoroughfare, nicknamed the Granite Mile after the local silver-grey stone. It was the place to try on all the latest fashions from the likes of Clockhouse and Chelsea Girl.

The street used to be packed with shoppers from across the north-east of Scotland.

"I come to Union Street to take a bus somewhere else... there's nothing to shop for nowâ¦ it's horrendous," complains Marie Sim, a lifelong Aberdeen resident.

It's a similar story across the country: High Streets, once bursting with shops, hit first by the rise of out-of-town retail centres and then by online commerce.

But Aberdeen is in the thick of an ambitious turnaround plan. The port city has essentially declared war on its empty shop problem, taking an unusually hands-on approach to filling vacant units and bringing long-neglected upper floors of buildings into use.

The Marks & Spencer flagship store in Aberdeen closed in August, 80 years after it opened there

On top of that, the council is pouring tens of millions into trying to make the city centre a destination again. Like many other places, it's leaning into the "experience economy", with a new indoor food and drink market and a major redevelopment of the seafront.

High Streets are integral to how we feel about where we live. The sight of vacant shops and graffiti can quickly become a vicious circle, deterring shoppers, discouraging investment and leading to anti-social behaviour.

Politicians of all stripes have been trying for years to breathe new life into town centres - and Andy Burnham, who has made a focus on "place" part of his political identity, is the latest. He argues they should become "symbols of Britain's renaissance".

But can it be achieved? And if they're to survive, do town centres need a fundamental rethink?

One in eight properties on British High Streets are currently vacant, according to Green Street, the property and retail analytics company, a number that's stayed stubbornly high for years.

"If there was a silver bullet, it would have been fired years ago," says Jackie Sadek, a former government adviser on urban regeneration, who has spent nearly four decades working in town centre renewal.

One of the biggest obstacles is fractured ownership. Shops and buildings are often owned by dozens of different landlords, sometimes through offshore companies or complex structures, each with their own interests and priorities. That can slow decision-making and make large-scale regeneration difficult.

But even with empty units that are available, you need to find someone willing to take them on.

One in eight properties on British High Streets are currently vacant

The cost of running a High Street business has also risen; employment costs are higher, and there's the burden of business rates.

Firms need customers. Footfall on many High Streets has been in long-term decline as shopping and services move online, and this enormous structural shift has yet to play out fully.

Two-thirds of town centre experts think there is still 20% to 40% too much retail space, according to a survey by retail body Revo and real estate consultancy Lambert Smith Hampton.

In Aberdeen's boom years of North Sea oil, from the late 1970s through to the early 2010s, it didn't have to work hard to fill its hotels and restaurants and get the cash tills ringing because of the wealth the energy industry brought to the city.

I remember weekends with friends as a teenager, spending whatever cash we had in the fashion chains and shoe shops. Later came the pubs and bar-hopping, and trips to the art deco Capitol Theatre (now office space), where I saw Duran Duran.

But by the end of 2022, Union Street had hit rock bottom. Aberdonians had long deserted the High Street for a shopping centre, which opened in 2009 next to the railway station.

"They'd fallen out of love with their city," says Bob Keiller, head of Our Union Street coalition, a community-led group formed after an emergency summit to reverse the decline.

The first thing it did was listen to what residents wanted, receiving thousands of submissions. Restoring a bit of civic pride and filling empty units became the focus.

It's been a co-ordinated effort, involving letting agents, the council, landlords, businesses and a big team of volunteers, led by Keiller, a former FTSE 100 CEO, who isn't getting paid. He has a map on his office wall, detailing the status of every property, unit by unit, with colour-coded Post-it notes.

They audited the street, tracking down absent landlords, and logging what was empty and why. Volunteers offered to give the empty properties a clean and a lick of paint, to make them more attractive to let. They also spruced up the streets, picking litter, weeding, and painting bins. A small team still goes out once a fortnight.

Bob Keiller heads a community-led group set up to help revive Aberdeen's Union Street

There's a website where all the available properties are collated; crucially, all of the financial incentives are also listed. These can include council fit-out grants (where a new business gets money to decorate premises), and a two-year discount from business rates.

In 2023, 57 of the 198 units on Union Street were empty. As of today, 33 have been filled, with a further 14 in the process of being let.

But it can feel like a game of whack-a-mole because since the initiative started, another 20 units have become vacant. "The key thing is, we're filling quicker than emptying," Keiller insists.

Keiller's operations director, David McKeith, describes it as putting an arm round the shoulder of those they can help and being a "wasp in the ear" of people who just say no.

At one end of the office, a small, young team produces social media content, linking visitors and residents to a new Our Aberdeen app, showing what the city can offer.

For Keiller, the "small wins" - like tackling graffiti - are needed to help change public perception.

"We've realised the psychology is not to do it once but be prepared to do it two or three times before people that do these things eventually say, 'there's no point in putting it there because somebody's going to paint over it.'"

Attention is also turning to the often-forgotten upper floors of buildings along the once-iconic Union Street. In a separate initiative run by the business group Aberdeen Inspired, experts are getting inside those vacant high-up spaces to identify the barriers to bringing them back into use.

Aberdeen sought the help of a High Street troubleshooter, Iain Nicholson, who's worked with dozens of towns and cities through his Vacant Shops Academy.

Out of more than 1,000 empty units he helped audit nationally, on average more than half didn't have a letting agent actively marketing the property. He says some councils don't even know who the landlords are.

Empty shops, he says, won't all be filled by the market alone. Towns and cities need to encourage a broader range of occupiers, from arts and culture to leisure, health and well-being.

He says what's striking about Aberdeen is its joined-up approach, and the fact people "rolled up their sleeves" and overcame differences. "That's not something you can yet say about everywhere."

Every town and city faces its own mix of challenges.

Nicholson also helped Crewe in Cheshire. As a smaller place than Aberdeen, it is focusing on arts, culture, and community, with a new women's centre opened this month.

And English councils are being encouraged to take a more interventionist role.

They can now force an empty commercial unit that's been vacant for at least a year to be offered to tenants through a High Street Rental Auction, even if the landlord doesn't want to let it.

A noodle bar (left) is one of the new businesses in the area. Parts of the city centre have been undergoing construction work (right)

It's early days but the mere prospect of losing control of a property is delivering some results. Bassetlaw Council in Nottinghamshire, an early adopter of the scheme, says more than 30 shops across the district have been let or are currently under offer as a result of the policy.

But filling empty units is only part of the challenge. The bigger question is what town and city centres are actually for.

Changing habits mean we won't need so many shops. Instead, it's argued, town centres will need more people living and working in them, with new homes and office space alongside services that can't be done online. With an ageing population, for example, bringing NHS health centres into the heart of a town could be a big opportunity.

In Aberdeen, the building that used to house BHS - a department store shuttered in 2016 - is being converted into an entrance plaza for the new food and drink market.

The physical appearance of Union Street is changing too. Like many Victorian shopping thoroughfares, it was built with a wide road (probably to give space to horses and carriages). Now parts of the pavements are being widened to make it a more pleasant place to spend time, though many Aberdonians are weary of the disruption, and of new traffic restrictions.

Money is a big barrier. Aberdeen's new indoor food and drink market is costing Â£40m, funded by the council and the UK Levelling Up Fund. In other places, regeneration schemes can struggle because the cost of redevelopment outweighs the likely financial return, making support from the public sector essential. And the economics are becoming more challenging.

"Higher interest rates, rising costs of construction and other issues are impacting the viability and delivery of new housing and big redevelopment projects," says Steve Norris, a director at Lambert Smith Hampton. He says that big projects usually require cash from both the public and private sectors.

He wants to see "more creative ways of funding".

Part of Burnham's plan is to give local leaders more freedom to invest in town centres and drive regeneration, building on the approach he has championed in Greater Manchester. But bigger projects take years.

Volunteers have played a role in the redevelopment of Aberdeen's Union Street

Making it easier and cheaper to open and run a High Street business is one of the more immediate solutions being called for, with business rates a particular concern.

Burnham has promised further rates reform. All eyes are on this month's Budget to see how far he will go. All eyes are on this month's Budget to see how far he will go in England. Business rates are devolved in Scotland, Wales and Northern Ireland.

The chief executive of Next, Simon Wolfson, argues that the bigger challenge is not business rates, but planning reform.

"The most important thing that the government can do for high streets is let them develop," says Wolfson, who's also a Conservative peer.

Back in Aberdeen, there are some signs of traction.

Lauren Reid and her mother Rose moved their fashion boutique to Union Street in 2024. A council grant helped with the refurbishment after they decided to make the leap.

Can they make the sums add up? "We're getting there," she says.

She feels the same about the future of her street. "I always say Rome wasn't built in a day."

Lauren Reid and her mother Rose moved their fashion boutique to Union Street with the help of a council grant

Right now the area feels like a construction site - but another gaping hole will soon be filled. Robert Keane is a local property developer who in 2023 bought the Trinity shopping centre, once home to Debenhams. The space is now set to be re-occupied by a national leisure brand. A notoriously tiring stairway leading to the Trinity from the train station is also being replaced by an escalator.

"We saw a real good opportunity, because it was dying," he says.

He reckons he can make a go of it, cutting the cost of parking and overheads, resetting rents and being more nimble than previous far-flung owners.

It was a bold move given Lambert Smith Hampton suggests that more than one in 10 UK shopping centres is ripe for demolition.

But he's betting the dynamics of Aberdeen are finally changing.

"I think by 2027 it should look and feel like a different city."

That is certainly the hope. Yet walking along Union Street today, past the boarded-up shopfronts and roadworks, it can still feel like a city caught between its past and whatever comes next.

How the High Street became a window on our political instability

Why the UK is dithering over what to do about e-scooters

#266. Football kit giveaway aims to boost participation

Millie Bright said she wanted girls to feel "comfortable and confident" in their kit

Former England captain Millie Bright has helped hand out new kit to grassroots football teams in Leeds as part of an initiative aimed at keeping girls in sport for longer.

Thirty teams based in communities facing social or economic challenges were chosen by anti-discrimination charity Kick it Out for the donation.

Bright, who began her career at Doncaster Belles, said it was "vital" to keep girls playing sport.

"Every young girl deserves a kit, no matter what level they play at, and, whilst the top of the game is doing really well, real life still exists. People can't afford to give their children kits, but that doesn't mean they should go without."

Craig Gilmore set up Champions CIC, in Beeston, six years ago, to provide free Saturday morning training for children in the local area, as well as breakfast and lunch.

"With the cost of living, football's expensive [and] bills come first," he said.

"We work with a lot of families that can't afford the direct debits and the ongoing costs of grassroots sport nowadays.

"The new kit is amazing. It gives them that sense of belonging, that team pride, it's a massive boost for them."

Bright spoke with young footballers during the event in Leeds

The new kit was provided by Kick it Out, in partnership with the Morrisons Foundation.

They hope it will inspire these girls to continue sport as young women and adults.

"Girls' participation struggles with drop-out rates," said Gemma Clark, from Kick it Out.

"One of the barriers is kit. We want to remove that barrier so that more local girls can keep playing."

Bright told the BBC she was fully behind the campaign.

"As a girl or a woman, it's about feeling wanted and equal," she said.

"We deserve to have kits. I don't want to see any girl dropping out of the game because they don't feel valued or comfortable.

"These are the days that really make a difference. Hopefully this shows the girls that the people at the top care. The power of having a kit is a small gesture, but has a massive impact."

#265. Lessons from a miserable month for hedge funds

The struggle to invest in anything other than AI

#264. Analysis: The Fed's chairman sees an AI revolution coming. Now he has to figure out what it means

Artificial intelligence is about to sweep through the economy. The Federal Reserve is bracing for impact.

"We are at a hinge point in the U.S. economy," Fed Chairman Kevin Warsh said at a conference he convened in San Francisco on Monday. But exactly when and how the economy could turn, and what that means for interest rates and inflation, isn't yet clear.

The meeting of Warsh's AI task force saw the new Fed chairman publicly asking the questions Americans will ultimately expect him to answer. He hinted at a few of his private views on whether white-collar workers could soon face massive job losses, but he largely left the speculation to an array of experts he has assembled to advise the Fed.

With the technology racing ahead, Warsh won't be able to put off giving his own answers much longer. AI lab Anthropic plans to price the shares for its initial public offering on Wednesday and could raise as much as $64 billion at a $1.4 trillion valuation. Vast spending from AI firms is prompting the Fed to contemplate raising interest rates in December to slow down the rate of price increases as the tech sector heats up the economy.

Meanwhile Americans are souring on some aspects of the technology. The Nov. 3 midterms saw voters enact an array of state-level measures to slow down construction of the data centers that are essential to powering AI.

Warsh moderated three out of four sessions at Monday's event and was involved in selecting the speakers. Among them was one of the few prominent Democrats who was willing to speak out against that anti-data center trend.

It is "a very troubling sign" that so many anti-data center measures passed in the midterms, Gina Raimondo said. She is a former secretary of Commerce under President Joe Biden and a former governor of Rhode Island.

Stopping AI "would be very bad for America" and for the people who voted for the measures, Raimondo said.

Warsh didn't say whether he shares that view, and how it would translate into monetary policy isn't exactly clear.

The event was an unusual one for the Fed. The central bank employs a small army of Ph.D. economists who can rapidly weigh in on anything affecting the U.S. and global economies. But in keeping with his long-standing critiques of the Fed as insular and outdated, Warsh has chosen to draw expertise from outside the institution to help it figure out how to think about what AI will do to productivity and jobs.

Warsh has assembled a task force on that issue, alongside four others devoted to aspects of Fed policymaking.

The public heard Monday from one of the three official members of the task force: venture capitalist Marc Andreessen. Warsh went to Stanford University with Andreessen's wife, and the two have been longtime friends. Another task force member, Stanford economist Chad Jones, is on leave and working at Anthropic and couldn't speak during the company's blackout period ahead of the IPO. The third, Xbox CEO Asha Sharma, didn't attend the event.

Andreessen is hugely optimistic about the promise of AI as a technology, but believes the government won't let Americans experience its full effects. Heavy regulation in areas such as healthcare and education mean technology can't replace people's jobs in those sectors, he said.

"To the extent our economy is a free market economy, then maybe you could see the, let's say, extremely rapid displacement scenarios people are talking about," Andreessen said. "But we've mostly outlawed the kind of job transition that … both the utopians the doomers talk about."

Warsh returned repeatedly to one scenario for the economy that draws on past experience. The so-called China shock unfolded after that country joined the World Trade Organization in 2001 and moved heavily into low-cost manufacturing. Americans saw prices on clothing and furniture drop rapidly, for instance, but at the cost of severe job losses. One widely cited estimate found the U.S. lost a million manufacturing jobs and 2.4 million jobs overall in the years that followed.

Warsh suggested to one of the panelists that the widespread adoption of AI might produce its own version of the China shock, this time focused on service workers.

"I don't want to hold this too tightly," Warsh said. "But if in some ways over the last generation or generation and a half, the China shock, for lack of a better word, lowered prices of goods in the United States, the way that I heard you talking about the philosophy of this new technology is it might do something for services."

In other words, AI could do, say, legal or administrative work more cheaply than a human. That could lower prices but put many people out of a job.

Warsh asked panelists whether "we should expect disinflation over the medium term, from what AI could do to services."

They largely suggested it was possible, which could mean the Fed might eventually want to lower interest rates over the long term.

But at the end of the day, it is Warsh and his fellow Fed members who will need to have conviction on the answer.

There are still many steps between the task force discussion and any kind of change in Fed policy. First, the three task force members will have to assemble their points of view into a report for Warsh sometime in the next few months. Then the Fed itself will need to debate those results and decide whether or how to use them to shape monetary policy.

But with AI growing bigger every day, the Fed may need to make up its mind sooner rather than later.

#261. 'Break-in and rising costs could kill my business'

Gordon Kidd says he is struggling with business-related costs and a recent break-in has not helped

The owner of a cycle shop says he is considering closing the business after a break-in and soaring costs.

Gordon Kidd, who runs Shifnal Cycles, estimates that a burglary last month cost him about Â£20,000 after three customer bikes and a frame were stolen and the subsequent repairs to his shop.

Along with rising bills and a "lack of government support", the former armed forces veteran said the business in Shifnal may not be viable if financial pressures continue.

A government spokesperson said that, from next year, businesses such as cycle shops would see their business rates either frozen, reduced or cut to zero, with half of the high-street retailers paying no rates.

West Mercia Police said it had received a report of a burglary on 31 October, and an investigation was ongoing.

"They stole customers' bikes, which, for me, as a shop owner, is my biggest fear," said Kidd, who was concerned the burglary could affect his reputation.

"That might put them [customers] off from using my shop, and that kills a shop."

He added: "I've got to do what's financially viable for the business.

"I've got to look at another avenue now, and I've got to look at a more cost-effective way of doing it."

Burglars stole three cycles and a frame and damaged the shop in the raid

Alongside coping with the impact of the burglary, Kidd called on the government to do more to help small businesses.

"I don't know how the government expects small businesses to operate and make a living," he said.

"We just break even with the repairs side of things, and it's tough."

He said the break-in "had not helped whatsoever".

"A lot of people probably think that bike shops make a lot of money when we really don't," he said.

Kidd, who not only sells cycles but also repairs and services them, told the BBC that the break-in had made him feel angry that his safe space had been invaded.

He suffers post-traumatic stress disorder and said the business was "like therapy".

"Working on bikes calms me down; it helps me keep in control of my PTSD and my mental health in general."

A government spokesperson said: "Corporation tax remains capped, and there are generous tax reliefs offered for hiring young people.

"We are also making the business environment work better through our Â£4.3bn business rates support package, small business plan and action on late payments.

#259. Thrift shop asks shoppers to nominate a charity

Julie Rindam (front) and Jeanette Worthington (back) plan to be transparent with donors by having a book in the shop which will evidence where money is going.

Shoppers and donors at a new charity shop will be able to help decide which organisations benefit from the money it raises.

Twisted Sisters Thrift Store, in Tunstall opened last week, and is inviting customers to nominate causes they would like to support, with a different charity set to receive donations each month.

Owner Julie Rindam said the idea came after the closure of a charity shop; she had run for eight years.

"The pastor is retiring and it was either another empty shop or I took it on."

Rindam said many of the shop's regular donors wanted to continue supporting charitable causes, leading to the idea of allowing the community to decide where the money goes.

"All my donators still wanted to drop off to us, so we thought, well, how about a different charity every month?" she said.

"We ask all the donators who they'd like us to donate to, so every month it will be a different charity benefiting."

Rather than supporting a single organisation, the shop plans to keep a record of suggestions, being completely transparent with customers and donors before selecting a charity at the end of each month.

"Everyone who drops off, we ask and we keep a book and every month we'll pick a different charity to benefit from, whether that's City Dogs Home, Cancer Research, Diabetes or other charities," Rindam said.

The owner hopes that the store will also be a centre for the community.

Shop manager Jeanette Worthington said the approach was designed to ensure local people remained involved in how the money was distributed.

As well as raising money for charity, the store also aims to act as a community hub, offering free food, sanitary products, contraception and school uniforms when available.

Worthington said supporting local people was just as important as supporting charities.

"People know they can come here, it's a safe space," she said.

"We will help anybody if we have the time and if we can't do it, we'll say come back at such and such a time."

Janet Smart had been a regular donater to the shop before it was taken over by the new team.

Regular customer and donor Janet Smart said the shop had become a valuable part of the community.

"When I'm decluttering everything, it comes to here because I know they don't charge a lot. People haven't got much money today," she said.

"You meet such nice people in here as well."

She added that the social side of the shop was equally important.

"I think for people on their own, it's nice to be able to come in somewhere and people chat with them."

Jack Spencer is managing to find clothes and items that he wouldn't be able to find in more mainstream stores.

Shopper Jack Spencer said the store offered affordable options for people who might otherwise struggle to buy certain items.

"It feels a lot more personal, a lot more out there. You go in, you don't know what you're going to walk into, it changes constantly," he said.

"You might find something that you could have been looking for, but you just couldn't afford it."

For Rindam and Worthington, the hope is that both the charities and the local community will benefit.

#256. JetBlue unveils new first-class seats to bring back profits

JetBlue Airways unveiled its new domestic first-class seats Wednesday as more of the country's carriers bet on the premium travel boom to make them profitable again.

JetBlue announced it would offer a domestic first class — BlueFirst — in December 2024 and plans to start flying aircraft with the new seats and full on-board product next month. The carrier showed off the new cabin at its home hub at John F. Kennedy International Airport on Wednesday. Retrofits will continue during 2027, CEO Joanna Geraghty told CNBC late last month.

JetBlue will have 12 of the seats on its Airbus A320 fleet and 30 extra-legroom seats, down from 42 of those options previously. Because the airplanes don't have ovens, JetBlue said it is offering an array of cold food options like sesame noodles, pastrami sandwiches and tortellini salad, though they vary by route.

The rollout comes as the New York airline tries to stabilize after last posting a profit in 2019. Last month, JetBlue executives sought to calm investors after shares in the airline hit a record low following a report that bondholders had contracted restructuring advisors.

"We have no intention, no plans to file for bankruptcy. We are not engaged with any restructuring company," Geraghty told CNBC in an interview on Oct. 27.

Geraghty told CNBC's Phil LeBeau on Wednesday that JetBlue has "met with a few of the creditors over the last couple of weeks as part of ... normal investor engagement." She said the company made it clear to bondholders that JetBlue doesn't need to reorganize.

The airline's shares are up more than 40% this month, more than other passenger carriers, as of midday Wednesday, though they are still off about 11% so far this year.

JetBlue is betting that resilient travel demand, especially for roomier seats will help fuel its turnaround plan, which aims to get the carrier to an operating profit next year and $1 in adjusted earnings per share in 2028.

Airline executives have said they expect fares to stay elevated next year as they grapple with higher costs, including but not limited to fuel, such as labor and airport fees. Geraghty said Wednesday that even if fuel prices moderate next year, "I don't think fares are going to fall as much as people expect."

Airlines large and small are racing to outfit their cabins with roomier, more expensive seating options to help cover ever-higher costs, from jet fuel to labor to airport operation fees.

For example, a ticket in the new recliner first-class seats between Kennedy airport and Fort Lauderdale-Hollywood International Airport in Florida is going for $847 roundtrip in the first week of March while standard economy is $446.

On Tuesday, budget carrier Frontier Airlines unveiled its new first-class seats, which will take up the first two rows, and said it would outfit its Airbus fleet with premium seating that has extra legroom and in-seat power through row eight. The airline will take out a row of seating on its planes to make the pricier ones fit, Chief Marketing Officer Bobby Schroeter told CNBC in an interview Monday.

JetBlue last month reported that it ended the third quarter with $2.2 billion in liquidity and that its unencumbered asset base is now $7 billion, including $2.8 billion in aircraft and engines.

JetBlue had total debt of $8.4 billion as of Sept. 30, the carrier said in its earnings filing.

Geraghty told CNBC last month there is a "fundamental disconnect between the narrative in the bond market and what our financial results actually demonstrate."

When asked whether JetBlue's eye on keeping costs down includes furloughs, layoffs or buyouts, Geraghty last month said "absolutely not."

#255. Berkshire Hathaway raised its stake in Delta in the third quarter. It also trimmed a key bank holding

Berkshire Hathaway made several moves in the stock market during the third quarter, adding to key positions and trimming others.

The Omaha-based conglomerate, now led by CEO Greg Abel, increased its Delta Air Lines stake by 12%, scooping up 7.13 million shares. The position was worth $5.37 billion at the end of the third quarter.

Abel succeeded legendary investor Warren Buffett, 96, at the start of this year. Buffett continues to control roughly 13% of Berkshire common stock, Reuters said Monday, after the former CEO donated $5.5 billion to nonprofit foundations.

Berkshire boosted its stake in Delta as the Atlanta-based carrier's shares tumbled 11% in the third quarter, hurt by rising jet fuel prices due to the U.S.-Iran war that pressured the entire airline industry. That marked the stock's worst quarterly performance since the first quarter of 2025, when it plunged 28%. Delta last month also cut its full-year earnings forecast, citing a surge in fuel costs.

To be sure, buying that dip has been a winning investment thus far. Delta is up 3.8% so far in the fourth quarter, while the S&P 500 has dropped 3.1%. Shares are also up nearly 15% over the past month.

Berkshire also trimmed its holding in Bank of America by nearly 6%, selling more than $1.6 billion worth of stock.

BofA was under pressure in the third quarter, falling more than 4%, as interest rates surged alongside stubbornly high inflation and expectations of tighter Federal Reserve monetary policy. At one point in September the 30-year Treasury bond yield surged above 5.6% to its highest level since 2002.

Despite the share sale, Bank of America remains a top Berkshire Hathaway holding, with the conglomerate's stake valued at $24.8 billion at the end of the third quarter.

Berkshire also initiated a position in Bending Spoons and nearly doubled its stake in homebuilder Lennar in the latest quarter.

#253. Why are more roofs not made of solar tiles?

From installer to instructor Kate Collardson (left) has a long history in solar systems

Some people in the US "still hate the look of solar", Kate Collardson acknowledges.

She would know. Collardson handles operations for the Solar Service of Colorado, a solar maintenance and inspection company. She's also held just about every other job in the industry, from installer to instructor.

For those that hate the look of solar panels, an alternative is a solar roof, where the tiles (or shingles) house solar cells, but look much the same as regular tiles.

This is especially important for historic buildings, where heritage rules limit changes to their appearance.

For many people, the first time they'd heard of this product category was in August, when Tesla's website stopped offering solar roofs, external. This followed disappointing sales and a lawsuit over sudden price hikes, external.

Other companies have also pulled out, but Tesla's decision in particular sent shockwaves through the solar roof industry.

Some see it as a death knell for solar tiles; others, as an opportunity.

Tesla abruptly stopped marketing its solar tiles in August

For instance, the small Swiss company Freesuns is considering expanding to the US.

Deborah Learoyd, Freesuns' CEO and managing director, estimates that the company's sales have grown 20â30% since it was established in 2019.

In general, solar roof components continue to be manufactured in smaller batches. This limits the economies of scale and standardisation, though flexible manufacturing could help somewhat.

Learoyd says that while the unit costs for Freesuns' products are slowly decreasing, "a lot of the cost is actually the fact that every roof is a little bit different. And so it has to be engineered a little bit differently, installed a little bit differently".

Solar roofs typically require collaboration among roofers, solar installers and electricians. And building regulations tend to be more complex than for solar panels.

The additional time and paperwork can take a toll on a roofing business, Collardson explains. "You try to put a roofer into the equation and they're going, 'This is taking forever. I'm losing money. I could have sold 10 roofing jobs in the time that it takes me to sell this one'."

Collardson has to charge more for repair of solar shingles, compared to a standard photovoltaic (PV) setup.

She explains, "With traditional solar, we generally have electronics on the panel and a map that shows us exactly how much each panel is producing." So when something goes wrong, it's easy to spot where.

"With solar shingles, we don't really have the benefit of that mapping; there's no room underneath the shingle for the electronics." Thermal cameras can identify the trouble spots, however.

But then there's the matter of accessing them. "You can remove the one in the middle of the array, but you have to walk on the others, and you really shouldn't walk on them because you're going to create micro-fractures and that's going to cause issues down the road."

Before repair costs are factored in, a solar roof might cost twice as much as a system of solar panels mounted on a roof.

Freesuns solar tiles on the LycÃ©e Jean-Piaget in NeuchÃ¢tel, Switzerland

Solar roofs are also less efficient - one reason is that the tiles are just smaller.

Another factor is the energy lost as heat. Since the tiles sit close to the roof, there's less airflow than with standard solar panels mounted on a roof. Heat impairs solar performance.

Coloured tiles are even less efficient, comments Ben Graves, co-founder of UK solar company Sunsave. "The reason why all solar cells are black, and increasingly the whole panel is now black, is because black is the best absorbing colour."

Overall, much more space would need to be devoted to solar roof tiles to produce the same amount of energy.

Sunsave does not offer solar roofs. "Most people in the UK just don't have enough money to pay for it," Graves believes.

While appearance was a bigger factor five years ago, now "the vast majority of consumers are most interested in how much they can save".

But supporters of solar roofs argue that they should not be pitted against standard solar modules on roofs.

"Let's not compare PV roof tiles to a standard PV system, but look at PV roof tiles as generating additional electricity from our roofs instead of just having the roof sit there," urges Martin Heinrich who leads a research group at the Fraunhofer Institute for Solar Energy Systems in Germany.

A solar roof can cost twice as much as installing solar panels

While standard solar panels have gone from strength to strength, solar roofs have not advanced to the same extent.

One advancement that could especially help solar roofs is matrix shingling technology.

This is a different way of connecting solar cells. The solar cells are cut into narrow strips, layered like overlapping shingles, and connected to each other using electrically conductive adhesive, rather than metal wires.

This means that the electrical current can flow around shaded areas, unlike with standard modules. "If you get like a bird pooping on a part of the cell, the current can go around it," Learoyd explains.

In addition to increasing energy output, this improves the appearance as the silver wires aren't visible.

Another advantage, Heinrich says, is that "since we cut the cells into smaller areas, we can fill out special sizes more easily. You don't have to adhere to the regular pattern size."

Freesuns is an early adopter of matrix shingling technology in building-integrated PV. "We see it as a big opportunity for the future in terms of how we would make our tiles," Learoyd says.

What would also help the company is better software for automated design. This would speed up the design process, since each roofing job is unique.

Learoyd believes this would lower costs and give "the whole construction industry much more confidence in using the product".

Ten years after her own solar roof was installed, and despite the sector's setbacks, Learoyd remains optimistic.

She believes that for people with the means, it will eventually become standard to install a new roof with some sort of embedded solar. "It's just a no-brainer."

The critical tech staying safe by going underground

Why airlines are warning over lithium-ion batteries

#250. How long will humans be needed for translation?

Google's translation service handles one trillion words per month

"Il pleut des cordes," say the French when it's raining hard.

"It's raining ropes," is the direct translation. It's the kind of idiom which can trip up an AI translator.

"You can imagine the dilemma this throws AI into," says FranÃ§ois Yvon, a research director specialised in automatic translation with the French National Centre for Scientific Research CNRS.

He says a well-trained system will know that ropes are not literally falling from the sky and use the English phrase "it's raining cats and dogs".

"But is it going to find the equivalent when it's translating into Indonesian?" asks Yvon.

A comment on the weather is pretty low-risk, but more and more translation is being done by machines, and on topics with higher stakes than the weather.

In April, Google said people are now translating, external one trillion words a month with Google Translate.

That doesn't count the others: DeepL, Microsoft, Claude, ChatGPT and Gemini, not to mention made-to-measure corporate translation systems.

Direct sales of machine translation, external (i.e. outside general AI subscriptions) were worth an estimated $1.4bn (Â£1bn) last year.

AI translation is the "right call" in many cases says Jonathan Zimmermann

"For plenty of use cases, raw AI translation is now the right call: multiplying your costs by 100 to catch the last few percent of errors isn't rational if you're translating internal documents or product listings," says Jonathan Zimmermann, founder of OKA (Open Knowledge Association), a Swiss non-profit that translates Wikipedia articles into other languages.

"For an encyclopedia it's different. Wikipedia runs on trust. A wrong fact can sit there for years and get cited by journalists and AI systems," he says.

With more than 10,000 published pieces, mostly into Spanish, Portuguese and English, OKA is, Zimmerman says, the single largest contributor of translated articles to the collaborative online encyclopedia.

Which is something it absolutely could not have become without AI.

"Translating 10,000 articles the traditional way would cost millions. In our process, the AI produces the draft, then a human translator checks every sentence," he explains.

But that human input is required less and less.

"The machine output is now good enough that skipping the human entirely has become a serious option, and a real temptation across the industry. Two or three years ago, with tools like DeepL, our translators still rewrote a good chunk of every draft.

"Today the prose often doesn't need fixing at all. So, the question is no longer whether AI translation is good, it clearly is, but whether it's good enough to remove the human," says Zimmermann.

Ariella BaltiÃ© is a translator and legal expert specialised in translating court documents from English into French.

She was contacted recently by someone who'd already translated his documents with AI, asking her to check them.

"There were two major errors right at the beginning," she said.

The AI had translated "Le demandeur" (the plaintiff) as "the requester". "Totally awkward for legal papers," says BaltiÃ©.

When the AI saw "Madame X, Ã©pouse Y" (Ms X, wife of Y'), it came up with "Ms X marries Y".

In other words, it mistook a noun for a verb, completely losing the meaning of the phrase.

The consequences of mistranslation in the legal sphere can be serious. In the US, for example, errors in machine-translated asylum applications have led to delays in the release of detainees.

Ariella BaltiÃ© specialises in legal translation work

Accuracy is a matter of life and death in the medical field.

"The units for dosages and blood tests often include Greek letters," says Carol Bereuter, who is a specialist in medical translations.

She came across one automatically-translated text that had translated the greek letter Î¼ as milli (one thousandth) instead of micro (one millionth).

"That's a serious error. If you give someone a milligram when it's meant to be a microgram, you are giving them a thousand times the dose," points out Bereuter.

She also makes the point that, as AI translators have got better, catching errors has got more difficult.

"Whereas in the past, with things like Google Translate, the sentence would jump out at you because there'd be a grammatical error or it would sound clunky, today it sounds perfectly legitimate. It sounds fluid. It sounds natural. So the errors are a lot harder to spot."

Carol Bereuter is a specialist in medical translations

"If it's something that will bear your signature, there's a risk for your reputation. And if decisions are going to be taken on the basis of a translation concerning a medical procedure, a contract you might sign or a major purchase you might make, the risk is even greater," warns FranÃ§ois Yvon.

Even sentences that seem quite clear to a human can trip up an AI service, he says.

"When you have words with different meanings, it's usually very clear for a human which one applies because they understand the context, but when a machine translator comes across the sentence 'translate into Polish', for example, it is quite capable of deciding it must be all about shoe cream, says Yvon.

Context is often crucial. If an AI translator hasn't grasped that we're talking about a football match it is liable to mis-translate "taking a corner".

Last year OKA ran a study, funded by Wikimedia. It studied 119 articles, 10 language pairs and more than 1,000 hours of translator work.

"27% of the AI-generated text still got corrected before publication. Three quarters of those corrections were errors the AI introduced, and 5 to 6% of errors changed the meaning of the original text," says Zimmerman.

Eradicating those errors is likely to be challenging.

"As things stand, I think the fundamental way these systems function makes it quite hard to imagine how they could become 100% reliable," says FranÃ§ois Yvon.

This is because probability is a crucial part of the way AI translates each word rather than another and what is probably correct is not the same as what is correct. Especially in the realm of something as subtle and surprising as language.

He thinks a new generation of tech will be an improvement.

"We will see translation systems that are increasingly capable of evaluating what they do and correcting themselves using different AI tools simultaneously: one to do the translation, a second to criticize it and a third to produce a new version, for example."

But perhaps the big moment will be when AI can show doubt.

"The real breakthrough won't come until these systems are not merely capable of translating but also of telling us when they're not sure," Yvon says.

The critical tech staying safe by going underground

#249. JD Vance says he'll decide 'sometime next year' about 2028 presidential run

U.S. Vice President JD Vance said Monday he will make a decision next year about whether to run for president in 2028.

"We'll figure it out sometime next year," he told Fox News host Laura Ingraham in an interview that aired Monday evening.

"I'm not an expert in this but my understanding with a presidential campaign is there's a lot of infrastructure that you have to set up, fundraising, all that stuff and so you can't declare a month before the Iowa caucus that you're going to run for president," he said. "So, you know, I haven't made a decision."

Vance has yet to openly declare any ambition to succeed President Donald Trump when his current term ends, but speculation that the vice president has his sights set on the White House has been mounting for some time. In the wake of the GOP's crushing defeat in the U.S. midterm election on Nov. 3, anonymous sources involved in campaigns told Reuters Vance was among the high-profile Republicans who had begun early preparations for a 2028 run.

Candidates typically announce their intentions to run in the election in the spring of 2027, with parties holding nominating conventions to formally select their presidential candidates between July and September.

Polymarket currently slates Vance as the frontrunner to win the Republican presidential nomination for the 2028 election, with the prediction market giving him a 37% chance of becoming the nominee. He added that lawmakers needed to double down on "economic nationalism" and bring "prosperity to the homeland" to deal with "economic pain" in the United States.

#246. As Christmas rush looms, how do China-made toys find their way to the US?

China is the world's largest toymaker, producing the majority of toys on sale in the US

On the underside of most children's toys on sale in the US you'll find the same three words â "Made in China".

Nearly eight in 10 toys (78.3%) imported into the country in 2024 came from China, according to US government figures. , external

Last year, toys imported from China were worth $9.8bn (Â£7.4bn), trade figures show. , external

With Christmas on the horizon, the toy industry in the US and elsewhere is about to have its busiest time of the year by far.

As retailers prepare their stocks for the festive rush, be they online giants or "mom-and-pop" physical stores, the BBC World Service's Follow the Money series traces how a toy gets from a factory in China to the US. And where the money goes.

Guangdong province, which neighbours Hong Kong in southern China, is the heart of the country's toy manufacturing industry.

In the city of Dongguan workers on the floor of the Mihox factory pop plastic toys out of steel moulds, and pile them into crates ready for painting.

The company says it employs more than 800 people and makes around 55 million toys a year for the American market, which represents half of its business.

Follow the money: The journey of a $30 toy from China to America

Every toy starts with a design from a customer, such as an American toy company. The factory then makes a prototype, and builds moulds from which plastic parts and other components are formed.

Going from the first idea to finished toy takes one to two years, says Melanie Wang from the Mihox sales team. Much of that is the customer going back to the drawing board. "They modified the design again, again, and again to improve it."

Once the design is signed off, things move more quickly. A mould takes about a month to make, and the production run lasts about three months.

The Mihox factory in Dongguan employs more than 800 people

So, how much of the US retail price does the factory get? "About 20%," says Wang.

On a $30 toy, that's $6, which has to cover the price of the plastic and other materials, production costs including wages, and what profit the factory makes.

Chinese factories typically make a profit of between 3% and 10%, according to Jay Foreman, chief executive of US toy firm Basic Fun. So for a $30 toy that's between 60 and 18 cents of the $6 the factory charges to make it.

Samson Ko is president of the Toys Manufacturers' Association of Hong Kong. Many of his members moved their factories across the border into mainland China in the 1980s, attracted by cheaper land and labour.

He says the way toys are made has barely changed since then. "Automation now takes place mostly in the moulding and the packaging process. But for soft toys, or even some of the electronics nowadays, it's still very labour intensive."

And Chinese labour isn't cheap anymore, he adds, after recent rises.

Melanie Wang explains that Mihox gets 20% of a toy's retail price

Average pay in Vietnam is about half of that in China, said one report, external at the start of this year.

Yet fewer than 10% of Ko's members have moved any production outside China, and in those cases mostly only because customers asked them to do so.

The Chinese factories remain busy, he says, because a single toy can undergo more than one manufacturing process, from moulding the parts, to adding electronics, sewing materials, and final assembly. This often requires it travelling to different factories.

Ko adds that in Guangdong these separate facilities are located close to each other, and used to "working in harmony". He says this makes the whole manufacturing process run more smoothly than in other countries.

From the factory, the toy goes by truck to a port, such as Shenzhen, the biggest in Guangdong.

Ivy Tse is co-founder of FreightAmigo, a Hong Kong logistics company that runs an online platform for both Hong Kong and mainland China exporters. These businesses can book a cargo container, sea passage to an overseas destination, arrange insurance cover, and secure finance.

Tse says it takes about 15 days for a cargo ship to get from Shenzhen to the US west coast, and 25 to 30 days to the east coast. This year's Christmas toys left China in August and September.

Freight, from factory to the US, usually adds about 10% to the manufacturing cost of a toy, adds Tse. So if we stick to a toy that retails for $30 in the US, and therefore costs $6 to make in China, the cost of getting it to the states is 60 cents.

Once the toy reaches the US there is currently a tariff on Chinese toys that has to be paid to the US Customs and Border Protection (CBP).

Following talks between President Trump and Chinese leader Xi Jinping last month, the two governments have recommended toys for lower tariffs. But no new rate or start date has been set, and the US must first hold a public consultation. For now the basic rate is 12.5%, and some toys pay more.

This is charged on the manufacturing price of the toy, not on how much it retails for. So for a $30 toy, that basic rate is 12.5% of $6, which is 75 cents.

This tariff is paid for by the American company that has imported the toys. One such firm is Learning Resources, a family business based near Chicago that sells educational toys. Half of these it imports from China.

"We pay 100% of the tariffs, let there be no doubt about that," says the company's chief executive Rick Woldenberg.

Jay Foreman says that most of the money spent on a toy in the US doesn't leave the country

The cost of making the $30 toy, getting it to the US, and paying the import tariff, comes to $7.35. So that leaves $22.65, or 75.5% of the total.

That money never leaves the US, explains Foreman from Basic Fun. His firm, which is based in Florida, makes Tonka trucks and Care Bears cuddly toys.

"It's kept by my company, the [US] transportation company, the retailer," he says.

If we allow $2.65 for the US delivery firm, the toy company and the retailer will typically share the remaining revenues 50-50. So that would give them $10 each.

While profit margins are commercially sensitive and therefore not generally revealed, industry studies show that the toy firm and retailer would usually make a profit of up to 25% or $2.50.

Getting a toy onto the shelves in the US requires both a lot of work and a long journey.

#245. Iran escalates threats as third U.S. carrier arrives in Middle East, tanker struck in Hormuz

Iran has ratcheted up threats against U.S. forces and commercial shipping as a third American aircraft carrier arrived in the Gulf, stoking fears of renewed escalation in the nine-month conflict.

Iran's Revolutionary Guards Navy warned shipping companies against using the U.S.-backed southern route through the Strait of Hormuz, describing it as fully closed and a "minefield of fire," according to regional media outlets citing a statement from the force.

Brig. Gen. Mohammad Akrami-Nia, a spokesperson for the Iranian Army, said the Strait of Hormuz would not reopen until "the enemy accepts Iran's conditions."

Separately, CNN reported Monday that new U.S. intelligence indicated Iran was developing plans that could target senior Trump administration officials with a so-called dirty bomb, or a chemical weapon in Washington, D.C. CNBC cannot immediately verify the report.

The intelligence was characterized as an effort by Iran to develop options rather than evidence of an imminent attack, CNN reported. U.S. officials have held several meetings over the past week to consider how to respond to the potential threat.

The heightened security concerns come after Iranian threats prompted Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent to relocate to military housing last week.

"We remain vigilant, monitoring any and all potential threats, but at this time there are no credible threats to the homeland," a White House spokesperson said in a statement to CNBC.

Oil prices inched lower in Asian trading Tuesday, with international benchmark Brent crude futures dropping 0.7% to $91.8 a barrel and U.S. West Texas Intermediate futures falling 0.7% to $85.1 a barrel.

On Tuesday, the U.K. Maritime Trade Operations said a tanker was struck by an unidentified projectile in the Strait of Hormuz and subsequently caught fire. The agency did not say who was responsible and urged vessels to transit with caution and report suspicious activity.

Separately, the Iranian navy said four oil tankers had struck mines and caught fire in the eastern Strait of Hormuz over the previous 24 hours.

The escalation in Tehran's rhetoric came as the USS Theodore Roosevelt arrived in the Gulf region on Sunday, joining the USS George H.W. Bush and USS George Washington. That added to U.S. firepower near Iran even as President Donald Trump said the war was winding down.

Trump has sent mixed signals regarding the conflict in recent weeks, alternating between declaring it could soon end and warning of renewed large-scale strikes if efforts to reach a deal fail.

After meeting Qatari Prime Minister Sheikh Mohammed bin Abdulrahman Al Thani last week, Trump said the war with Iran could end "fairly soon," adding that it "militarily is effectively over."

Tehran said Sunday that it launched six drones at a U.S. air-defense radar at Bahrain's Riffa air base, the second attack Iran claimed against the kingdom in three days. Bahrain's military said it intercepted and destroyed several Iranian aerial attacks.

Secretary of State Marco Rubio condemned the attacks in a call Monday with Bahraini Foreign Minister Abdullatif bin Rashid Al Zayani and reaffirmed Washington's commitment to the security of Bahrain and other Gulf partners.

The conflict began in late February when the U.S. and Israel attacked Iran, prompting Tehran to retaliate with strikes on Israel and Gulf states hosting American military bases. Indirect talks between Washington and Tehran through Qatar have since appeared to run into a stalemate as warring powers disagreed over terms to reach a lasting peace deal.

Washington appears unwilling to accept several of Iran's demands to end the war, including lifting the U.S. naval blockade on its southern ports and lifting sanctions, while stressing that Iran cannot possess a nuclear weapon.

Iran is likely to continue escalating attacks on tankers and container ships, along with occasional strikes on U.S. military targets, in an effort to pressure Washington into offering concessions, said Ian Bremmer, president and founder of Eurasia Group. In response, Washington will likely expand its blockade on Iran's maritime and inland trade routes, he said.

"The presumption is that eventually Iran can't handle the economic pressure and will accept better terms," Bremmer said, calling that view a "misread of the Iranian leadership and their capacity to resist."

Over the coming year, Iran is likely to intensify efforts to disrupt shipping through the Strait of Hormuz, Bremmer said.

#244. Living with a stink? Technology might help

The operators of Newby Island landfill have been ordered to take action over odours

Milpitas, an affluent city in California's Silicon Valley tech hub, has an odour problem.

For decades, residents have had to endure rank smells wafting from industrial sites, among them a 342-acre landfill.

"When I first moved here, I could not open my window for over 10 years," says Chia-Ling Kong, an engineer for a tech firm who lives around half a mile from the dump.

While things have got better in recent years, Kong says she and her neighbours must still contend with around "two or three hours of bad smells a day".

Perhaps befitting Silicon Valley, the community is putting its hopes in technology.

The operators of Newby Island landfill have been ordered by local authorities to use drones and install monitors to systematically track and measure the chemicals responsible for bad odours. They have also been told to automate the collection of landfill gases responsible for smells.

Kong is optimistic a data-driven approach will make a difference. "Hopefully with this continuous monitoring, it makes it easier to pinpoint problems at the moment it starts."

The operators of the landfill say they are committed to operating it safely and protecting air quality.

Just like in Milpitas, where Kong is part of a citizen's campaign against the odours, communities around the world are raising a stink about bad smells making day-to-day life miserable. Sometimes a rubbish dump is to blame, or it might be a sewage treatment works, or a reeking factory.

When the complaints start stacking up, resolving them is not always simple. Many countries have no regulations in place dictating how to manage smells.

In the US, for example, the Environmental Protection Agency does not regulate environmental odours as a pollutant. This leaves states and local governments to take charge of the issue.

Urban sprawl is often part of the problem, bringing communities closer to stinky facilities that may historically have been kept at a distance.

This is creating more demand for technologies to monitor and prevent smells. Odour consultant Micheal Bull says people are increasingly concerned about neighbourhood stinks.

"You're getting developments being built nearer to odorous processesâ¦ as land has become more valuable, there is also infilling," Bull says. He is speaking about his experiences in the UK, but it's a similar story in many parts of the world.

Bad smells from waste sites are among the most difficult to resolve

The technologies used to tackle industrial pongs generally fall into three categories: they absorb odours; convert them into something less smelly through a chemical reaction; or burn them away.

Experts on fixing industrial odours say that the best technologies, when used correctly, can remove 90% or more of the target compounds responsible for smells.

But this can be expensive and some operators opt for simpler and cheaper solutions. These include masking odours from landfill or sewage processing by spraying giant quantities of deodorisers.

Odour experts contacted for this article were dubious about the effectiveness of such industrial scale air fresheners. "In my view they're not likely to change the odours," says Bull, who likens them to fabric conditioner.

Bad smells from waste sites â a common cause of complaints â are among the stinkiest and most difficult to resolve, according to Carlos Diaz, an environmental consultant based in Spain. "Landfill is difficult to treat because you have a very large surface," he says.

In some places at least, technology is making a real difference. Residents in Des Moines, Iowa, have long complained of the so-called Eastside Funk, a putrid scent that residents report drifts over parts of the city.

A study commissioned by city authorities showed that a trio of industrial meat companies were among culprits.

"Within 10 miles, there's about half a million people," says Dalton Jacobus, who works in the Department of Neighborhood Services. "If the winds are out of the wrong direction, 100,000 people can be in the middle of that odour plume."

The authorities are now paying around $240,000 (Â£182,000) a year on technology to track the stink, so they can take action. "What we wanted to do was build a ring of odour monitors around the facilities that we suspected of emitting odours," says Jacobus.

The monitors, which measure levels of chemical compounds in the air, work in combination with a weather station to provide real-time data on the concentrations that are coming from each facility based off the wind patterns.

This has put an end to finger pointing about who's to blame each time a smell sparks complaints, says Jacobus. "Now we can say with reasonable certainty that 'this odour event did originate from your facility, what was going on'?"

In Des Moines, authorities have set up "electronic noses" to pinpoint bad smells

Meanwhile, some of the sites that have been blamed for causing unpleasant smells in Des Moines have spent millions of dollars to upgrade their own odour control systems.

Smithfield Foods, which operates a meat processing in Des Moines, says it has invested $2m to install a regenerative thermal oxidiser that destroys the chemicals associated with bad smells. Even so, the company says it does not believe it is the source of odour complaints.

Before installing the monitors â which some local companies help pay for â officials in Des Moines would drive out to the source of a complaint to sniff the air.

Relying on the human nose to sniff out industrial smells is still commonplace. Claudia Barrios ObregÃ³n from Kunak, the company behind the monitors in Des Moines, says: "Right now, the way that cities control or measure odours is olfactometry: a human panel measures and gives an opinion of what the odour is like. But that's not that realistic."

The Spanish company's monitors are an example of so-called electronic noses that make it easier to detect smells.

Whatever technology is used, it can be hard to keep everyone happy, as smells are so subjective. "Unfortunately, the general rule is that the nastier we perceive the odour to be, the more sensitive towards it we are," says Johan LundstrÃ¶m, a professor in the science of smell at the Karolinska Institute in Stockholm, Sweden.

#241. The tech helping crack decades-old cold cases

Crime scene evidence is often not enough to identify suspects

Randy Gail Sperino was last seen alive on the evening of 9 November 1993, as she was getting into a pickup truck in Granite City, Illinois.

The 34-year-old's body was found hours later in a field nearby, the victim of blunt force trauma.

The identity of her killer was a mystery. DNA evidence found at the crime scene was tested against standard law enforcement databases, but no matches were found.

For three decades, investigators from the Madison County Sheriff's Office chased leads and interviewed hundreds of people as they attempted to identify the perpetrator.

The case looked like it would remain one of more than 250,000 unsolved homicides in the US.

Then, last year, detectives tried a fresh approach. They sent genetic evidence to Othram, a Houston-based company that uses DNA technology to help identify the perpetrators in cold and current crime cases.

The Madison County detectives had been inspired by investigative genetic genealogy techniques used to identify the Golden State Killer, Joseph James DeAngelo, back in 2018. In that case, an investigative team constructed a family tree to help identify the killer.

They were able to do so thanks to DNA data that relatives had provided to commercial databases, similar to consumer ancestry platforms.

Unlike traditional forensic DNA testing, which generally relies on a direct comparison between evidence and a known person, investigative genetic genealogy can identify distant genetic relatives of an unknown individual.

Othram set about applying this technique to track down Sperino's killer. While the suspect's own DNA profile wasn't available, profiles submitted by his extended relatives allowed the company to identify a male family member.

"That lead broke the case open," said Captain Tim Lawrence of Madison County Sheriff's Office, which was responsible for investigating the crime.

It was a crucial clue that led the detectives to Albert "Buddy" Zigler, 70, who police say then confessed to the murder. He was charged in May this year.

Wes Sperino, Gail's son, said at a press conference: "I finally get closure and I finally know who did this."

Albert Zigler was charged with murder in May

The technique that led detectives to Zigler is credited with revolutionising how forensic cases are solved.

It has been highly successful in helping solve cases involving unidentified human remains and identifying suspects in longstanding investigations.

Othram claims it has helped solve thousands of cases. The company's chief executive David Mittelman likens investigative genetic genealogy to a GPS system: "The DNA profile is the GPS signal and it says 'you belong here.' But to understand where 'here' is, you need a map."

Public records, obituaries, and census data build that map so Othram's scientists can establish how different family trees connect.

However, Mittelman is quick to point out that DNA technology can only go so far before "a cascade of investigative steps" are needed.

An Othram scientist prepares a bone for DNA extraction

DNA science isn't the only technology that is helping detectives solve cold cases.

One of the challenges facing detectives on major investigations like the hunt for Sperino's attacker is the sheer amount of evidence they dig up.

Officers investigating her murder realised AI could help them wade through the mountains of evidence they had accumulated since 1993.

They used a platform called Centerline AI, from Florida software company CentralSquare, to analyse more than 2,000 pages of records and identify evidence for the DNA testing that helped to break the case.

The AI helped them reconstruct timelines and organise witness information. "It's so much easier than having a detective have to read through 2,000 pages," Lawrence says. His team could interact with the AI like a chat bot.

This also helped when it came to interviewing Zigler after they had tracked him down. Detectives were able to ask questions of the AI to help "figure out if Zigler was being truthful", says Lawrence.

But the technology is "only as good as the investigator," says the captain. There comes a point, he says, "where you leave the technology behind and you have to go track people down and talk to them."

Investigative genetic genealogy has its limitations too. Mittelman says: "It can't solve every case. If you have no DNA, you can't really use this technique."

"But this technique is a lot more powerful and a lot more likely to yield identities than having to rely on the chance that you find a match somewhere in a database," he adds.

While firms like Othram only use ancestry platforms where users have provided consent for law enforcement matching, there remain some concerns about the ethical implications of using this data.

A lack of funding is another hurdle. Mittelman says: "Every law enforcement agency wants to do it. There's just not a lot of funding for it."

Sometimes cases are funded through donations or crowdfunding. Othram operates a website listing cases that members of the public can help fund.

While investigative genetic genealogy has been adopted in countries such as Sweden, Denmark and Australia, it is not yet used in the UK. The Home Office is conducting a study to consider whether it should be adopted in the UK but did not respond to a request for comment.

The UK is already in a strong position, says Syndercombe Court. "We have such an efficient DNA database that if you can get any DNA out of a crime scene, you've got over a 65% chance of putting a name to that person, which is enormously powerful," she says.

X-planes: Are they needed in the new era of drones?

#240. Nvidia has lost $2 trillion in value. Here's what options traders expect from earnings

Jensen Huang's forced exit from Nvidia shocked Wall Street and Washington, wiped $2 trillion off its market value, and raised serious questions about national security and regulation of the AI complex. Traders, for the moment, must content themselves with the comparatively mundane business of Tuesday's earnings, and options traders are expecting big moves.

With volatility in the stock near the highest since April 2025, options traders are now pricing a 7% reaction in the stock after its report, the biggest implied move going into an Nvidia report since February 2025, and well above the median move of 3.2% over the last eight quarters.

Implied volatility reached 68 Monday, compared to under 40 in early October, according to ThinkOrSwim data. Nvidia vol reached a multi-year high above 92 in the aftermath of President Trump's tariff Liberation day in April '25. In other words, the company that for much of the AI boom was the most reliable winner in the market, is now more volatile than all but 35 companies in the S&P 500.

"Longer term, I see significant downside risk unless Nvidia can attract an exceptional leader, someone like Tim Cook or AMD's Lisa Su," Nigam Arora, founder of the Arora report, said in a text ahead of the company's after-market report on Tuesday.

Since hitting all-time highs in October, Nvidia shares have fallen 35%, shedding $2 trillion in market capitalization along the way. Including Nvidia, there are only five companies in the S&P 500 worth more than $2 trillion. However, there is evidence that bulls have been holding out hope while bears snatch up profits: the ratio of open puts to call contracts has actually widened towards calls, according to Barchart data. The ratio is currently 0.87, down from a one-year high of 1.02 two weeks ago and the lowest since the first week of October.

The most popular contract by volume on Monday was the 155-strike put expiring at the bell Monday, but the most popular trade for later expiry was the 160-strike call expiring Thursday, and the second-most-common purchase was the 190-strike call expiring Friday, SpotGamma data show.

One catch is that most call contracts traded Monday looked like neutral to bearish positions opened by traders selling calls. Of the 5 million options traded on Monday, 3 million were calls, but more were likely opened by sellers than buyers. Of the $2.3 billion in options premium traded on Nvidia Monday, $1.2 billion was tied to puts, SpotGamma data show.

With bears taking profits in puts the past month, there's another potential snag on open interest. There are very few open put contracts at or below the $157 level at which the stock closed Monday. That suggests any bad surprise would find many traders with little downside protection.

By the same token, there's notable open interest in call contracts from the 175-strike and above, suggesting profit-taking on a bounce could limit Nvidia's recovery past levels it breached over the past week.

Either way, Tuesday's earnings could provide fireworks.

#239. American Airlines brings fresh food back to NY-Chicago first class for first time since 2019

American Airlines said Wednesday it's bringing small, fresh "bites" to first class on flights between its hubs in New York and Chicago, the carrier's latest effort to win over higher-spending customers.

The changes start this month. Customers will be able to pick between two options, which include prosciutto and melon or apple cinnamon chia pudding for breakfast and a Thai noodle salad or mezze plate for lunch and dinner, American said Wednesday.

American last offered fresh food for first class on these routes in 2019, a spokeswoman said. Currently, customers in first class on these flights get a smaller, packaged snack. Airlines largely have limited food options for shorter flights, even in first class.

American has fallen behind Delta Air Lines and United Airlines in profits and investments in premium options. The airline has been working to step up those premium offerings in recent years with new cabins, lounges and plans for seatback screens on its narrow-body fleets.
