#212. Ukraine damages Putin’s prized Crimea bridge as Zelenskyy lauds ‘unprecedented operation’

Ukraine has damaged the Kerch Bridge linking Russia to the occupied Crimean Peninsula, delivering a symbolic blow to President Vladimir Putin as Kyiv reels from strikes on its own infrastructure.

Sergey Aksyonov, the Russia-installed governor of Crimea, confirmed that the strategically important bridge, which had been briefly closed on Monday, was attacked overnight for the second time in two days.

Videos and photos circulating on social media appeared to show the partial destruction of the bridge, with one section having collapsed into the water. CNBC was unable to independently verify the footage.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy described the attack as an "unprecedented operation" several weeks in the making, with Ukraine's defense forces, security services and weapons manufacturers involved.

"Russia strikes our infrastructure every day, including bridges in Zaporizhzhia, Dnipro, Kyiv, and the Odesa region," Zelenskyy said Tuesday on X.

"We are restoring our infrastructure. But we are also reminding Russia that war will always return to where it began. Ukraine's defensive capabilities are strong and will remain so, and we will never leave terror against our cities and people unanswered," he added.

Train and vehicle traffic was suspended due to the attack. No injuries have been reported.

Ukraine has repeatedly targeted the bridge since Russia launched its full-scale invasion in early 2022.

A 12-mile crossing of road and railways, the Kerch Bridge is one of Putin's signature projects. The crossing, sometimes referred to as the Crimean bridge, was built on his orders shortly after the Kremlin forcibly annexed Crimea in 2014 to support Moscow's claims to the territory.

Rail traffic on the bridge was opened on one track, Russia's Ministry of Transport said in a statement via Telegram on Tuesday, while restoration work continues on the second section.

The co-owner and chief engineer of Ukrainian missile and drone maker Fire Point, Denys Shtilierman, appeared to confirm the company's role in the attack, following reports that a Ukrainian Flamingo cruise missile was used in the strike.

"We dedicate this to the memory of our friends and colleagues Andriy and Yuriy, without whom this would not have been possible. R.I.P.," Shtilierman said via X on Tuesday. A spokesperson at Fire Point separately confirmed the company's role in the attack.

Analysts at the Institute for the Study of War, a Washington-based think tank, said Ukraine's attacks on the Kerch Bridge were likely part of Kyiv's ongoing and systematic strike campaign aimed at denying Russian forces the use of major ground lines of communication via Crimea.

Russian forces, meanwhile, struck two bridges crossing the Dnipro River in Kyiv.

Vitaliy Klitschko, the mayor of Ukraine's capital, said via Telegram on Tuesday that specialists were working to assess the extent of the damage to the North and South bridges.

Klitschko said Russian attacks on Kyiv overnight had also caused disruptions to water and heating supplies.

The strikes come ahead of Putin's trip to China this week for the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation forum in Shenzhen, where a trilateral meeting with U.S. President Donald Trump and Chinese President Xi Jinping is planned on Thursday.

Asked whether Ukraine's latest attacks might be connected to the upcoming trilateral summit, Kremlin spokesperson Dmitry Peskov said this could not be ruled out.

"Combat operations continue, and our military is striking targets that are somehow connected to Ukraine's military-industrial complex. And Ukraine is continuing its terrorist attacks," Peskov said, according to Russian state news outlet Ria Novosti.

#211. The rise and fall of Jensen Huang - how the tech superstar became mired in scandal

BBC's Samira Hussain reports from Wall Street on what the fall of the tech superstar means.

Jensen Huang, known for sporting a black leather jacket to shareholder events, appeared to have it all. He was courted by leaders in the US and China alike, worshipped by investors and seen as a loyal family man. But a rockstar approach to his personal life has ultimately led to his resignation.

The 63-year-old has been married since 1985, long before the foundation of Nvidia, the company that propelled him to tech stardom.

He is known as a workaholic and a demanding manager, with high standards for those who worked for him, which included his own children, promoted within the company. But he has seen his personal reputation go into freefall this week, undone by romantic entanglements that throw doubt on his focus and his judgement.

On Thursday, he resigned following back-to-back revelations of two affairs. News of the first - a relationship with a businesswoman alleged to be a Chinese spy - ricocheted around the world, raising eyebrows among investors. It was followed on Wednesday by reports that the tech boss had also had an affair with an Nvidia colleague.

His departure creates uncertainty in the global stock market, which was already knocked-off balance in recent weeks. Nvidia is a linchpin in the US's race for technology leadership with China.

Huang may not be a household name on the same level as Elon Musk or Mark Zuckerberg.

But his role in the tech industry, especially in relation to the explosion of artificial intelligence in the last three years, may prove more consequential. Nvidia dominates the market for the microchips that are used to power the new technology.

The company, which is still valued at nearly $4n (Â£3.02tn) despite recent events, is at the centre of the AI race between the US and China, the world's tech and economic powerhouses.

Huang enjoyed access to the seats of political power on an unrivalled scale, being courted by both US President Donald Trump and China's President Xi Jinping.

His remarkable rise to the top of his profession has led to him being dubbed the "Taylor Swift of tech" and seen him amass an estimated fortune of $145bn, according to the Bloomberg Billionaires Index.

Despite always donning his trademark all-black outfits and taking a playful approach to investor presentations, Huang has worked for years to present himself as a family man, someone who exemplified the American Dream.

Born in Taiwan in 1963, Huang and his family moved to the west coast of the US when he was nine years old. He graduated as an electrical engineer from Oregon State University, and then with a Master's degree in the same field from Stanford University.

It was at college where he met his wife, Lori Mills. The pair were lab partners and married in 1985, eight years before Huang founded Nvidia.

They have two children, Madison and Spencer, who now both work in senior roles at Nvidia.

The company became a key provider of the computer chips and circuits used for gaming before it expanded in a new direction. It now creates the key processing chips used to power the growing AI technology.

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resigns after tech giant rocked by second affair allegation

Who could replace Jensen Huang as Nvidia's chief executive?

Huang has hailed AI as a "new industrial revolution" that is on par with the advent of electricity. Nvidia is the world's second most valuable company and its customers include the biggest firms on the planet, Google, Meta, Amazon, Microsoft, SpaceX, Tesla, OpenAI and Anthropic, all spending big on its products.

So it is no surprise that at a recent AI summit at the White House, Huang was picked out from a group of 42 to flank the president, alongside Elon Musk, both men with whom Trump has famously close relationships.

"Jensen Huang is a tech visionary of the likes of Steve Jobs," Stephen Witt, the author of Thinking Machine, a biography on Jensen Huang, told the BBC, referring to the late boss of Apple. He added that Huang was responsible for creating AI hardware as we know it today.

He described him as a "demanding" boss, who "really pushes his people to the limit".

But while the former Nvidia CEO is "very brilliant" and a "world-class computer scientist", there's also "a level of arrogance" and "a level of self-deception as well", he said.

Huang had gone to great lengths to present himself as a family man, he added, something that was "very important to him".

"He praises his wife, he talks about her in very glowing ways," Witt added. "The fallout of this would be bad for anyone's family. But for someone who has gone to such great lengths to cultivate an image of being a family guy and who keeps his children very close, it is very bad."

First reports emerged on 29 October in the New York Times that Huang had conducted a relationship with Alice Huang, no relation. US officials allege the businesswoman associated with Singapore-based firm Megaspeed is a Chinese intelligence asset.

After the revelations of the first affair, Huang admitted to having been "unfaithful" to his wife and apologised for hurting their family.

Trump, as well as other leaders in the AI tech industry, initially backed Huang to continue in his role at Nvidia. The president described him as "honourable".

But the reporting of a second affair, with Kimberly Powell, vice president of healthcare who has worked at Nvidia for 18 years, prompted a board meeting, followed by the announcement Huang was stepping down.

In the aftermath of that news Trump said Huang had done "a fantastic job" and proposed Tim Cook of Apple as a suitable replacement at the head of Nvidia.

#209. How a sex scandal at Nvidia is putting pressure on the White House

How could the Nvidia CEOâs affair threaten US national security?

How close is Donald Trump to Jensen Huang?

The Nvidia boss was one of the few business leaders with direct access to the US president. He had met Trump repeatedly, travelled with him to Britain and, in May, joined him on Air Force One for his trip to China.

Jensen Huang wasn't just another tech billionaire seeking the president's ear. He had become an important voice in one of the biggest arguments inside the administration: how much of America's most advanced technology should be allowed to reach China.

Nvidia makes the chips that power the AI revolution. Washington has restricted exports of its most advanced processors to China since 2022, believing they could help Beijing develop its military and technological capabilities.

But enforcing those restrictions is difficult. Chips can be bought legally by customers in countries such as Singapore and Malaysia and then diverted to China. Chinese companies can also rent access to computing power through data centres outside the country.

Jensen Huang has long argued that the restrictions are counterproductive. They shut Nvidia out of a huge market and encourage Chinese companies to develop their own alternatives. Trump appeared sympathetic to this view, allowing Nvidia to resume sales of certain chips to China last year in return for a share of the revenue, although its most advanced Blackwell chips remain off limits.

But that relationship now looks rather more complicated.

The allegations surrounding Jensen Huang's reported affair with Alice Huang, of no relation but a former executive at Singapore-based company Megaspeed, have raised the possibility that a foreign intelligence operation was targeting not just a powerful businessman, but someone with unusually close access to the American president.

US intelligence reportedly suspects Alice Huang of having links to Chinese intelligence. Beijing has rejected the allegations. Nvidia's board says it has no reason to believe company secrets or national security were compromised. But the affair has opened up a much bigger question: what did the administration in Washington know, and what did it do about it?

That question leads directly to Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick.

His department was responsible for controlling exports of sensitive American technology. It was also being pressed to act on concerns about Megaspeed, an Nvidia customer suspected of helping divert advanced chips to China.

On Thursday, Megaspeed chief executive, Tan Yong Pong, also known as James Tan, said the company "takes its compliance obligations under US export control laws extremely seriously" and is "committed to working constructively and transparently with the US government". He said the company had co-operated with US Commerce Department inspections.

A highly classified memo was shared with Lutnick about the concerns surrounding Alice Huang and Megaspeed. He resisted taking action against the company, a decision that is now under scrutiny.

Other senior officials wanted a tougher response.

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent was among those who reportedly questioned why Megaspeed had not been placed on the Commerce Department's blacklist.

On Friday, Bessent pointed the finger at Commerce for not blacklisting the company, telling CNBC that several government agencies "were in favour of putting Megaspeed on the entity list for months".

"But Commerce has the pen... and you will have to ask them about their internal process," he added.

There have also been questions about why an investigation into the Singapore-based company was stopped after Nvidia intervened. The New York Times reported that Nvidia's general counsel contacted Commerce Department investigators probing suspected chip smuggling by Megaspeed invoking a senior Trump appointee. The department allegedly then froze its investigation and handed the case to the Justice Department.

This apparently highlighted a significant disagreement about the direction of Trump's China policy. Huang wanted access to the Chinese market. Other officials were supposedly worried about the national security consequences. Lutnick was caught in the middle.

Ex-Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang summoned to testify before Congress about affair with alleged Chinese spy

Bessent blames Lutnick's Commerce Department for failing to block Megaspeed

Members of Congress from both parties want answers. Democrats have launched an investigation into how the Commerce Department vetted Chinese companies approved to buy AI chips. Republicans are also questioning the administration's approach.

And Jensen Huang's departure has not eased the pressure.

Republican representative John Moolenaar, who chairs the House Select Committee on China, said it was in the national interest for Nvidia and other American AI firms to succeed, but that companies "should never jeopardise our nation's security" by giving Chinese firms access to their most advanced chips.

Senator Elizabeth Warren, the top Democrat on the Senate Banking Committee, said Huang's resignation did not absolve him of responsibility, and urged Lutnick to explain why export control loopholes remain open.

Meanwhile Jensen Huang's team told Republican lawmakers on Wednesday that he plans to cooperate with their request to testify behind closed doors.

The White House has not responded to the BBC's request for comment.

It has, however, told the New York Post that Trump has complete confidence in Lutnick, describing him as "the most transformative commerce secretary in modern history".

That is a fairly emphatic show of support, particularly amid reports that officials are weighing whether Lutnick should be forced out.

There are some obvious questions. If Lutnick had indeed been warned about the risks, why did his department fail to act? And if other senior officials were indeed pressing for action, why did it take so long?

The BBC has contacted the Commerce Department for comment. But there is an even bigger question for Trump.

The president cultivated a close relationship with Huang. He was directly involved in decisions about chip sales to China. And, according to reporting in the New York Times, he was briefed on the intelligence concerns.

This is becoming difficult to dismiss as the misjudgement of one businessman or one government department.

Jensen Huang's personal life may have triggered the crisis, but the questions now reach into the White House itself. How much influence did he have? What did the president know? And who was ultimately responsible for protecting America's most sensitive technology?

Who is Nvidia's interim CEO Colette Kress - and who could take over permanently?

The rise and fall of Jensen Huang - how the tech superstar became mired in scandal

#207. Deutsche Bank shares sink as whistleblowers reveal Epstein payments, Trump tax leak, Russia bets

Deutsche Bank shares fell sharply in early trading on Tuesday after whistleblowers leaked about 15 million files from the German lender, with media reports revealing alleged historic failures across its risk management and financial crime controls.

These include suspicious payments linked to late financier and convicted sex offender Jeffrey Epstein, and accidental leaks of President Donald Trump's tax statements.

The leaks contain board minutes, emails, internal investigations and other material that point to systemic failures across the Frankfurt-listed lender's business functions stretching back two decades.

Shares in Deutsche Bank were 4.7% lower by 9:20 a.m. E.T., having fallen more than 6% in early trade in Frankfurt.

Deutsche Bank processed $582 million in suspicious payments linked to Jeffrey Epstein, far more than previously known, the FT reported. Suspicious payments are not, by themselves, proof that every transaction was criminal.

The German lender also mistakenly leaked details of U.S. President Donald Trump's taxes in March 2017, during his first term in office, the FT said. The blunder — described by CEO Christian Sewing, who was then Deutsche's private banking head, as "an awful mistake" — was kept secret from the White House.

The wide-ranging investigation — which was led by a consortium of more than 200 journalists worldwide including the FT, Der Spiegel and Le Monde — also said Deutsche Bank had failed to offboard around 28,000 problematic investment bank clients who had been identified for removal by early 2018.

It also alleges that the bank concealed information around cost targets in 2021, when external monitors in New York reportedly warned that Deutsche's risk appetite was running ahead of controls.

The files also contain details of the bank's relationships with oligarchs and transactions that the investigation links to Russian money-laundering concerns. It was these ties that motivated whistleblowers to send the information to Indian journalist Josy Joseph and South African journalist Khadija Sharife.

The reports once again put Deutsche Bank's risk management practices and controls under the spotlight following years of regulatory scrutiny.

The bank — which has been raided multiple times by German officials this year — has, since 2012, paid out some 18.6 billion euros ($20.5 billion) in settlements over alleged market manipulation, money laundering, mis-selling and misrepresentation, among other things, the FT said.

In a statement, Deutsche Bank said the issues detailed in Tuesday's media reports go back many years.

"Where relevant, we reviewed these matters at the time and worked closely with regulators," the lender said.

The bank said it has undergone a "fundamental transformation" under CEO Christian Sewing, pointing to 7 billion euros of investment in its risk controls between 2019 and 2025.

"Deutsche Bank is a different bank today and remains focused on executing its strategy which has proven successful, as evidenced by record profits in 2025 as well as in recent quarters."

CNBC has reached out to BaFin, Germany's financial regulator, for comment.

#206. Retailers are turning to entertainment to win shoppers’ attention

Legacy retailer Gap Inc. made an unconventional hire earlier this year: a chief entertainment officer.

It was a newly created role as the company tapped former Paramount executive Pam Kaufman to helm its entertainment, content and licensing platform. Gap has since rolled out major campaigns, partnering with bands like Just Your Type and building on the success of last year's ads with girl group Katseye.

"Beloved brands have value beyond the products they sell. Consumers identify with them, trust them and want to be part of their world," Kaufman told CNBC in a statement. "The key is authenticity: Every extension has to reflect what the brand stands for and give its fans a meaningful new way to experience it."

She said it's part of a broader goal to emphasize what Gap calls "fashiontainment," a strategy to blend retail and entertainment to better align with consumers. Gap is part of a growing cohort of retailers investing in entertainment and experiences both in-store and through exclusive online content to drive engagement.

"Brands are stories, and we are the storytellers," CEO Richard Dickson said at the Goldman Sachs Global Consumer and Retail conference in September. "I think you could see that come to life when you look at some of the work that we've done, putting our brands in the center of culture and cultural conversation."

In another blending of retail and media, Mattel made waves a few years ago for its "Barbie" movie, building on the intellectual property of a decades-old fan favorite and drawing in massive box office success. At the time, Dickson was president and chief operating officer of the company, witnessing firsthand the sectors' intersection before departing for Gap.

"I think the post-Covid consumer attitude around shopping certainly evolved a lot," said Bryan Eshelman, who leads the retail practice at consulting firm AlixPartners. "It has gone from shopping as a fulfillment task to shopping, in many instances, more as an entertainment form."

Between experiences in physical stores and media campaigns featuring exclusive content, Eshelman said, retailers are getting more creative about the ways they're driving engagement and marketing.

"Community, at the end of the day, is one of the key ways to drive loyalty, so I think retailers of all types are trying to tap into some sense of community, some sense of lifestyle, some sense of belonging that's beyond the product that they offer," he said.

Branded entertainment has been around for a long time. Procter & Gamble made headlines in the 1930s for helping to coin the term "soap opera," and companies have for decades created media campaigns to draw in customers.

But with the rise of social media, content creation and short-form offerings, experts said audiences are becoming hungrier than ever for something new and original.

The retail and entertainment intersection is happening at a time when intellectual property is becoming more valuable than ever, according to experts.

As consumers encounter more marketing than ever, the companies that will win the battle for attention are the ones that can "cut through all of the noise," said Alex Michael, senior managing director at investment firm LionTree.

"The hardest thing to find and to get is audience these days," Michael told CNBC. "IP, in that sense, is the most valuable thing out there, and only becoming more valuable because it's attached to audience."

At the same time, he said, the way IP is distributed is broadening to include video, audio, short-form, long-form and live event content. Consumers are also redefining their loyalty to brands, which means companies have to "play much earlier in the consumer journey" than they have in years past to secure their trust, he said.

The trend is also likely helped by executives who have worked in both retail and entertainment: Gap's Kaufman came from Paramount, for example, and Dickson and Skydance co-CEO Ynon Kreiz held top roles at Mattel.

"The gray areas have never been more pronounced as to what is classically defined as retail or media or entertainment or live events," Michael said. "People who have cross-sector expertise are increasingly more valuable because you can't be defined by a single vertical, especially as things change so quickly."

Still, there's room for newer players, especially with the studio landscape rapidly changing, said Sean Akaks, the CEO of SonderCo, an agency that partners brands with celebrities and entertainment properties.

"I think it's a really interesting time for nontraditional players to be making content because you're seeing this massive consolidation of these legacy entertainment companies," he said. "I think there are just more open lanes for independent creators and also brands and anyone outside the traditional studio system to create content."

That's where retailers might tap creators, Akaks said, because they can use their platforms to reach consumers directly.

"I do think there's a world where we're going to see creators and brands sort of co-creating IP that maybe they're owning together," he said.

Once those companies get consumers into the store, they're also leaning into experiential retail, making shopping a more immersive experience than it otherwise would be, like Dick's Sporting Goods offering interactive activities and simulators in its stores.

According to data from AlixPartners, companies investing in experiential retail such as Coach and Ralph Lauren saw close to 10% year-over-year increases in consumers' average basket size.

"The idea that retailers would potentially invest in some distributed electronic content to help drive traffic, and then when you get there, it's experiential — it really goes into the kinds of flywheels that entertainment companies are also trying to build and grow," said Jeff Goldstein, co-lead of the media and entertainment practice at AlixPartners.

Yet, the entertainment that retail brands are developing is only one piece in a larger puzzle to retain customers and attract new ones. Translating that into tangible sales growth isn't necessarily a given.

"The basics of having great product in stock at a transparent price that feels like value is not swept away by animatronics," AlixPartners' Eshelman said. "The fundamentals are still there, and if you're going to have a good retail proposition, you need to have that balance. You can't mask bad product at a bad price with no inventory with glitz and glamour."

#205. Heat pumps: How much heat does your home really need?

Floorplans. Air duct diagrams. Heat pump specifications. Most people in the market for a new heating system probably don't want to think about this level of detail.

But, perhaps surprisingly, some do want to think about those things. In extreme depth.

Every day, roughly 150 new users sign up to an app called Cool Calc, which works out the heat requirements of properties in the US.

Just over one third of those new users are not tradespeople but private homeowners, says founder and chief executive Bill Fiore.

When choosing a heating system, people can find themselves bombarded with information â how powerful a heat pump do they need, for example? It isn't always easy to take a tradesperson at their word.

"As an overall trend, we are seeing homeowners be more informed," explains Fiore. "They want to do a calculation on their own."

Cool Calc is used by tradespeople and homeowners to work out heat loss

A heat loss calculation is a figure, usually measured in watts, that defines how much heat a home loses during cold weather. It's worked out room by room and is a key part of designing an efficient heating system.

This estimate is especially important to get right when installing a heat pump, which is sized fairly precisely, according to the projected heat loss.

Should that calculation be off, the heat pump will likely perform poorly. You could be left with an underpowered device that struggles to warm your property, for example. Given the costs involved, homeowners are often anxious to ensure installers avoid such mistakes.

Certain new technologies are not, for the moment, helping, adds Fiore. "People are going to AI and they're getting a lot of misinformation because it's not backed by anything credible," he says. Cool Calc is planning to integrate with AI chatbots to try and improve the quality of the advice they spew out.

It is probably a good idea to be cautious about your new heating system's design, says Kevin Kircher at Purdue University. He and colleagues recently conducted a study, yet to be published, examining the heat loss calculations provided by tradespeople to roughly 75 US homeowners.

"We were surprised," says Kircher. "The heat loss calculations often led to overestimating the design heat loads by a factor of two or more." That would result in oversized systems that use much more power than necessary.

Not only that, some people reported charges of more than $1,000 (Â£745) â and in one case as much as $2,500 â just for a heat loss survey, adds Kircher.

Besides using excessive amounts of energy, an oversized heating system can also feel uncomfortable, says Dan Dempsey, a former senior engineering fellow for a major heating and cooling provider in the US.

Dempsey, who took part in Kircher and his colleagues' study, lives in Indiana. He says it is his retirement hobby to monitor the performance of heating and cooling systems in the homes of friends and family members, by analysing data from their smart thermostats.

"I spent five years manually downloading everything, every week," he says. He points out that oversized systems tend to cycle a lot, meaning they switch on and off again in quick succession.

This leads to discernible swings in temperature: rooms that fail to remain at their target temperature of, say, 20C, but rather fluctuate, which feels uncomfortable, says Dempsey.

For people seeking a better understanding of their home's heat loss, various free or partially free apps, including Cool Calc and AutoHVAC, are available. There are options for UK homeowners, too, such as web app Heatpunk.

Heat loss apps help drive business to renewable energy firms says Andy Rankin

Many things can influence the heat loss figure, including the amount of insulation in your home, whether the building has double or triple glazing, and also the size of your radiators.

Heatpunk allows people to draw a floor plan of their home and input data about the property in order to estimate its performance. Thousands of people use it every month, mostly professional installers, says Andy Rankin, founder and managing director of Midsummer Energy.

The web app also suggests specific heat pump models for each property, based on the results of the calculation. Naturally, these are models available through Midsummer Energy. "It's been a very effective marketing tool," says Rankin.

Dwellow, another company that offers heat pump installations in the UK, is keen to follow suit. The firm recently launched its own heat loss estimator.

While professional heat loss calculations remain essential, getting people to do their own "self surveys" first could ultimately reduce how much time professional heating engineers must spend analysing properties says Ian Mclaren, Dwellow's co-founder and director.

"Going into a home and just being able to check the information rather than start from scratch could turn a survey into a half-hour trip â far more efficient and less costly," says Mclaren.

There's no substitute for engineers doing a good job when it is their turn, however, stresses Maurizio Sibilla, of the Centre for Advanced Built Environment Research at the University of West England, Bristol.

"The solution is better installer practice, clearer customer-facing evidence, and improved methods, not expecting homeowners to do the engineering themselves," he says, adding that providing homeowners with information about the calculation and also data on heat pump performance post-installation can reassure them that the system works as expected.

Adam Chapman questions whether homeowners should do their own heat loss survey

Doing your own heat loss calculation may end up "over-intellectualising" things, says Adam Chapman, chief geek at heat pump training firm and installer network Heat Geek.

"I don't personally see what a homeowner would gain from it," he says. "We will guarantee the efficiency with way less radiators and pipework upgrades than any other system out there."

Chapman points out that only the most careful of surveys will capture a home's heat loss well.

He says it is common for Heat Geek installers to discover retrofitted windows that haven't been foam-insulated into their openings, leaving draughty gaps, or cavity walls that a homeowner thought had been insulated â but which were actually missed by the builder.

"It's stuff you can't see," he says. "What we actually need is home surveys that go and spot for these sorts of things."

Graduates are managing AI - but are they learning much else?

#202. The rise of 'housefishing' - when does using AI go from enhancing to misleading?

The images Olivia Nicole saw of a home on Rightmove (l) were completely different to how the property looked in real life (r)

Have you ever been impressed by the pictures of a property on an estate agent's website, only to find the place looks completely different when you view it in person?

Then there's a chance that AI was used to enhance the images you saw.

From repainted walls to imaginary lawns, estate agents are increasingly using the technology to make homes seem more appealing, arguing it helps buyers visualise the potential of a place.

But experts warn it can go too far and mislead prospective buyers - a trend dubbed "housefishing".

Earlier this year Olivia Nicole, 29, was searching online for five-bedroom properties in south-west London when a house on Rightmove caught her eye.

But when she arrived to view it several days later she initially thought she had come to the wrong place.

"The rooms were much smaller than they were in the pictures and the decor was totally different," she tells the BBC. "Some structural features were also different."

For example, there was a raised section of the wall behind the sofa that didn't appear in the images. The windows were also noticeably smaller. Olivia was so surprised she took her own pictures of the property for comparison.

AI has been used to make the property look more luxurious

It later turned out the pictures she had seen on Rightmove, which also appeared on the Zoopla and Winkworth websites, were enhanced with AI.

But that wasn't clear in the listing, Olivia says, and she feels the agent wasted her time.

"It was frustrating, because I had taken several hours off work for the viewing which I had to make up for."

All three companies told the BBC they had strict guidelines on how AI could be used on their websites and have since taken the images down.

Estate agents have long used wide-angle lens cameras or Photoshop to make homes seem bigger or more appealing to buyers, but AI is making this process much easier and more accessible.

It is difficult to quantify how common the practice is, says the Guild of Property Professionals, a trade group of independent estate agents, but it has issued guidance on how to use AI enhancement responsibly.

According to a recent survey, external of 906 UK homebuyers, one in five who had who purchased a home in the past year said they had viewed a property where the listing photos had been digitally enhanced to make the home appear better than it actually was.

Jack Bushrod reviews quirky property listings he finds on Rightmove for his social media channel, Housing Horrors, and says he has seen a "surge" of AI-enhanced images over the last six months.

He thinks using AI is fine up to a point - to declutter a busy room, or add furniture to an empty one so a prospective buyer can see its potential.

But he draws the line at anything that misleads a prospective buyer or wastes their time.

"I've seen one house where they had removed a pillar and added a window. Others might use AI to remove stains on the carpet or cracks in tiles. It's little details you would only notice in person."

He says most of the enhanced images he's seen weren't clearly labelled as AI-generated.

Cara says AI was used to empty her son's room in the image on the left

Cara, 38, is currently selling her home in Lowestoft, Suffolk, and says she was surprised when she saw the AI-enhanced pictures of her property her estate agent, Howards, put on Rightmove.

In one photo, her son's room was completely emptied of toys and clutter.

In another, of her daughter's room, the agent appears to have removed a doll's house, brightened the room, and used AI to change the shape of a rug, but that image has since been taken down.

Cara has had several viewings so far and says that one person has commented on the differences. But she says she trusts Howards, which has acknowledged the use of AI in the property listing.

"If they'd changed something structurally or it was really wild and didn't look like our home at all then I would put my foot down," she says.

In one picture (l), the AI removed a doll's house and changed the shape of a rug

A spokeswoman told the BBC that Howards had now introduced a policy of labelling all AI-enhanced photos. She added that the firm never used AI "to misrepresent the fundamental features of a property".

"It's about helping people imagine the possibilities, not changing the reality of what's being sold."

Trading Standards told the BBC that estate agents can use AI to make basic enhancements to photos, or to virtually furnish an empty room or garden, as long as they indicate they have used the technology.

But it says structurally changing a room, for example by adding a window, or hiding features could mislead consumers and break the law.

Jack Bushrod thinks all estate agents should have to clearly label AI-enhanced photos and mention any use of the tech prominently in the key features slide of a property listing.

He also expects to see more housefishing as time goes on.

"My worry is the more estate agents use AI, others will see the trend and jump on the bandwagon."

#200. Demis Hassabis’ AI company raises $5 billion, months after he left as Google DeepMind CEO

Isomorphic Labs, an artificial intelligence drug discovery startup founded by former Google DeepMind chief Demis Hassabis, has raised a $5 billion Series C, the company said on Tuesday.

Isomorphic's round comes three-and-a-half months after Hassabis stepped back as CEO of DeepMind, Google's AI lab, which he co-founded before the Big Tech giant acquired it in 2014.

The round was co-led by Greenoaks and Sequoia Capital, alongside Andreessen Horowitz, with investment from Alphabet, Google's parent. The fresh funds will help fund the design and development of new medicines using AI.

Drug discovery startups have seen record funding poured into them by investors in 2026 as companies look to capitalize on advances in AI. OpenAI and Anthropic have also been investing in tools to develop new medicines using the technology.

Hassabis has been CEO of Isomorphic Labs since the startup spun out of DeepMind in 2021.

"When we founded Isomorphic, our conviction was that biology could be understood and modelled at the most fundamental molecular level and radically accelerated through AI," said Hassabis.

"This round enables us to accelerate our progress: building foundational biology models, deepening our systemic disease understanding, and driving our first therapeutic programs towards clinical trials to deliver tangible impact for patients," he added.

Isomorphic is building a proprietary drug design engine and therapeutic candidate pipeline designed with AI, which it says will help it deliver the next generation of medicines.

Anthropic announced it was launching an AI drug discovery program in June and OpenAI partnered with Novo Nordisk in April.

Drug discovery startups have raised $23.2 billion in 2026, according to deal-counting platform Dealroom, a record figure.

Isomorphic last raised in May, with a $2.1 billion Series B.

#199. Burnham says 'win-win' deal can be agreed between UK and EU

Andy Burnham said he believes a "win-win" deal can be agreed with the European Union, adding it could be "more like the Brexit that people thought they were voting for" in 2016.

The prime minister is exploring closer ties with the bloc and is expected to sign new agreements on food, energy and youth mobility at a UK-EU summit on 20 November.

Speaking after meeting European leaders in Ireland on Thursday, Burnham said he hopes a deal could help lower prices and boost the UK economy. He predicted the negotiations could "go down to the wire".

The Conservatives have previously said Labour are "dragging us into the European Union via the back door with their EU reset".

While Burnham has argued that Brexit has done "more harm than good", he has ruled out major changes to the UK's relationship with the EU before the next general election.

Labour's 2024 general election manifesto pledged there would be "no return to the single market, the customs union, or freedom of movement", and Burnham has committed to this.

The manifesto also said the party wanted to "make Brexit work" and reset the UK's relationship with the EU.

Former Prime Minister Sir Keir Starmer struck a deal in May 2025 covering fishing, trade and other areas and next week's summit will see the UK government seek to agree further changes.

Burnham has also made clear he wants to examine the "pros and cons" of the options for Britain's long-term relationship with the bloc.

Burnham's push for closer EU ties causes ripples in his party and others

UK cannot 'cherry pick' EU single market deal, Barnier tells BBC

Brexit referendum 'possible' in Labour election manifesto, says Burnham

Can Burnham avoid another Labour welfare rebellion?

Speaking to reporters on Thursday, Burnham was asked to explain if his decision to reopen the debate on the UK's future with Europe had helped or hindered the negotiations linked to the summit.

Burnham replied "I think it has helped" before adding: "We've got a big week ahead of us to try and get this deal over the line.

"But let's be clear what's at stake: what we can achieve through this deal is lower prices for British consumers, lower costs for British businesses, more growth in our economy so there's really big prizes here, and I will fight now over the days to come to make sure we get this deal over the line."

Pressed on whether discounted university tuition fees for European students would be part of the deal, Burnham said "requests are coming from the European side, we are putting our requests on the table too".

He added: "I believe there is a good deal to be done that could be a win-win for Britain and Europe. I think it actually could be more like the Brexit that people thought they were voting for in 2016."

Ahead of the summit, the UK and EU agreed a draft deal on removing post-Brexit checks from food and agriculture exports.

Similar texts for deals on emissions trading and a youth travel and work scheme have also been circulated to EU nations.

As a result of the agreement, the government claims the UK economy will eventually see Â£5.1bn worth of annual benefits.

Burnham also told MPs last month that the summit presents an opportunity to make progress on "controlling illegal immigration".

Conservative Party leader Kemi Badenoch has previously strongly opposed any move to align with EU rules, saying it would "actually take us backwards".

On Thursday, Conservative MP and former minister Dame Priti Patel said: "Andy Burnham's obsession with the EU risks dragging Britain backwards, damaging our economy and leaving hardworking British taxpayers footing the bill."

The Liberal Democrats earlier this month described the draft deal as a "patch, not a fix" and said the "real solution is to re-join the single market and to establish a customs union with the EU".

Reform UK believes one of the "key benefits" of Brexit is that Britain no longer has to "adhere to ridiculous EU rules" and any form of alignment could have a negative impact on the country's trade policy.

The Green Party has called for the UK to re-join the customs union, with leader Zack Polanski previously saying "full membership of the EU remains the best option for the UK".

#196. Eric Trump-backed robot soldier startup can't pay staff after founder borrowed $4.6 million from company to buy home

Foundation Future Industries, a maker of military humanoid robots advised and backed by Eric Trump, failed to pay employees last month as its cash reserves dwindled, according to people with direct knowledge of the matter.

Workers have yet to receive paychecks after San Francisco-based Foundation missed payroll on Oct. 30, said the people, who asked for anonymity to speak candidly about a private company.

The situation has particularly angered some inside the startup because its co-founder and CEO, Sankaet Pathak, borrowed $4.6 million from the company last year to buy a luxury home, money that could have been used to pay workers, the people said.

Public records show that an LLC that names Pathak as sole principal purchased a $4.6 million Sausalito, California, property with "sweeping, cinematic views" of the San Francisco Bay in October 2025.

A spokesman for Pathak acknowledged receiving a detailed list of the major points from this story on Thursday, but didn't respond to requests for comment. Pathak's previous startup, a fintech firm named Synapse, collapsed in 2024, leaving thousands of Americans locked out of their savings accounts.

Foundation's cash crunch comes at a precarious moment for the company, which Pathak has said is close to unveiling a next-generation, weapons-ready robot that will help the U.S. compete against Chinese rivals. At the same time, Pathak is seeking to raise hundreds of millions of dollars in funding at a $3 billion valuation.

The company has garnered headlines this year for Pathak's claims that his robots could soon take on battlefield tasks in Ukraine or patrol the U.S. border with Mexico. Broadcast interviews and articles about Foundation also include claims that the company has won $24 million in Pentagon contracts.

"What we're about to unveil in the next couple of months [is] going to be the strongest humanoid robot that exists anywhere in the world, including China," Pathak said in an April television interview.

That April interview introduced Eric Trump, the son of the U.S. president, as a Foundation investor and chief strategy advisor. It is unclear how large his stake is.

Eric Trump and his brother Donald Trump Jr. have invested in an array of defense firms, including makers of military drones and autonomous weapons. A July Washington Post investigation found that startups backed by investment vehicles linked to the brothers had secured at least $3.2 billion in government business.

"We have to win robotics in the United States of America," Eric Trump said in April. "When you go up and interact with these robots ... they fist bump you, they high-five you, they follow your commands."

Eric Trump's involvement in Foundation drew the swift ire of Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., who asked whether Foundation won U.S. defense contracts because of nepotism.

"Eric Trump went on FOX to brag about winning ANOTHER multimillion-dollar contract from the Trump administration," Warren said in an April 23 social media post. "This looks like corruption in plain sight."

Eric Trump didn't respond to requests for comment. His spokesperson recently told the Financial Times that Eric Trump had "no involvement in Foundation's management, operations, strategic decisions or commercial activities, including the solicitation, negotiation, procurement or execution of any government contracts."

While the Eric Trump announcement created speculation within Foundation that a Democratic-led Congress may look to investigate the company, Foundation itself didn't actually win the $24 million in contracts, despite that assertion being widely reported across news outlets.

Instead, it acquired Florida-based Boardwalk Robotics in late 2024, and government research contracts won by that firm account for the claim, said a person with knowledge of the matter. Foundation used Boardwalk's robot Alex as the basis for its first robot, the Phantom MK-1, this person said.

Foundation's deal for Boardwalk included performance milestones for the robot that haven't been reached, said a separate person with direct knowledge of the deal.

In 2024, CNBC reported that letters Pathak sent to prospective investors cited an imminent investment and $300 million purchase order from General Motors, claims the automaker rejected.

Foundation co-founder Mike LeBlanc said at the time he was "embarrassed" about the marketing materials.

Insiders fret that Foundation could soon fold if Pathak doesn't secure fresh funding. Pathak has been seeking to raise hundreds of millions of dollars at a $3 billion valuation since at least January.

Pathak is Foundation's sole board member, a structure that has raised concerns among prospective investors about corporate governance, said the people familiar with the matter. He also owns most of the firm's shares.

That setup allowed Pathak to borrow $4.6 million last year, which he called a short-term loan at the time, the people said. Existing investors are aware of the loan through the company's financial disclosures, the people said.

"This is what happens in a company with no governance," said one of the people. "Are you just going to let it ride into the ground, and you ride off into the sunset?"

Foundation also ran out of cash for payroll in August, though employees only had to wait about two days to get paid after the company secured a bridge loan from an existing investor, the people said.

Pathak told employees this week in a memo that he was working on sourcing money to pay back wages.

The company was founded in April 2024 by Pathak, Tribe Capital CEO Arjun Sethi and LeBlanc, co-founder of Cobalt Robotics, a maker of autonomous security guards, according to a fundraising pitch at the time.

Foundation went on to hire dozens of robotics engineers and artificial intelligence experts, managing to lure some from Tesla and Amazon-owned autonomous vehicle company Zoox.

Before Foundation, Pathak was known as the founder of Synapse, a kind of middleman that enabled fintech firms like Mercury, Dave and YieldStreet to offer savings accounts and credit cards with the help of small community banks.

Synapse was a behind-the-scenes player until its collapse left more than 100,000 Americans with a combined $265 million in deposits locked out of their Federal Deposit Insurance Corp.-backed accounts.

While some customers received partial payments during Synapse's bankruptcy proceedings, thousands remain without access to their money after more than two years. A bankruptcy trustee, the former FDIC Chair Jelena McWilliams, said as much as $96 million in customer funds went missing.

The bankruptcy proceedings concluded last year without uncovering where the funds went; Pathak blamed a banking partner named Evolve, and Evolve blamed Synapse for the mess. U.S. regulators didn't intervene until the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau sued Synapse last year, opening up a $55 million fund for customer relief.

Pathak withdrew $320,000 in loans from Synapse as it spiraled toward bankruptcy in 2024 amid disputes over unmatched ledgers, according to the consultant and independent journalist Jason Mikula.

Federal prosecutors convened a grand jury to investigate possible criminal misconduct at Synapse, The Wall Street Journal reported last year. The Journal report didn't mention Pathak specifically.

The controversy didn't dissuade Eric Trump from investing in Foundation and Pathak, who he called a friend in the April broadcast interview.

"I walked into his factory, and I couldn't believe what I saw," Eric Trump said. "I'm telling you, he's doing a phenomenal job."

#195. Ex-Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang summoned to testify before Congress about affair with alleged Chinese spy

How could the Nvidia CEOâs affair threaten US national security?

Former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang has been summoned to testify next month before a Senate committee about his alleged affair with a woman suspected of being a Chinese spy.

Huang abruptly quit on Thursday as head of one of the world's most valuable companies after revelations of alleged extramarital affairs, in a stunning fall from grace that sent shockwaves through Silicon Valley.

Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, who is under scrutiny over exports of sensitive Nvidia AI chips to China, has also been summoned to appear before the Senate Banking Committee in December.

Nvidia has lost $550bn (Â£415bn) in stock market value in just five days as the scandal dominated headlines.

Senator Tim Scott sent letters on Thursday to Huang and Lutnick requesting their testimony in front of the Senate Banking Committee.

"Recent reports raise serious concerns about our national security and the protection of sensitive US technology," the South Carolina Republican posted on X.

"The American people deserve answers," he added.

Scott asked Huang to appear voluntarily on 3 December, but also warned the panel could issue a subpoena to compel his testimony.

Three House of Representatives committees - on intelligence and foreign affairs and a panel on China - have also sent a letter to Huang summoning him for a closed-door interview on 1 December.

How a sex scandal at Nvidia is putting pressure on the White House

How AI visionary Jensen Huang was undone by his own bad decisions

Lutnick is under scrutiny because unnamed officials in President Donald Trump's administration have been briefing US media that the commerce secretary stalled efforts to tighten export restrictions on advanced AI chips to China.

A commerce department spokesperson told US media that Lutnick had "proactively" agreed to appear before the Senate Banking Committee.

Huang, who has been Nvidia's public face since 1993, admitted on Sunday to having been "unfaithful" to his wife after reports he had an affair with a suspected Chinese spy, Alice Huang.

On Wednesday, he was reported to have had a second relationship, this time with an Nvidia executive.

Alice Huang (no relation) worked as an executive at the Singaporean firm Megaspeed. She has been suspected of being an intelligence asset for China, according to the New York Times. China has rejected that allegation as a smear.

Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick has also been summoned to appear before the Senate Banking Committee in December

US Attorney General Todd Blanche said on Thursday the Department of Justice had not yet drawn any conclusions about whether Alice Huang was engaged in espionage for Beijing.

Nvidia officials have repeatedly declined to comment since Wednesday. A personal representative of Huang also declined to comment on Thursday.

According to US media, Huang's alleged second affair was with a long-time Nvidia executive, Kimberly Powell, who reported directly to him within the company.

Sources have told the BBC that Nvidia employees had noticed the pair were unusually close.

Who is Nvidia's interim CEO Colette Kress - and who could take over permanently?

The rise and fall of Jensen Huang - how the tech superstar became mired in scandal

Huang had been widely expected to weather the storm after the first affair became public, even as Nvidia announced it had launched an investigation into whether its CEO had been "the target of a foreign intelligence effort".

One current Nvidia staff member told the BBC that the initial reports of his infidelity were more or less brushed off by rank-and-file workers inside the company.

It wasn't until Huang's alleged affair with the Nvidia executive became public that workers started to feel "this is crazy", the person said.

Another Nvidia employee said they had been told not to speak with the press or otherwise comment on the situation in public.

Many staff have earned millions of dollars in compensation through their ability to cash out their Nvidia shares as the stock soared.

Nvidia has said it has "no reason to believe that either national security or Nvidia secrets were compromised", but pledged that its independent inquiry would look at all available information.

The company's meteoric rise had been led by Jensen Huang, who became a major booster of the AI industry, which relies on Nvidia for the powerful computer chips it manufactures.

Initially those chips were designed for computer gaming.

But as AI technology evolved in the last four years into chatbots and large language models, companies building AI tools suddenly required huge numbers of the Nvidia computer chips that could process large amounts of data.

Nvidia is now hunting a permanent replacement for Huang. Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress was named on Thursday as interim CEO.

Several tech insiders that the BBC spoke with this week noted that Huang's downfall has been a major topic of conversation in San Francisco Bay area circles, an area filled with tech employees, industry executives and billionaires.

Huang has been married to Lori Huang for 40 years. Both of their children are also executives at Nvidia.

Nvidia's stock fall this week is the most significant five-day drop the company has seen since going public 27 years ago.

Its market value rose by more than 470% in the past five years, peaking just a few weeks ago. It is now down by 31% against a backdrop of wider uncertainty in the tech sector.

#192. Pressure grows on Trump's Commerce Secretary Lutnick in fallout from Nvidia scandal

How could the Nvidia CEOâs affair threaten US national security?

US Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent has blamed Howard Lutnick's Commerce Department for failing to properly oversee the export of high-spec AI chips, as the fallout from the Nvidia scandal widens to President Donald Trump's cabinet.

Pressure is building on Lutnick from across the political spectrum to explain his department's actions.

Jensen Huang was ousted as Nvidia CEO after revelations he had an affair with a businesswoman who was also allegedly a Chinese spy.

Megaspeed, the firm she worked for, had been probed by US government agencies over suspicions it was smuggling Nvidia chips into China, reports said, but was not on a list of firms US companies are prevented from working with.

Bessent told CNBC several agencies "were in favour of putting Megaspeed on the entity list for months".

"But Commerce has the pen... and you will have to ask them about their internal process," he added.

Bessent's comments come after Lutnick was called to testify before the Senate Banking Committee next month over the Commerce Department's role in allowing Nvidia to export its advanced chips.

Other congressional committees have asked both Lutnick and his department to retain all records relating to Nvidia and Huang.

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said some parts of the administration wanted more controls on trade with Megaspeed

Bessent said he was "frustrated" by delays in putting the company on the export control list and reports of leakage of AI chips into China.

"President Trump made it very clear that China should not receive any Nvidia chips above H100s, so any leakage is very frustrating because it hurts our great companies in the AI race, because it gives the Chinese an advantage," he said.

Last year, the Commerce Department had been investigating Megaspeed over suspicions it had been smuggling some of Nvidia's advanced AI chips into China, according to the New York Times. Megaspeed has been contacted for comment.

There was also pressure on the Commerce Department to add it to the list, but the Financial Times separately reported that the department was delaying efforts to have the cloud computing firm included.

However, Bessent also pointed the finger at Nvidia.

"The story has been thus far of Jensen Huang's personal foibles," he told CNBC, external.

"The real story here should be: did Nvidia know their client, and was there a robust effort at Nvidia to make sure that there was no leakage and that they were selling to what were obviously pass-through shell companies?

"It's important that anyone at the company there be held accountable for that."

Bessent's pointed comments are a rare instance of internal debate within the Trump administration spilling out into the public view, although tensions have reportedly simmered between Bessent and Lutnick in the past.

In 2024, the two reportedly jostled for the role of treasury secretary ahead of Trump's return to the White House, and last year the pair butted heads over the administration's tariff policy, according to US media.

How a sex scandal at Nvidia is putting pressure on the White House

How AI visionary Jensen Huang was undone by his own bad decisions

Huang has been summoned to testify before the Senate Banking Committee about his alleged affair with the former Megaspeed executive, who is suspected of being a Chinese spy.

The commerce secretary has also been summoned to appear before the committee in December.

"Recent reports raise serious concerns about our national security and the protection of sensitive US technology," committee chairman Republican Senator Tim Scott posted on X.

Democrats on key congressional committees have asked the Commerce Department and Lutnick to keep all records relating to decisions to allow Nvidia to sell its advanced AI chips to Chinese companies, including all their communications with or about Nvidia and Huang.

Ways and Means Committee Ranking Member Richard E Neal and Senate Finance Committee Ranking Member Ron Wyden are starting investigations into the way Nvidia was allowed to sell these chips.

They say Nvidia's advanced H200 chips could be sold to Chinese firms if they were first imported from their manufacturing facility in Taiwan into the US for inspection.

A 25% tariff - a levy paid by US importers to the federal government - was imposed on those chips under a section of the Trade Act that Neal and Wyden say is meant to allow the US president to take action against imports that risk national security.

"Trump's use of the statute appears to have done the opposite, undermining US national security and putting Americans at risk," they said.

Who is Nvidia's interim CEO Colette Kress - and who could take over permanently?

#189. Rivian, Lucid, Scout CEOs aren’t counting on Democrats to restore EV federal support after midterm wins

Leaders of several U.S. electric vehicle manufacturers said they're not counting on increased federal support for their businesses once Democrats gain control of Congress in January, but they said they'll gladly take it if it does happen.

That was the overall sentiment from CEOs of Rivian Automotive, Lucid Group and EV startup Scout Motors during interviews with CNBC after Democrats' historic midterm election wins.

"We've honestly constructed Scout from day one to be in an environment where we're not going to count on, let's say, legislation or count on things that may or may not be there," Scout CEO Scott Keogh, an automotive veteran, told CNBC on Wednesday.

EV makers have had to contend with flip-flopping support from Washington over the years. The Trump administration has ended or rolled back support for climate change initiatives and EVs that were supported by the Biden administration, including an up to $7,500 customer incentive for purchasing an EV.

Legacy automakers such as General Motors and EV startups alike had counted on such credits to help them boost their sales and assist in electric vehicle profitability. But now they don't have that same level of federal assistance.

Rivian CEO RJ Scaringe said it's his hope that the Democratic-controlled Congress and Republican President Donald Trump can find middle ground to make progress for the EV industry, but Rivian's not counting on that.

"We try not to plan for those sort of things. The reality is it's probably going to have less of an impact than we think," Scaringe said Nov. 6 following the election results. "I think we're going to actually have a lot of challenges getting things done."

Fellow EV manufacturer Lucid, which is backed by Saudi Arabia's Public Investment Fund, is taking a similar approach.

"You know, they say, 'Plan for the worst and … hope for the best.' That's all we can say," Lucid CEO Silvio Napoli told CNBC on Monday. "Anything positive, we'll take it."

The lack of expected support comes as Democrats seem to be more focused on hot topics such as healthcare and data centers rather than climate change and support for alternative energy technologies such as EVs.

CNBC previously reported that House Democrats plan to bring to the floor bills reinstating the enhanced health insurance subsidies that expired under the GOP-held Congress and reversing the Medicaid cuts Trump signed into law last year. They also plan to focus on oversight of the Trump administration and affordability.

Rachel McCleery, executive director of the nonprofit policy advisory Coalition for Reimagined Mobility at SAFE, said she believes affordability concerns also could include the cost of vehicles, including EVs.

"I fully expect there will be legislative debates over what to do moving forward, including on EV and EV supply chain incentives," McCleery, a former U.S. Department of Treasury senior advisor and Ford Motor policy director, said. "Bringing down the cost of vehicles for U.S. consumers is top of mind for Democratic policymakers, and electric vehicles are a critical aspect of this agenda because of the multiple challenges greater EV adoption solves for."

Scout's Keogh said if legislators decide to offer any type of support, he would prefer it to be for EV production, which could then have a trickle-down effect on vehicle pricing and help companies amid inflated costs.

"I always prefer it on the production side," Keogh said. "Anything that can drive down production cost is going to, of course, get passed along to the customer."

Affordability has been a concern for EV adoption as the vehicles typically cost more than traditional models with internal combustion engines.

Sales of EVs spiked last year ahead of the Trump administration ending the up to $7,500 incentive after September 2025, but have since come down from that high.

Cox Automotive reports the average price for a new electric vehicle as of September was more than $54,800, according to the most recent available data. That compared to the average overall industry at just over $51,100 last month.

"Ultimately, incentives can help accelerate adoption, but the industry can't build its long-term strategy around them," Stephanie Valdez Streaty, Cox director of industry insight, said Friday. "The goal has to be making EVs affordable and compelling enough that they make sense for more consumers with or without an incentive."

#188. Investors look to Nvidia's earnings and Anthropic's IPO to refresh the tech rally

I've spent the past 20 years pushing back against the periodically popular notion that markets are replaying the late-1990s and early-2000s tech bubble and bust.

We've simply never matched the levels of gross overvaluation, pervasive public greed, fevered IPO issuance, corporate malinvestment and Wall Street malfeasance that set up a generational market peak, a halving of the S&P 500 over two-and-a-half years and a lost decade for stocks.

We still haven't. Even if June was the peak in AI speculative fervor, as seems possible, and if Oct. 6 proves to have been a consequential broad-market top, the major indexes simply never stretched far enough into the thin air to make a potential retrenchment nearly as damaging as the early 2000s were.

Still, in a more muted fashion, markets have been behaving in ways that generate uncomfortable comparisons to past buying climaxes accompanying a technology revolution.

There is still room and time for the market to prove that the messy sell-down in AI-reliant growth stocks is simply a painful adjustment phase and the S&P 500's six-month purgatorial range a mid-cycle refresh. But it will need to start getting on with it.

Everyone wants to celebrate the way the market has absorbed the damage to semiconductors and other AI-buildout plays, rendering it a controlled demolition rather than indiscriminate destruction.

The Financial Times Unhedged newsletter on Monday called it "The perfect sell-off," the same day the Wall Street Journal cheered, "When Nvidia Stumbled, Equal-Weight Funds Delivered."

This is true, as far as it goes. A rush into defensive and "quality" stocks has largely offset the drag from Nvidia's $2 trillion market-cap loss from its October peak.

But recent defensive leadership, especially from low-growth consumer staples and Big Pharma, sends a less-reassuring macro message, and those groups lack the earnings momentum, narrative appeal or market-cap heft to carry a bull market to new heights.

BTIG technical strategist Jonathan Krinsky has noted echoes of the 2000 period when "boring" defensive stocks ripped as tech rolled over, supporting the tape for a while. Barclays equity desk noted Monday that the "quality factor" basket of stocks just had one of the best 100-day runs ever of the past 20 years.

And JP Morgan here plots the stampede into "low-volatility" stocks, mirroring the hasty retreat from "high-momentum" areas. There's room here for defensives to get even more popular before tripping multi-year extremes, but is that what an index investor should root for?

As for the ascendance of everyone's favorite, the equal-weighted S&P 500, Ned Davis Research analyst London Stockton this month noted that just over 50% of S&P 500 stocks were outperforming the index itself in 2026. Sounds healthy, right? Only to a point.

He explains: "Levels [of stocks outperforming the index] above 58% have most often occurred in and around years where there has been a large bear market." The current phase follows a period when leadership was extremely narrow, producing a "resemblance of the prior three years (2023-2025) to the two years prior to the last Tech bubble (1998-1999) with a very small percentage of companies outperforming the overall S&P 500.

"It is not likely that we are in a situation like in 2000," Stockton concludes, "but this highlights the importance that these large tech stocks at the very least do not decline substantially for the S&P 500 to continue rallying higher."

There are other straws in the wind that harken to prior tech busts:  The Federal Reserve is trying to restrain the corporate sector from overheating in the latter part of a cycle, same as in 1999. Retail traders have maniacally bought the cascading decline in Nvidia shares the past week or so, invoking the voracious dip-buying made famous in the late-'90s which worked until it failed spectacularly.  The market's refusal to pay up for sizzling earnings growth raises alarms about whether they are pulled forward, unsustainable or overstated; profits from 2000 were later revised down radically.

And Nvidia's alleged breach of chip-export rules and the forced departure of CEO Jensen Huang are a reminder that major corporate scandals were a key feature of the Tech Bubble's prolonged reckoning, though nothing resembling the multiple accounting frauds of that era are implied here.

Again, this is the kind of litany of worry and woe that gets recited each time a tech-propelled bull market breaks stride for a bit; more often than not a recovery follows to clear the gloom.

Perhaps Nvidia's results on Tuesday clearing the way for the company to restart stock buybacks and the final allocation of Anthropic's IPO shares will refresh the tech bid. With luck, the huge "hyperscaler" tech-platform stocks will move back to the fore as well-funded stewards of a more sober AI expansion.

Maybe, after weeks of Wall Street insisting that hedge-fund tech-stock positioning is scraping multi-year lows, this will finally presage an upside pain trade.

The vaunted seasonal, post-election tailwinds that the consensus was expecting to fill market's sails and lift the indexes from the floor of an enervating six-month trading range are running late. They need to arrive quite soon. Otherwise, the soggy tape - along with signs of ebbing liquidity reflected in Bitcoin weakness a strong dollar and jumpier equity volatility - will make those tech-bust echoes louder.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "Despite pervasive bearish sentiment and the average stock trading near new highs, our historical studies suggest the market remains vulnerable to disappointment. With defensive leadership still dominating, risk appetite weak, and financial conditions still tight, maintaining a short-term cautious/bearish stance still seems warranted."

-Twenty years ago this week, I wrote a cover story for Barron's that antagonized plenty of friends in the stock-trading profession by foretelling a future of fast, cheap electronic markets that has largely come to pass.

Called "Death of the Floor," the piece detailed the regulatory, technological and behavioral shifts that were ushering in high-frequency, low-touch trading and pressuring the old exchange model.

Reading it now, the conclusions seem utterly obvious and inevitable, though at the time there were still 2,500 NYSE floor brokers and specialists trying to remain in the middle of most trades.

"For the specialists who manage trading in individual stocks and for the floor brokers who depend on the in-person auction model, a tough challenge is becoming even tougher. To stay in the game, they must master cybertrading. For the small investor, this is almost entirely good news. Trading costs should continue to fall, while the likelihood of getting the best price should improve."

Happily, exchanges have adapted and evolved – to zero commissions, thousands of ETFs and microsecond executions - and we have a market that can handle multiples of the capital flow that was routine back then.

If I have one regret about the piece, it's that my good friend and former colleague Bob Pisani thought the CNBC reporter skeleton illustrating the article was meant to depict him.

I assured him, many times, that it was not.

-Almost every business relies heavily on its most loyal repeat customers. But this skew seems extreme for online-betting companies.

A Bloomberg piece picked up here by Yahoo Finance reports that one-third of all sports bettors produces some 97% of revenue at digital sportsbooks. It says the 1% most active gamblers on average lost 78 cents of each dollar bet, twice the loss rate of the norm among all customers.

One can view this as simply paying for entertainment (I "lose" 100 cents of every dollar I place on a movie ticket), but the social disapproval toward the industry seems to have grown.

Add it to the swirl of issues surrounding the legal and de-facto decriminalized wagering industry.

Perceived hostility of Congressional Democrats to prediction-markets venues taking sports bets in states that haven't explicitly approved sports gambling is seen as a plus for DraftKings and Flutter.

Meantime, Robinhood has blurred the lines between investing and gambling by aggressively expanding into prediction markets. And on Monday it was reported that Kalshi has looked to combine investing and betting from the opposite direction, acquiring a brokerage firm in order to offer stock trading to its prediction-markets clientele.

Adults should be free to behave unwisely with their money in search of a windfall. But brokers promoting the negative-sum game of wagering while professing to help the little guy accumulate long-term wealth sends a dissonant message.

The ducks that were quacking loudly ahead of SpaceX's June IPO are much quieter in advance of Anthropic's planned debut this week.

Here is the year-to-date performance of two ETFs that hold shares of recent new listings, Renaissance IPO and First Trust US Equity Opportunities, relative to the S&P 500.

As of June 12, when SpaceX priced, these funds had gone vertical as the public worked itself into an anticipatory lather, the Renaissance fund running ahead of the S&P by some 20 percentage points in the prior six months.

Since then, the IPO funds have deflated (both now have large positions in SpaceX, of course) and there's a bit more focus on whether Anthropic's target valuation is justified given the vast rethink of the AI-proliferation thesis.

All else being equal, a more favorable setup now versus then for a market being asked to swallow a new $1 trillion-plus ticker.
