#187. Why is Microsoft's outage lasting so long and will emails be lost?

Watch: What we know so far about the Microsoft outage

A major Microsoft outage has impacted schools, healthcare systems, businesses, courts and governments around the world.

Services including Teams, Outlook and AI software Copilot, are being restored almost 24 hours since issues first surfaced.

Microsoft said the outage was not a cyber-security incident and was caused by a technical issue. It has apologised for the disruption.

Here's what we know about the services affected and what went wrong.

Schools, GPs and businesses hit by global Microsoft outage as company scrambles to solve issue

Some Microsoft 365 services have been affected by "connectivity problems" with problems reported in at least 74 countries.

From late on Thursday, customers reported varying levels of access - many were unable to send or receive emails, view certain files or details on their calendar apps.

But some users were able to use Teams, messaging and online meeting functions without issues.

As of Friday afternoon, Microsoft said it had seen "encouraging recovery progress" in Europe and improvement in North America.

The company added that it was focused on "addressing the remaining areas of disruption" but warned "some users may still experience intermittent issues".

In a later statement, the company said it was continuing to see improvements "with email queues significantly reduced and approaching normal levels".

The company attributed the ongoing outage to an issue during an earlier network capacity upgrade.

"We have addressed the underlying issue and continue to restore service to remaining customers and regions," it said on Friday.

Although services are beginning to resume, Microsoft has warned of continued "intermittent access issues", meaning some users will still struggle to load their inboxes and could experience "delayed email delivery".

In other words, it is not quite safe to assume every email you send is going to arrive in its intended recipient's inbox right away.

Earlier, Microsoft said it would be extending the "timeout window" to 48 hours "to help protect delayed messages as delivery catches up".

The email timeout window refers to the amount of time Outlook will wait to receive an email.

When they do show up, the timestamp will be when they were delivered rather than when they were sent.

Although email service Outlook appears to be resuming, the outage has so far lasted for more than 20 hours - in outage terms that is considered a long time.

Prof James Ferryman from the University of Reading's department of computer science said the details the company has disclosed provide some clues.

"What Microsoft has told us is that the part of its servers that sets up secure connections is getting swamped, and once that happens the server just stops answering," he said.

"The trouble is that this kind of failure snowballs.

"One server falls over, its work gets passed to the next one, and meanwhile huge numbers of phones and laptops keep trying to get back in.

"You can't just turn it all back on at once. You have to bring it back bit by bit."

The challenge is therefore not just fixing the fault, but managing the huge surge of devices trying to reconnect once services start coming back online.

Gergely Orosz, a former leading software engineer at several major tech companies including Skype and Uber, said this could be the "most disruptive" business email outage "of all time".

He said it was still difficult to know just how many people have been affected by the disruption, but added he "cannot recall" an outage over business email ever taking this long to return to normal.

Australian cyber-security expert Troy Hunt told the BBC the outage could have far-reaching consequences.

"Microsoft is a massive part of the internet backbone and reminds us how critical email still is.

"Not only does this outage affect many people's ability to work but also things like the ability to communicate about appointments, purchase things that need verification, create or access accounts and services," he said.

In a statement, Microsoft said it was "deeply sorry for the disruption this incident has caused our customers and partners.

"We know the trust they place in our services and take that responsibility extremely seriously. We are seeing broad recovery and stability across our services."

More than 430 million people use Microsoft 365 apps according to Microsoft's website. The full scale of the outage has not been confirmed.

Microsoft 365 is used by companies worldwide and is expected to have a knock-on effect to other services.

Tech giants OpenAI and Anthropic both earlier reported problems as a result of the outage.

ChatGPT-maker OpenAI said it was investigating an issue affecting its connections to Microsoft's services, which had caused some tools to not work as expected.

From city governments in the US to Australian spy agencies, disruption across the globe has been widespread.

Courts from Los Angeles to Texas earlier warned that communications might be delayed.

In Canada, the public broadcaster CBC reported that a healthcare network running several hospitals in Toronto was experiencing email issues, but that patient care was not affected.

The government of Japan also said its shared IT platform had been disrupted.

Across the UK, GP surgeries, pharmacies, police and schools have been affected by the outage.

Some GP surgeries had urged patients not to submit messages via their website while police forces were also earlier asking the public to call if they want to make a report.

Have you or your business been affected by the outage?

#182. Microsoft says services fully restored after day-long global outage

Products such as Microsoft Teams and Outlook have been out of service for much of Friday

Microsoft has said its services have been fully restored after a worldwide outage affected several of its tools, causing disruption.

The tech firm said early on Saturday morning that efforts to address the underlying problem had been completed and customers using Outlook, Teams and Copilot should no longer be affected.

The outage hit more than 70 countries, disrupting everything from business operations to government services and personal users. More than 430 million people use the affected products worldwide.

Microsoft apologised for the technical issue, which it said was introduced during a "network capacity upgrade" and was not caused by a cyber-attack or an artificial intelligence agent.

Experts said the event was a "stark reminder" of how heavily businesses and vital services rely on a small group of big tech providers.

Users began reporting they were having trouble accessing Microsoft 365 products on Thursday.

By Friday, the far-reaching issue was affecting institutions from the Japanese government and the Philippines Supreme Court to the Australian spy agency, and trains, hospitals and courts in North America.

Services were beginning to recover by Friday afternoon UK time, nearly 24 hours after the issues first began to emerge.

Why is Microsoft's outage lasting so long and will emails be lost?

Courts, spies and MPs: How the Microsoft outage has been felt around the world

In the UK, some parts of the NHS were affected, as were some schools and police forces.

UK cyber minister Liz Lloyd said the incident had "limited" impact on critical frontline services such as 999 calls and that there had been no disruption to the transport network.

In Canada, however, the national rail network said passengers may not be able to receive email updates about their trips, and hospitals across the country also had issues with emails.

In the US, staff members from the House of Representatives told Politico it had set back an entire day of legislative work as everything was on Microsoft's Outlook.

Singapore's government acknowledged some of its systems had been impacted, and in India, the outage disrupted work for many on Friday.

In Turkey, advisers and officials attending the COP31 climate summit in Antalya were left frustrated by being unable to access Outlook.

The issues also affected smaller businesses and individuals.

Pete says his business has been brought to a standstill

Peter Pentz, a winemaker in South Africa, said his entire business had been effectively brought to a "standstill".

His winery's communication with exports was also brought to a standstill, he said, and "critical production work" had been halted as the Cape West Coast winery "relies on these systems for parts of our day-to-day operations".

Georgia from Staffordshire in England said she had been due to complete her first house purchase after waiting eight months to get to this point - but the outage has delayed that.

"The solicitor mentioned there may be a delay due to not being able to contact other agencies," she said.

"We're not the only one completing today - there are hundreds of houses and for them to ring everyone in time is unfeasible."

Microsoft first acknowledged users in the Americas were unable to access Exchange Online mailboxes at 17:54 GMT on Thursday, and shortly after warned the disruption was possibly affecting Microsoft 365 globally.

It launched an investigation and engineers began working to restore services, but by 04:36 on Friday Microsoft was reporting only a "partial recovery" in some parts of the world.

By 13:32, the tech giant said there had been "broader improvements" in North America, "sustained improvements" across much of Europe and "encouraging recovery signals" in the Asia Pacific region.

Microsoft said it restored services gradually due to the scale of the problem and to help ensure services remained stable once they were up and running again.

"We are deeply sorry for the disruption this incident has caused our customers and partners," a Microsoft spokesperson said.

"We know the trust they place in our services and take that responsibility extremely seriously."

A full post-incident report will be released once Microsoft has completed a review.

Experts said that while workers may have been happy to not have email access for a working day, the incident showed the dependence many organisations have on these cloud-based services.

"The global Microsoft outage is a stark reminder of how heavily we rely on a handful of big tech providers," said Rob Demain, chief executive of cybersecurity firm e2e-assure.

Matt Saunders, field chief technology officer at tech consultancy Adaptavist, said it would be challenging to mitigate against a similar problem.

"Classic IT disaster planning strategies suggest using a second service for resilience, but in practice, it's almost impossible for a business to function without tools such as Microsoft," he said.

"In the wake of this outage, organisations must have a plan in place for how to work without such tools, for when incidents like this occur."

#180. How the Jensen Huang scandal created a headache for business-friendly Singapore

A major scandal involving Nvidia founder Jensen Huang and a suspected Chinese spy based in Singapore has cast a spotlight on the city-state's success as a global chip hub - and a location for alleged illicit trade.

On Thursday, Huang stepped down from the US chip giant after admitting he had an affair. He was reportedly involved with a woman named Alice Huang, who ran Singaporean data centre company Megaspeed, a major buyer of Nvidia's chips.

For decades, Singapore has painstakingly cultivated a reputation as an open, business-friendly economy offering easy access to both the US and Chinese markets.

With the AI boom, it has become a major hub for the trade of high-end semiconductor chips in recent years.

Singapore was the second-largest billing centre globally last year for Nvidia, accounting for nearly a fifth of what the US chip giant earns.

But notably, less than 1% of its earnings were for chips that actually entered Singapore, as the island acts as a hub for many Asian companies to place orders and send them elsewhere.

Since 2022, when the US restricted advanced American semiconductors from being exported to China or acquired by Chinese companies, US officials have been concerned that other countries such as Singapore could be used by Chinese companies to gain access to the chips.

It became the biggest buyer of Nvidia's chips in South East Asia and is suspected of giving backdoor access to these chips to Chinese companies via its data centres in Malaysia, Indonesia and Thailand.

While Singapore does not prohibit the transfer of advanced AI chips to companies abroad, such moves can breach US export controls.

Singapore has said that while it does not enforce "unilateral export controls imposed by other countries" it expects all companies in its jurisdiction to respect such regulations.

The city-state has had some success in policing this.

It has charged four companies and five individuals - including Chinese nationals - for various offences including fraud, following US concerns that Chinese AI company DeepSeek had been buying Nvidia chips from third parties in Singapore, among other places.

They also tried to probe Megaspeed, but were faced with a number of difficulties.

In a statement released on Thursday, the government said that since February 2025, they had been looking into whether Megaspeed and other firms had made false declarations about data servers which were purchased and exported through Singapore.

The companies stated the equipment would end up in neighbouring Malaysia, but Singapore authorities suspect the equipment's final destinations were elsewhere.

Singapore said it sought assistance from US authorities several times to get more information from one of the investigated companies - Supermicro, a US-based company which the BBC understands was supplying computer servers to Megaspeed.

But "despite these repeated requests, Singapore has to date received limited information or assistance", it said in its statement. The BBC has sought comment from the US State Department.

Singapore authorities said they had also investigated Alice Huang - not for espionage, but for any possible role in Megaspeed's suspected fraud.

In the end, they found "insufficient evidence" of crime and let her leave Singapore in February this year, after holding her passport for several months.

Since launching their probe nearly two years ago, Singapore has yet to bring any charges against Megaspeed or any of the companies and individuals linked to the case.

Singapore and US investigations into Megaspeed are still ongoing.

In 2024, US officials had paid a visit to a Malaysian data centre owned by Megaspeed and saw Nvidia technology still in their boxes, raising concerns that Megaspeed was shipping high-end chips out elsewhere, according to a report by the New York Times.

Last year, Megaspeed appeared to have imported 50,000 more chips than Nvidia could account for, according to a Bloomberg report. Nvidia later adjusted its own figures after visiting more warehouses.

Megaspeed has maintained it has always acted lawfully and that previous inspections by authorities found no evidence of illegal activity.

On Thursday, the company said it "welcomes appropriate government scrutiny" and that it is "committed to working constructively and transparently with the US government".

China has also long rebuffed accusations that its companies and institutions are illegally obtaining high-end US chips.

Jensen Huang resigned from Nvidia on Thursday following a board meeting

How Megaspeed and Alice Huang came to Singapore highlights other vulnerabilities stemming from the city-state's openness.

In recent years, the city-state has become a prime destination for Chinese companies, with tech firms such as TikTok and Manus making it their global headquarters. Besides Singapore's ease of doing business, it has a population that is mostly ethnically Chinese and largely views China positively.

For a Chinese firm, Singapore offers a base near the mainland and a "neutral address" that global customers and investors accept, said Winston Ma, adjunct professor at New York University's School of Law.

But critics say this has given rise to "Singapore-washing", where Chinese companies use the city-state's clean image to obscure their origins, reduce suspicions and even cover up dubious activities.

Washington-based security analysis group C4ADS told the BBC they had assessed Alice Huang's setup of Megaspeed to be such a case.

BBC checks of corporate records in China, Hong Kong and Singapore found that in 2019, Huang sold a gaming company and its subsidiaries to Chinese gaming firm 7Road.

In March 2023, a 7Road subsidiary in Singapore named Huang as its executive director. The next day it changed its name - to Megaspeed.

Huang was in charge for eight months before she transferred the company to a Singaporean man.

During this period, Megaspeed went through "a critical period of corporate restructuring and regional expansion", and her corporate relationships raised questions about whether "Megaspeed's Singaporean identity is genuine or effectively Chinese-controlled", according to C4ADS.

Even after she transferred Megaspeed, C4ADS assessed that she was still "practically in control" of the company, based on her links to Megaspeed via another company as well as LinkedIn posts where she continued to identify herself as Megaspeed's "chairwoman" at conferences.

Megaspeed has not answered BBC queries regarding the company's and Alice Huang's links to China and the Chinese government.

Alice Huang, seen here in Taipei with Jensen Huang last year, ran Megaspeed for several months in 2023

All this reinforces the dilemma faced by Singapore.

"For the longest time, Singapore has managed to enjoy the best of both worlds with excellent relations with US and China," Nanyang Technological University politics and international studies lecturer Dylan Loh said.

Singapore has long positioned itself as a neutral party between the two superpowers. Its prime minister Lawrence Wong has insisted that his country is neither pro-China nor pro-America, but "pro-Singapore".

But Loh says this balancing act "has now fast become very difficult to manage and sometimes quite fraught too" with the US-China dispute over chips.

With the Megaspeed case, the tightrope Singapore has walked is set to get even narrower, according to analysts.

Beijing may ask why Singapore is investigating issues pertaining to US restrictions and whether they are siding with the US' interests, says Chong Ja Ian, a Singaporean political scientist and non-resident scholar with Carnegie China.

Washington, in turn, might read Singapore's latest statement as "pushing blame" on the US, which could lead to backlash from the White House, he said.

"Singapore has made a policy position not to take sides. But by not choosing a side, they run the risk of being seen as complicit" in either side's agenda, he said.

But Benjamin Ho, an assistant professor and China expert at Singapore's S Rajaratnam School of International Studies, noted that this was "not the first or last time Singapore will be caught between the US and China".

"This is the price that an open economy has to grapple with - you get caught in the middle when things turn sour."

Additional reporting by Jack Lau of the BBC's Global China Unit.

#177. Minister backs legal right to job support for young people

Watch: Pat McFadden refuses to rule out that some young people might lose benefits

The work and pensions secretary has backed the idea that the government should legally guarantee young people help to get into employment.

Pat McFadden told the BBC that job support should be given a "legislative underpinning", adding the level of assistance on offer was currently "vague".

McFadden also confirmed that some current benefit claimants would see their current entitlement to benefits reduced when they are reassessed.

But his Conservative counterpart Helen Whately said the idea of legally guaranteeing support "doesn't make sense" and ministers should focus instead on reducing taxes paid by employers.

It comes ahead of a long-awaited review by former Labour cabinet minister Alan Milburn, which will recommend on Tuesday that under-25s should be guaranteed job placements or training to help them find permanent work.

His report is also expected to say that under-25s should no longer be able to claim the health element of UK-wide universal credit - or personal independence payment (Pip), the main disability benefit in England and Wales.

Some could be eligible for a new "additional cost allowance" to cover day-to-day costs related to health conditions, under the plans.

Speaking to Sunday with Laura Kuenssberg, Milburn said the government should divert money away from the benefits system to boost work support, adding he wanted to see "billions" extra spent on help over the next decade.

However, the idea of reducing the entitlement of existing benefit claimants could prove controversial among Labour MPs, who scuppered a previous benefits-cutting plan under Sir Keir Starmer last year.

Milburn said the reduced entitlement for some should be accompanied by a new "duty on government" to provide jobs support to young people, such as apprenticeships, college places, job coaching, or a job.

He added that the temporary nature of previous job support schemes, including the Kickstart programme during the Covid pandemic, meant that they had been less effective as reducing long-term joblessness.

How the new guarantee would work in practice is not yet clear, with McFadden adding it would have to be "carefully" drafted.

Although local councils have some specific legal duties, including in the areas of child protection and homelessness, duties on central government currently tend to be much broader, such as operating a "comprehensive" health service.

Watch: Helen Whately says legal guarantee of work or training for under-25s 'doesn't make sense'

Asked on the same programme whether he agreed with the idea of a guarantee, McFadden said: "I do think we need some legislative underpinning to this."

He added that the offer of support "needs to be more firm," adding: "People need more certainty about what they are entitled to."

He confirmed that the government planned to move younger claimants onto a new benefits system when they undergo scheduled reassessment, and some of them would not receive "what they would get today".

Other claimants, such as those with long-term disabilities, would receive an increased amount, he added.

Also speaking to Kuenssberg, shadow work and pensions secretary Helen Whately said her party backed moves to reduce welfare spending.

But she criticised the proposed legal guarantee of work support, arguing it might "sound a nice thing to do," but ministers would be better off reducing taxes and regulations on businesses to help youngsters find "real jobs".

She added it was "absurd" the government had increased taxes for employers in order to "subsidise things that look like jobs". She added: "Just stop doing the taxing.

Elsewhere in his interview, former health secretary Milburn said:

a sustainable reduction in welfare spending cannot be achieved in two or three years, warning that "crude cuts" to the benefits system would create other additional costs for government

GPs should not be responsible for signing sick notes declaring people unable to work

ministers should "look very carefully" at Labour's manifesto commitment to phase out the reduced minimum wage for those aged 18-21, adding that moves towards this had "imposed a lot of cost" on firms

It comes as the government announced it will further widen eligibly for its scheme that offers jobless under-25s subsidised six-month job roles on the minimum wage.

From next April, eligible claimants will be offered a role after 12 months of unsuccessfully looking for work, rather 18 currently.

It is the second time that eligibility for the scheme has been widened, after the maximum age was increased from 21 to 24 earlier this year.

#173. Suspected China spy linked to Nvidia's ex-boss left in 2025, Megaspeed says

The Nvidia scandal has sent shockwaves through Silicon Valley as well as the political establishment

Alice Huang, a suspected China spy who has been linked to Nvidia's former boss Jensen Huang, left her executive role at Megaspeed in August last year, the company says.

Jensen Huang resigned as chief executive of the AI chip-making giant Nvidia after it emerged he had allegedly had affairs with Alice Huang as well as with one of his employees.

Megaspeed is the biggest buyer of Nvidia chips in South East Asia and the scandal at Nvidia has raised questions about the possibility that high-end AI chips may have ended up in China despite US export restrictions.

The Singapore-based company said on Sunday it had launched an internal investigation into its business.

Megaspeed said it was aware of reports "containing assertions about our business, supply chain and global operations that are not supported by verifiable evidence".

It added that it "conducts legitimate and lawful business" and has "retained external legal counsel to conduct a thorough internal investigation".

Alice Huang stepped down from her role as director of Megaspeed near the end of 2023, according to Washington-based security analysis group C4ADS but it said she was still "practically in control" of the company.

Alice Huang - who is of no relation to Nvidia's former boss - had been investigated by Singapore authorities in February 2025 as part of the country's probe into Megaspeed and other companies.

Also on Sunday, US Congressman John Moolenaar said that Jensen Huang had agreed to testify before the House Select Committee on China, which he chairs, as well as the Foreign Affairs and the Intelligence committees.

The committees had threatened to issue subpoenas to Jensen Huang if he did not comply with their requests to testify.

Moolenaar told CBS, BBC's US partner, that none were ultimately issued.

Jensen Huang's testimony is expected to take place behind close doors, with the dates still being finalised.

Moolenaar said: "That's going to be a very important meeting to understand to what degree privileged information was accessed, and to what degree this potential alleged Chinese spy influenced Nvidia's philosophy and may have had an influence on some of our policies with respect to AI and export controls."

Separately, a group of tech leaders including Space X's Elon Musk, have been trying to rally support to have Jensen Huang reinstated as Nvidia's chief executive.

A representative forJensen Huang declined to comment on a report in the Financial Times suggesting that he was discussing a comeback to the firm he co-founded.

The BBC understands that the likelihood of him being reinstated is very low while the company's internal investigations are ongoing.

The move would also face scrutiny from Washington, with Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent calling for Nvidia to hire its next chief executive from outside the company.

For the moment, Nvidia's chief financial officer Colette Kress is leading the firm and will present the company's results on Tuesday, 17 November.

On Friday, Bessent blamed the US Commerce Department, led by fellow cabinet colleague Howard Lutnick, for failing to properly oversee the export of high-end chips.

Bessent told CNBC that several agencies "were in favour of putting Megaspeed on the entity list for months... but Commerce has the pen... and you will have to ask them about their internal process".

The so-called entity list restricts US trade with overseas firms and individuals.

Lutnick has been called to testify before the Senate Banking Committee next month over his department's role in allowing Nvidia to export its advanced chips.

Other congressional committees have asked both Lutnick and his department to retain all records relating to Nvidia and Jensen Huang.

Megaspeed is suspected of giving backdoor access to Nvidia chips to Chinese companies via its data centres in Malaysia, Indonesia and Thailand.

Watch: Who is suspected Chinese spy at centre of Nvidia affair investigation?

Nvidia has been banned from selling its advanced semiconductors to China.

While Singapore does not prohibit the transfer of advanced AI chips to companies abroad, such moves can breach US export controls.

Singapore has said that while it does not enforce "unilateral export controls imposed by other countries" it expects all companies in its jurisdiction to respect such regulations.

Megaspeed said it "designs and maintains its global operational and supply-chain control frameworks to comply with all applicable laws and regulatory regimes". It said this includes "US export control requirements and local jurisdiction rules in every market where we operate".

China has denied that Alice Huang is a spy and warned the US not to use the scandal as a "pretext for imposing a technological blockade" on AI technology.

#171. South Korea will suspend all aircraft operation for 35 minutes and markets will open late. Here’s why

South Korea will suspend all aircraft takeoffs and landings nationwide for 35 minutes on Thursday to minimize noise during the English listening portion of the country's highly competitive college entrance exam, the government said Tuesday.

The restrictions will be in place from 1:05 p.m. to 1:40 p.m. local time, affecting 130 flights, including 81 international and 49 domestic services, according to the Ministry of Land, Infrastructure and Transport.

Planes already in the air will be required to remain at least 3 kilometers above the ground during the exam, while emergency flights will be exempt from the restrictions.

The measures will also apply to helicopters, light aircraft and drones, with all drone flights prohibited nationwide during the 35 minutes. Violators could face criminal penalties, the ministry said.

The government will adjust the departure and arrival times of affected flights, while airlines will notify passengers of the revised schedules in advance.

The Korea Transportation Safety Authority will also send text messages to licensed drone operators, including those who have already received flight approvals, warning that drone flights will be prohibited during period regardless of prior authorization.

"We will thoroughly implement the necessary measures, including restrictions on aircraft operations, to ensure students can concentrate ton the English listening test in a table environment," said Jeong Chae-gyo, director general of aviation policy at the ministry.

Jeong also urged airlines and relevant authorities to cooperate in adjusting flight schedules and notifying passengers in advance, and to advise travelers to check their revised departure and arrival times with their airlines.

South Korea's college entrance exam, known as Suneung, is a high stakes annual test that plays a major role in determining students' university admissions.

The country's stock market will also open an hour later than usual on Thursday, at 10 a.m. local time, and close at 4:30 pm, as announced by the Korea Exchange.

#168. Beauty advent calendars can cost hundreds but are they value for money?

It's easy to see the appeal of a beauty advent calendar. Like many women at this time of year, Julia says she often finds herself tempted to splurge on one - especially a perfume calendar. But she takes quite an unsentimental approach.

"I always look at what's in them. If I like enough of the fragrances, I'll buy it," says the 39-year old, who works as a senior commercial manager in engineering.

Luxury advent calendars are supposed to offer the thrill of a surprise but, for Julia, it is more important she actually wants the items inside - a thought that is also striking more and more customers in the wake of the scandal over the Skims calendar.

Customers likened the contents of Kim Kardashian's Skims calendar to something you would find down the back of a sofa

This week, Kim Kardashian's beauty brand offered all of its customers refunds for its $148 (Â£110) calendar after being widely criticised on social media over the quality of its contents, with some customers likening the hair clips, scrunchies and keychains inside to things you might find down the back of a sofa.

Julia says she earns a good salary but is keen to get value for money and doesn't want to waste it on items she won't use.

"I spent Â£160 on a Space NK fragrance calendar last year," she says. "I got some fragrances I'd been wanting for a while for a lot less than recommended retail price, and those I didn't like I managed to sell on for a slight mark-up on what I paid."

Julia, 39, says she thinks fragrance calendars are usually good value for money

Beauty calendars, which often cost upwards of Â£100 and sometimes much more, have been around for more than a decade and the marketing has grown ever more sophisticated.

High-profile brands will warn that their calendars risk running out, which may boost early demand, while unboxing videos posted by influencers can encourage spending by creating an infectious sense of surprise and delight.

And it appears to be working. Ipsos data shows, external around 15% of all advent calendars sold are beauty ones, with people aged 18-34 particularly willing to splash out.

But in the face of that tsunami of marketing, customers seem to be getting savvier.

"I love beauty, but I rarely buy beauty advent calendars. They are pricey and often only have a few things that I actually want to try or are trending products," says Priya Raj, 30, a fashion journalist.

She thinks some are good value but, for her, the advantage is being able to try on a small scale without forking out for a full-size product.

"For me, an advent calendar - particularly a beauty one - serves the purpose of allowing me to try out different products, particularly in the lead-up to Christmas, and then I can hopefully snag a bargain in the Boxing Day sales."

Priya Raj, 30, a fashion journalist, says the calendars "serve the purpose of allowing me to try out different products"

Podcaster Rhea Somaiya, 28, also likes the general idea, but has a healthy scepticism around a lot of them in practice.

"I do think some brands take the consumer for granted in that they provide each day with something so menial that people are bombarded with irrelevant and cluttering items.

"And when that's the case, they certainly put me off as the excitement to open each window dwindles into irritation rather than joy."

Podcaster Rhea Somaiya, 28, is less keen on the calendars and says they leave consumers "bombarded with irrelevant and cluttering items"

Jessica Jasper, a marketing consultant, is more positive - she thinks it's worth it. But she also thinks some companies are just jumping on a trend and that "some brands are getting a little carried away with the pricing".

So can beauty calendars offer value for both the brands and the customer - an example of a win-win? Or are they mainly designed to make us spend more at Christmas than we need to?

"Some brands are getting a little carried away with the pricing," says marketing consultant Jessica Jasper

Heather Widdows, a beauty expert and philosophy professor at Warwick University, has pointed out that while you might buy some of the products in an advent calendar individually, it's highly unlikely you'd buy all of them.

"Beauty calendars may offer you lots of products for a really good price. They can also be a bit of fun and feel sensuous.

"But it is exceptionally unlikely that you would use all the products inside them, so they're probably not worth it."

This year, Julia has spent Â£99 on a John Lewis fragrance calendar which she thinks is good value for money. But it doesn't mean she'll keep everything in it.

In fact she's joined a Facebook group where people trade the ones they don't want for ones they do.

"The ones I keep are already a lot cheaper per mililitre than if you bought them at retail," she says.

#166. Avoid this Black Friday scam - how to check it's a real deal

This month's Black Friday and the tax return deadline at the end of January are both opportunities for fraudsters to scam you.

That's because people are more likely to expect emails, texts and other correspondence at this time of year.

Fraudsters will use the dates to impersonate genuine organisations and retailers by pushing victims towards fake websites to steal personal information.

Here's how to spot a scam and how you can avoid being caught out.

If you are self-employed or have more than one source of income you will need to file a tax return by the end of January.

Fraudsters seize on this deadline to blitz people with messages that look like they are official and from the UK's tax authority, HMRC.

Any such calls or messages should be ignored, but you can also check whether an email, external, text message, external or phone call, external claiming to be from HMRC is genuine.

"Reporting scams helps us shut them down and protect others," adds Lucy Pike, HMRC's chief security officer.

New figures from HMRC, shared with the BBC, show that it received 95,340 reports of suspected scams in the year to the end of October.

There were 900 reports of one specific scam which used emails containing a malicious attachment which directed customers to a fake gov.uk webpage designed to steal personal and financial information.

it never contacts people by email, text or phone to tell them they are due a refund or ask them to claim one

it never asks for personal or financial information by email or text message

it never leaves threatening voicemails about legal action or arrest

In the run-up to Black Friday you may be looking around for discounts, but fraudsters will impersonate genuine retailers to steal personal details or take payment for a product that will never be delivered.

Kirsty Adams, a fraud and scams expert at High Street bank Barclays, says make sure you pause before you buy.

"When you're scrolling on the sofa and a countdown timer starts ticking, or a deal pops up in your feed from an account that looks just like a brand you trust, it's easy to get swept up and pay before you've had a chance to check.

"No deal is worth rushing for. So take a moment before you buy."

The bank also advises doing these four things:

Pause and check, rather than being blinded by a countdown timer

Pay safely using a credit or debit card via a trusted payment service, not a bank transfer to a stranger

Open an official website or app yourself rather than clicking on links in emails, ads or social posts

Talk to a friend, family or your bank if you think something does not feel right, and calling your bank straight away if you think you've been scammed

Doing these things now will help you save money at Christmas

Don't fall for this - how to protect yourself from AI scams

#164. Big-ticket holiday items may be harder to find as shipping costs rise

This holiday season, big-ticket items are expected to be in short supply with diesel prices near records, trucking rates close to a post-pandemic level and carrying costs hitting multidecade highs. All of that is forcing many retailers to rethink their holiday inventory strategy.

"We are going to have some of the lowest inventory levels on some of these big-ticket items we have seen in history," said Deborah Weinswig, CEO of retail analytics firm Coresight Research.

Weinswig said retailers she is working with are holding less inventory of big-screen TVs, grills, large consumer appliances like washers and dryers, and other items that take either big amounts of space or cost a lot of money to hold.

"It's freight getting it from there to here; it's carrying costs; then it's just the trucking to get it into the stores," Weinswig said. "We're talking three different pieces right here and each is up significantly year on year."

The surge pricing for supply chain services is being caused by domestic and international policy.

According to Evercore ISI, the rates for general trucking have increased more than 47% year over year during the week of Nov. 2.

Industry experts say a large factor has been a crackdown by the federal government on non-citizen truck drivers and non-U.S.-based truck drivers operating in the U.S. That has created a capacity crunch that has raised rates and is expected to continue to create upward pressure.

At the same time, fuel surcharges are spiking.

Most transportation providers charge customers an extra fuel surcharge to offset changes in the cost of diesel fuel. That surcharge has spiked 64% year over year, according to Evercore ISI data, as diesel prices have hit new record highs due in part to the U.S.-Israel war with Iran.

And inventory holding costs — or the total expense of storing unsold goods, which could include the costs for capital, service, storage and other risk factors — are closely tied to the yield on the 10-year Treasury note, which exceeded 5% in October and remains at multiyear highs in November.

Collectively, the higher costs could mean bigger discounts for shoppers willing to wait, according to Bill Catania, CEO of logistics technology company OneRail, which powers same-day delivery for companies such as FedEx and Lowe's.

"You're going to see more discount and price promotions as we get closer to the holiday to liquidate inventory, so they're not having to transfer it or dispose of it or position it some other way," Catania said.

Retailers are also facing a conundrum in their holiday inventory strategy when it comes to shipping costs versus opportunity costs. In other words, is it worth it to stock up now, or wait it out in hopes that shipping rates will come down some but potentially miss out on having the right product mix?

It's leading to a wait-and-see approach for stocking shelves after early holiday sales in October, executives said.

UPS CEO Carol Tomé said on the company's earnings call on Oct. 27 that it's "just starting to get peak season forecasts from our customers."

Similarly, Derek Leathers, CEO of Werner Enterprises, a trucker for big-box stores and major retailers, said retailers are cautious.

"When rates are higher and the cost of capital is more expensive, you are going to have a much more intense focus on not having excess inventory," Leathers said.

Still, 2026 holiday sales are expected to top $1 trillion as inflation drives growth and customer spending is expected to remain healthy, according to Bain & Company's annual forecast. Leathers said even with the surge in trucking and shipping rates, he's seeing that retailers are willing to pay up to stock up.

"The bigger concern right now is wanting to make sure they have their capacity locked in. There is way more dialogue around the assurance of having a truck or whatever mode that needs to be secured," Leathers said. "Not saying they are price insensitive, because they are certainly always concerned about price. But you can't sell it if it's not on the shelf."

ShipMatrix, a data analytics firm for the logistics industry, forecasts 2.4 billion packages will be delivered during the 2026 holiday peak, a 4% increase year over year.

It's an increase that will likely lead retailers to increasingly use gig delivery, especially later in the holiday peak. Gig workers allow retailers and delivery companies to adjust to changes in order flow and accommodate surges of last-minute shopping without spending time and money onboarding new team members.

"Gig economy delivery is great. You know, it fulfills fast, but there's better unit economics out there," Catania said.

Industry experts said they also expect to see more retailers use drones for last-mile delivery for smaller items.

A 2024 PwC study forecast that the cost of drone delivery would "drop by more than 70% over the next 10 years, which will not only match traditional last-mile delivery costs, but surpass them and create an inflection point for the industry."

Amit Regev, CEO of drone company Flytrex, said the company is in active talks with retailers about using drones for holiday deliveries.

"The expectation now is becoming so much more instant," Regev said.

Retailers have been increasing their use of drones. Lowe's in September said it was launching a drone delivery service in partnership with DoorDash and Alphabet's Wing. Amazon earlier this year said it was aiming to offer drone deliveries in nearly 500 U.S. cities and towns by the end of the year.

Flytrex currently delivers for Walmart and a number of restaurants including Little Caesars and Papa John's.

"Customers are looking for that instant gratification," Regev said. "So if it takes too long, they might not want to order with you. And from what we are seeing that's a stronger incentive for them to try and test or deploy deliveries using drones."

#163. St. Louis Fed's Musalem says four more rate hikes may be needed in coming months

St. Louis Federal Reserve President Alberto Musalem indicated Tuesday that the central bank may need as many as four rate hikes in coming months, offering one of the most hawkish views yet by a Fed policymaker about the steps needed to quell inflation.

During a speech delivered in Argentina, Musalem made the case that current policy is not significantly restricting economic activity.

"The economy will likely require several interest rate increases before the spring of 2027 to achieve 2% inflation in a timely manner," he said.

Musalem said the real policy rate, which subtracts the inflation rate from the Fed's target rate, needs to be between 2%-2.5% by next spring, and estimated that the current rate is about a full point below that range. That position suggests the Fed may need to hike at each of the upcoming Fed meetings in December, January, March and April.

Musalem does not get a vote on the rate-setting Federal Open Market Committee over the period where he says that the rate increases will be needed, though he does get to present views and provide input on the "dot plot" of individual members' expectations. He will next vote in 2028.

To be sure, Musalem noted "an unlikely but still plausible scenario" in which no further hikes would be needed.

"Falling energy prices and lower shipping costs, or an increase in potential output growth driven by higher productivity growth, might ease inflation pressures such that inflation moves toward target in 18 months," he said. Another possibility is that demand might decelerate as spending on artificial intelligence slows.

However, he said the more likely scenario is that "persistent strong demand and ongoing supply pressures" combine to keep inflation above the Fed's 2% target, thus requiring further increases in the fed funds rate from its current range of 3.75%-4%.

"It would be preferable for a higher real policy rate to happen in a balanced way, driven both by lower inflation and a higher nominal policy rate," he said. "However, the nominal policy rate may need to do most of the work."

#162. Intel sued by investors after PC sales slump hits share price

Intel, the US chip giant, is being sued by its shareholders who accuse the firm of hiding the severity of a slump in its personal computer (PC) sales that caused its share price to plunge almost 29% in one day.

Billions of dollars was knocked off the value of the company after it forecast disappointing revenues ahead of its latest earnings report.

Shareholders allege that Intel made "materially false and misleading" statements that failed to disclose the slowdown in PC sales and the impact of its data centre projects, according to a proposed class action lawsuit filed in San Jose, California.

In October, Intel firm announced its computer hardware sales had dropped sharply in the three months that ended in September.

That report undermined earlier suggestions from the chipmaker that demand had been improving, sending Intel shares plunging, according to the lawsuit.

Shareholders claimed the "depressed demand for PC sales" caused "a dramatic slowdown" in quarterly earnings â a drop of 9%, or about $800m - to $8.1bn.

The lawsuit, filed by legal firm Robbins Geller Rudman & Dowd on behalf of owners of Intel common shares between 23 July and 29 October this year, was filed against the chipmaker, its chief executive Tan Lip-Bu and other company leaders.

Intel has poured huge investments into manufacturing to meet the enormous demand for AI computing power.

As more semiconductors are channelled towards data centres, chips for PCs and other electronics have become more expensive.

Intel boss Tan had told analysts that the industry was facing an "unprecedented memory crunch", resulting in a shortage of chip supplies for PCs.

"We need to address these challenges with urgency," Tan said in October.

Intel shares have fallen sharply in the past month by 37.5%, though its share price is still up by 51% overall this year.

#159. Gas starts flowing from Jackdaw after government green light

The Jackdaw platform is located 150 miles east of Aberdeen

Gas has begun flowing from the Jackdaw gas field, with the UK energy secretary saying it will help to heat millions of homes and businesses this winter.

Ministers last month announced the Jackdaw field, which lies 150 miles east of Aberdeen, had been approved after years of uncertainty and a court case.

It has now been announced that it has begun producing gas which will start flowing into Britain, according to its operator Adura.

The UK government approved production from the gas field in the North Sea last month, amid intense debate about the project's environmental and economic impact.

Miliband says he won't resign if government approves Rosebank oil field

Burnham defends Jackdaw decision as Badenoch urges PM to overturn ban on new oil and gas licences

Jackdaw is operated by Adura under a joint venture between Shell and Norwegian state energy firm Equinor.

A decision is yet to be taken on whether production can begin at Rosebank, the UK's largest known untapped oil field, which is located off the coast of Shetland in the North Atlantic.

Energy Secretary Miatta Fahnbulleh said: "Last month, I gave Jackdaw the green light and today its gas will start flowing into Britain, thanks to the hard work of the skilled North Sea workers.

"It means homegrown gas from this project will help to heat millions of British homes and businesses this winter and beyond, in a boost for our energy security.

"It also will help minimise the UK's use of imported higher emission gas as we continue our transition to renewables and nuclear."

Claire Mueller, Jackdaw project director at Adura, said the gas field was "now helping to power Britain".

She added: "At a time of considerable uncertainty around the world, Jackdaw will provide secure energy from our own waters to heat British homes and power our economy, while reducing our reliance on imports with higher emissions.

"Jackdaw also demonstrates what can be achieved when Britain makes pragmatic, evidence-led decisions about the energy it needs, while properly accounting for our climate responsibilities."

Mueller said the decision was a "positive step towards restoring confidence in the North Sea".

She added that Adura was "proud to be producing the oil and gas our country will continue to need for decades".

Why is the Jackdaw gas field approval so controversial?

Adura has said Jackdaw will provide about 6% of UK gas production, enough to power 1.4 million homes. After imports are included, that amounts to about 2% of total UK demand.

The gas will be pumped to the existing Shearwater platform for processing â known as a tie-back in industry terms â before being piped to the St Fergus gas terminal in Aberdeenshire.

The UK government said that over its expected 11-year lifespan, Jackdaw would support the UK's gas supply, protect hundreds of jobs and add Â£2.9bn to the economy.

The decision to green-light Jackdaw drew criticism from some Labour backbenchers and environmentalists, while Conservatives called on the government to also approve the Rosebank oil field.

Both projects were approved for development by the previous Tory government in 2022 and 2023 respectively but these decisions were struck down by the Scottish courts after a challenge from environmental campaigners.

This followed a Supreme Court ruling that developers had to account for the climate impact caused by burning fossil fuels in their planning applications.

Prime Minister Andy Burnham has pledged to stick to the UK's net zero target despite giving the green light to Jackdaw.

He previously said the decision would "significantly strengthen our energy security by increasing domestic gas production by about 6% and support the UK's energy supply for more than a decade".

A decision on the larger Rosebank oil project is yet to be made.

Protesters gathered outside the Department for Energy Security and Net Zero in Westminster on Wednesday morning

The co-leader of the Scottish Greens, Ross Greer, described the Jackdaw approval as "climate vandalism".

He said: "It is a devastating betrayal of our children's future and it won't even lower energy bills in the here and now."

Environmental campaigners argue that this summer's heatwaves demonstrate the need to tackle climate change by rejecting both Jackdaw and Rosebank.

Areeba Hamid, the co-executive director of Greenpeace, said approving Jackdaw was "the wrong decision" in terms of lowering energy bills, providing energy security, and addressing climate change.

Oxfam said the government had "drilled straight through the UK's climate commitments" and accused the prime minister of sending "the wrong signal as the impacts of climate change intensify".

The charity says the government must commit to not approving any new oil and gas licences, including the proposals for Rosebank.

Jackdaw's operators said its approval was vital to avoid domestic supply shortages

Jackdaw sits close to the boundary between the British and Norwegian sectors of the North Sea, in an area where Westminster originally granted licences to explore for oil and gas in 1970 and 1989.

The field was discovered in 2005 but it was expensive and technically difficult to exploit.

Shell did not commit to its development until 2022, shortly after the Conservative government had granted consent for production.

But in January 2025, after a challenge by environmentalists, the Court of Session in Edinburgh ruled that consent for Jackdaw and Rosebank had been granted unlawfully.

The judge, Lord Ericht, said the developers should have provided a more detailed account of the fields' impact on climate change as part of the permitting process.

The judge allowed the companies to continue with preparatory work short of producing oil and gas while they crafted new environmental impact assessments taking into account the eventual effect of actually burning the fossil fuels, not just the emissions which would be created in extracting them.

An inside look at Jackdaw, the UKâs newest and most contested gas platform.

When BBC News visited the platform this summer, Adura CEO Neil McCulloch said its approval was vital to avoid domestic supply shortages this winter.

However, the UK government has previously suggested that Jackdaw will have little impact on energy security and none on reducing domestic gas and electricity bills.

In August, in an interim response to a consultation on the future of the country's gas supplies, Energy Minister Michael Shanks said the UK would inevitably become more reliant on imports as domestic production declined, external.

"With diminishing output from the North Sea over the past decades, any gas produced from the UK Continental Shelf represents a tiny fraction of global demand and so will not lower prices nor materially boost supply," wrote Shanks.

Labour has previously said it is opposed to the issuing of new exploration licences but it will consider new production projects if they are tied back to existing facilities.

McCulloch described the Jackdaw decision as "an important and positive step forward" - but said attention now turned to Rosebank.

He has urged the UK government to approve production from the field, in which Adura owns an 80% stake. The other 20% is held by London-based Ithaca Energy.

Rosebank contains an estimated 300 million to 500 million barrels of oil.

But while most of Jackdaw's gas is likely to be consumed in the UK, Rosebank's oil will be taken off by tanker and sold on international markets.

UK government approves drilling in Jackdaw gas field with production to start this month

Chris Mason: Jackdaw approved but tricky energy trade-offs remain

#158. U.S. agencies say they initiated investigation into Nvidia partner Megaspeed in 2024

The U.S. Commerce and Justice Department on Monday stateside said they had initiated an investigation into Singapore-based company Megaspeed in 2024 to "address inaccurate information circulating in public reporting."

Megaspeed International, a cloud computing firm that operates data centers across Southeast Asia, has been drawn into the fallout surrounding the leadership shake-up at chip giant Nvidia.

In a joint release, the departments outlined a timeline of their investigations into Megaspeed, saying the probes began in 2024 over potential export-control violations.

In June 2025, Nvidia placed Megaspeed on its "no sell" list, after Commerce Department investigators requested records from Nvidia concerning direct or indirect sales to the company.

In the same month, U.S. officials and Singapore police conducted an in-person inspection of Megaspeed and interviewed Alice Huang, who was allegedly involved in a relationship with Nvidia co-founder Jensen Huang.

"Following this enforcement action, the Department of Justice took the lead role in the criminal investigation of Megaspeed," the statement said.

On Wednesday, Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick also reposted the statement, adding, "The Department of Commerce works to protect the U.S. economy and our critical industries every day. We are very proud of the work of the Bureau of Industry and Security."

In response to the statement, Chris McGuire, senior fellow for China and emerging technologies at the Council of Foreign Relations wrote on X, "Very, very strange statement tonight... about Megaspeed. The facts here just don't add up, and this does nothing to explain why Commerce did not cut Megaspeed off from chips."McGuire said that Megaspeed kept buying chips after Nvidia put them on their no-trade list, and asked if Nvidia also blacklisted Megaspeed's subsidaries Speedmatrix and PT Megaspeed International. "I would bet they did not! If they kept shipping to them after blacklisting Megaspeed, that would be very concerning," McGuire added.

The statement comes after Singapore's Ministry of Home Affairs said the first time authorities there learned of U.S. interest in Megaspeed and Alice Huang was in June. Singapore had already been conducting its own independent investigation and said she was not investigated for espionage.

The city-state barred Alice Huang from re-entering the country in February 2026 after authorities found that she had knowingly provided inaccurate information about her role at Megaspeed.

Alice Huang was reportedly recruited by China's Ministry of State Security to gather information on Jensen Huang. The Justice Department has not reached a conclusion on whether she was a Chinese spy.

Jensen Huang resigned as CEO last week after revelations about multiple extramarital relationships, including with two Nvidia employees. The tech giant has appointed Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress as interim CEO.

The agencies' statement also comes as Nvidia prepares to report its third-quarter results Tuesday afternoon U.S. time, with Kress set to lead her first earnings call.

Earlier on Monday in the U.S., the Commerce Department moved to close a loophole that has allowed Chinese companies to access Nvidia's advanced computing power through data centers in third countries.

The interim final rule from the department's Bureau of Industry and Security is aimed at restricting remote access to advanced U.S. computing technology.

It also comes amid growing scrutiny of Lutnick for not having done enough to prevent restricted Nvidia chips and computing capacity from reaching Chinese companies.

— CNBC's Anniek Bao and Dan Mangan contributed to this report.

#156. Faisal Islam: What's in the Brexit reset food deal and are there trade-offs?

Some British sausages have faced barriers to being exported to the EU

In Boston, Lincolnshire, a supplier of ready meals to Marks & Spencer (M&S) manufactures thousands of portions of spaghetti bolognese to be sent to the retailer's shops in the Republic of Ireland.

Every plastic pallet is meticulously individually tagged with a green-grey label âEireâ.

Every truck full of food sent to European Union countries requires hundreds of pages of forms, checks by vets, expense and delays.

It has cost M&S millions of pounds and is part of that reason the British business shut down 11 French stores in 2021.

Many smaller exporters stopped sending their goods to the EU entirely. Some produce, including sausages and certain types of seafood, were entirely stopped.

All this was the predictable consequence of former Prime Minister Boris Johnsonâs 2020 deal prioritising regulatory freedom over freeflowing goods trade.

That will end with a Brexit "reset" that Prime Minister Andy Burnham is set to sign with the EU on Friday.

The government claims it could boost the economy to the tune of Â£5bn a year and cut the cost of food for households.

But will the agreement mean a return to frictionless trade for food with the EU or are there trade-offs and quid pro quos the UK will have to make?

EU affairs minister Hamish Falconer, who is a key negotiator with the bloc, acknowledged a âchoiceâ is being made.

In exchange for being able to export produce such as Lincolnshire sausages to the 27 countries, the flipside is the extent to which the UK will be able to influence EU food policies.

The UK will have a "voice in the room", said Falconer, but no vote. Critics have said a reset will make the UK "a rule taker", not a rule maker.

But Falconer said the UK will be involved in a process to shape the rules.

A Brexit reset would not involve any upfront payments, said Falconer, but made it clear there could well be an "exit clause" in case a future UK government reversed the closer alignment.

He said this was "reasonable" to consider what would happen if a future Conservative or Reform government wanted to undo the new arrangements. Both parties have said they would if they were in power.

"The EU is worried about that and you can see why," he said.

The BBC understands that the clause is a result of EU concerns about the possible cost of having to rebuild facilities for border food safety checks which were put in place following Brexit.

The prime minister has said he believes there is a "win-win" deal to be had with the EU.

Speaking at a Marks & Spencer food court in central London, Falconer said reducing the cost of importing food "will mean less cost for supermarkets, and I hope that less cost then turns into cheaper produce on the shelves".

He said companies have to currently deal with "pages and pages" of form-filling.

Some exports are rejected for having the wrong colour of ink on forms, or an inaccurate Latin name for cooked meats.

The reset could also mean "tearing up" checks that were introduced on goods flowing from Great Britain to Northern Ireland, following Brexit, he said, as well as separate "Not for EU" labelling.

The whole deal to be signed on Friday is calculated to add 0.4% to UK GDP.

Minister for EU relations Hamish Falconer

Tom Bradshaw, president of the National Farmers' Union, welcomed reduced border inspections and paperwork but said that for the NFU it was "a moment of opportunity and risk" with farmers waiting to see what carve-outs and transitional arrangements would be included.

Trade volumes in food exports to the EU had "fallen significantly - by over a third" since 2019, he said.

But the UK should take a "pragmatic" approach to implementing any new rules.

He raised pesticides and precision breeding - or gene edited crops - as areas where he hoped some compromise would be found.

UK firms are, for example, developing tomatoes engineered to contain extra vitamin D.

Historically the EU has had much stricter rules on gene-editing, but this year relaxed regulations.

Falconer said people would have to "wait and see the detail" in this area.

Another contentious area has been crisp flavourings, with concerns raised that stricter EU rules on certain ingredients might limit the sale of some popular snack flavours in the UK.

"I want to take this opportunity to absolutely stamp down on misinformation about smoky bacon crisps," Falconer said.

Post-Brexit the UK had stayed "pretty closely aligned" to European food safety standards, he said.

Two leading snack brands had already adapted their recipes to accommodate EU rules, an example of the kind of alignment that would happen whether or not there was a reset of UK-EU relations.

"The truth is, almost every business in this country that wants to export food or drink will be aligning with EU rules because it's our biggest market.

"Lots of people, like the good people at Walkers and Frazzles have made these changes already, so I would want to reassure people, particularly smoky bacon fans, it's okay," he said.

Could smoky bacon crisps really be banned?

Ten years on, Brexit's economic impact is becoming clearer

#152. US bank services returning to normal after major outage

Thousands of customers at major US banks, including JP Morgan Chase, Wells Fargo and Bank of America, reported trouble accessing services, though Chase said its issue has now been resolved.

Chase Bank - the largest banking institution in the US - said a "software bug" in its hardware systems was behind the glitch and it was not a cyber security incident.

A spokesperson said "this was not a systemic issue" but noted the impact may have affected other banks "only to the extent that they were a party to a transaction with us".

The issue comes after a string of unrelated technical problems, including a massive Microsoft outage and a network failure that grounded all US flights.

In addition, United Airlines also reported a "widespread system outage".

The problems with the banks started on Monday afternoon. There were almost 5,000 reports of problems with Chase Bank's services, according to the Downdetector website.

Clients reported issues accessing their online accounts, viewing balances and performing transactions, like bank transfers and wire transfers, according to the BBC's US media partner CBS News, though Chase Bank told the BBC that customers still had access to their accounts and funds.

A Chase Bank customer in Texas told CBS that he could not deposit a check at a Dallas branch on Monday, reporting that he was told his transaction could not be completed. He said a bank teller told the customer in front of him that the "system was down" but did not provide additional details.

Microsoft says services fully restored after day-long global outage

Microsoft outage 'stark reminder' of how much we rely on tech

Chase Bank told its customers that "some transactions are taking longer than usual to complete" but they still had "access to their accounts and funds".

The bank, a subsidiary of JP Morgan Chase, serves more than 86m customers at 5,000 branches across the US, as well as 7.4m small businesses, according to the company's webpage. It is also the top credit card issuer in the US.

The exact nature of the bug was unclear. The BBC has contacted Chase Bank.

Bank of America customers had issues at ATMs and using mobile banking services online, while US Bank customers reported issues with their online accounts and the bank's mobile app, CBS reported.

Customers using another institution, US Bank, also reported issues transferring funds and Wells Fargo customers said they had problems signing into their accounts, getting account balances and making transactions.

US Bank said some its systems were not directly affected but clients using the Zelle, a digital payment platform, received error messages saying their payments could not be sent.

Zelle has said its service is "operating as it should" and issues at other banks could be affecting payments.

Customers also reported issues with Truist, PNC and Capital One but all these companies told the BBC they have not been impacted.

As of 18:00 EST (23:00 GMT), Chase, Bank of America and US Bank had seen a fall in the number of reported outages.

Just as the banking issue affected US customers on Monday, United Airlines confirmed a "widespread system outage", which it described as "a technology issue affecting departing flights".

The Chicago-based airline did not say what is believed to have caused the issue, but a spokesperson said it is not related to cybersecurity.

The issue did not appear to be affecting any other airlines.

The United issue is separate from the one that hit the US Federal Aviation Administration (FAA), but comes as other industries report different outages around the US.

On Friday, the FAA issued a rare nationwide a ground stop, halting all US air travel due to a telecommunication and network upgrade issues.

The halt lasted just over four hours, but the knock-on effect continues to snarl US air travel.

#151. Novo says Wegovy injection lowers BMI and obesity rates in children in trial

Novo on Monday released full results from a late-stage trial showing its blockbuster Wegovy injection helped children ages 6 to under 12 with obesity shed weight, significantly lowering their body mass index.

Wegovy reduced BMI by an estimated 19.7 percentage points on average compared with a placebo at 68 weeks, with BMI falling 17.4% among children receiving the drug and rising 2.2% among those receiving a placebo. Those results assumed patients stayed on treatment during the trial.

When evaluating all children in the study, including those who discontinued treatment, Wegovy lowered BMI by an estimated average of 16.2 percentage points more than the placebo at 68 weeks. All 167 kids in the study received lifestyle modifications throughout the trial, including a ​reduced-calorie diet and increased physical activity.

The detailed results mark an encouraging step forward in treating childhood obesity — a chronic disease that can carry serious cardiovascular, metabolic and psychosocial consequences but has had few treatment options for younger children. When left unaddressed, childhood obesity can extend into adulthood, bringing earlier and more severe health complications.

"This is a very significant disease burden in this very young patient population," said Dr. Andrea Traina, Novo's medical director lead of obesity.

In 2025, the disease affected an estimated roughly 177 million children and teens ages 5 to 19 worldwide, according to the World Obesity Federation. That figure is projected to rise to 228 million by 2040.

While Wegovy is already approved to treat obesity in children ages 12 and older, there are currently no approved medications for those under 12. The latest results offer evidence that Wegovy can potentially fill that treatment gap, safely and effectively reducing the severity of the disease in children as young as 6.

In Novo's trial, 85% of children started with moderate to severe obesity, which corresponds to BMI thresholds of 35 and above and 40 or higher, respectively. Nearly half already had at least one obesity-related complication, such as high blood pressure, high cholesterol or sleep apnea.

But more than 40% of children treated with Wegovy were no longer classified as having obesity after 68 weeks of the trial, assuming patients stayed on treatment. Novo initially shared that result in September.

"We are driving towards control of this chronic disease in that patient population when they are still so young and at such high risk for more advanced and accelerated disease progression because they developed it so early," Traina said.

Novo said Wegovy's overall safety and tolerability among children — how well patients managed the drug — were consistent with those seen in ​trials ⁠with adults. The company added that it observed no safety concerns related to growth or pubertal development in the kids.

Still, the two-year study does not establish whether treating obesity earlier can change its long-term trajectory, or what treatment and maintenance might look like as children grow into adulthood, Traina said.

"That is something that is still an evidence gap," she said, pointing to the potential of future research.

#149. Create more jobs for under-25s by cutting National Insurance, key report urges

Salon owner: 'We have parents offering to pay their kids' wages'

National Insurance (NI) paid by firms on under-25s should be gradually lowered to help create more jobs, a major report will urge.

There are nearly one million 16-24 year-olds not in employment, education or training (Neets) in the UK, official figures show.

Reducing NI costs for firms starting with retail and hospitality firms - which provide almost 40% of jobs for these people - will help improve their opportunities, the report will suggest.

Collette Osborne, a hairdresser who owns two salons in Nottingham, said she feels "suffocated by employment costs" including NI and while she used to employ eight apprentices a year, now she cannot afford to take on any.

Alan Milburn's report will also make recommendations on cutting the benefits bill

Her workforce has more than halved to 21 staff. Osborne told the BBC it costs "around Â£60,000 to train an apprentice before we can actually start allowing them onto customers' heads".

She said she has had parents so desperate for their children to find employment at her salons that some have even offered to pay their wages.

"I'm inundated with applications - and I mean inundated - which is heartbreaking because I can't take them on," she said.

Osborne does not feel she is getting help to offer jobs to youngsters.

"I feel like the government's foot is firmly on my neck," she said. "They promise breathing space. I feel suffocated, suffocated by employment costs."

The independent report by former Labour minister Alan Milburn has been in the works for a year and will be published on Tuesday in full.

He has already unveiled some of his proposals including:

Reducing benefits - under-25s should no longer be able to claim the health element of UK-wide Universal Credit or personal independence payment (Pip), which is the main disability benefit in England and Wales

A legal guarantee of a job, training or employment support with those who refuse to take up potentially losing their benefits.

Doubling the threshold at which businesses start paying NI for 21 to 24-year-olds from Â£5,000 to Â£10,000.

At the moment, businesses pay no employer NI contributions for workers younger than 21 or for apprentices under 25 â unless they earn above a threshold of Â£50,270.

However, for non-apprentices aged 21-24, employers pay 15% on annual earnings above Â£5,000.

This has been the situation since April 2025, after the then Chancellor Rachel Reeves increased the rate from 13.8% and lowered the threshold from Â£9,100.

Under Sir Keir Starmer, the government raised NI for firms but Milburn will say the Neets issue was not started by the increase.

Immediately axing NI for 21 to 24-year-olds would cost Â£5.1bn, according to research by the Resolution Foundation, a left-leaning think tank.

Milburn is set to say that an immediate across-the-board cut in employer NI is not the best use of several billions of pounds of public money.

But shadow work and pensions secretary, Helen Whately, said the government should "just stop doing the taxing".

Milburn told the BBC's Laura Kuenssberg programme he supported the government subsidising wages to encourage businesses to take on young workers.

"Because young people today are walking into the workplace often with a mental health problem, ADHD or anxiety, if you're a small business that's a cost that you've got to absorb," he said.

"The state has a responsibility to help them with that."

On Sunday, Work and Pensions Secretary Pat McFadden said the government would extend its subsidised jobs guarantee scheme for 18 to 24-year-olds who are claiming Universal Credit.

People who have been on benefits for 12 months will be eligible, down from 18 months and the scheme would be available for people in England, Scotland and Wales.

McFadden said the scheme, which guarantees workers a six-month job, "will help more than 90,000 young people into work by March 2029".

Additional reporting by Ollie Smith and Anuk Weerawardana
