#147. Nvidia dismisses 'Bring Jensen Back' campaign as Trump backs effort to reinstate ex boss

Donald Trump greeting Jensen Huang in September this year

Nvidia has dismissed a campaign calling for the company's co-founder Jensen Huang to be handed back his job as allies, including US President Donald Trump, supported the effort.

Trump said on Monday that he "would certainly not mind" if Huang was reinstated, joining a list of supporters including SpaceX's Elon Musk who are backing a "Bring Jensen Back" campaign.

But the BBC has been told the board of the AI chip giant is not interested in reinstating him after he resigned last week following revelations he had two affairs, including one that involved a suspected Chinese spy.

The same day, the White House also said it will further restrict China's access to AI chips from firms like Nvidia.

The reported affairs created a crisis for one of the world's most valuable firms - a central company in AI and the US battle against China for dominance.

Since Huang's departure, more than 175,000 people have signed an open letter calling for the board to reinstate the tech billionaire while an ongoing internal probe is conducted.

Major tech bosses, including Musk and Tim Sweeney, chief executive of Fortnite-maker Epic Games, have also supported Huang's return.

The rise and fall of Jensen Huang - how the tech superstar became mired in scandal

The "Stand with Jensen" campaign claims to have been launched by current and former Nvidia employees, partners, shareholders and supporters, all demanding the return of the former chief executive.

They expressed concerns the firm's co-founder "was removed without meaningful due process".

Trump gave his views on Huang's future on Monday, saying he "would certainly not mind" seeing the former boss given his job back.

Huang had been expected to weather the storm following the revelation of the affair but resigned last Thursday after a second extra-marital relationship emerged, allegedly with an executive at Nvidia who reported to him.

The scandal-hit tech boss was discussing a possible return to Nvidia with confidants, according to the Financial Times, external, though Nvidia's board is not considering reappointing him.

A representative for Huang declined to comment on the report.

The so-called war room also wants to get support for Huang from the White House.

Trump has spoken in glowing terms about Huang since the scandal erupted, and also said he believed Huang when the former Nvidia boss told him he had "no idea she was a spy".

Trump's view is at odds with his Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent, who has called for Nvidia to hire its next chief executive from outside the company.

The scandal has created tensions between the US Treasury and Commerce departments over shipments of high-end Nvidia chips to Alice Huang's former firm, Megaspeed. China has denied that Alice Huang is a spy.

It has raised questions about the possibility that the chips may have ended up in China despite US export restrictions.

The Nvidia scandal has sent shockwaves through Silicon Valley

The Trump administration on Monday also issued new restrictions on chip exports to overseas data centres, which have been allegedly used as a way for Chinese firms to gain access to advanced chips to train AI models.

The move comes ahead of a meeting this week between Trump and Chinese leader Xi Jinping, whose countries are locked in a race to lead AI development.

The interim rules will require companies in Singapore, Thailand, Malaysia, Indonesia and Brazil to apply for export licences for the most advanced chips in an effort to prevent them from being illegally diverted to China.

The measure is designed to ensure existing restrictions on sensitive US technology "cannot be circumvented through remote computing arrangements involving data centers in third countries," the Department of Commerce said.

Huang has been a vocal advocate for easing chip export restrictions on China, arguing that broad access to global markets is crucial for the competitiveness of the US.

He said in 2025 that the market for AI chips in China would eventually reach $50bn - a sum Huang said "is like Boeing - not a plane - the entire company".

In 2025, Nvidia won an approval to sell some advanced AI chips in China after Huang's repeated lobbying efforts in Washington.

How could the Nvidia CEOâs affair threaten US national security?

How a sex scandal at Nvidia is putting pressure on the White House

The ex-Nvidia boss' affair with Alice Huang, the former director of a Singaporean data centre firm called Megaspeed, has raised the level of scrutiny over deals abroad.

The small South East Asian business hub has been a draw for companies all around the world, many of which have set up headquarters in Singapore.

Major US firms like Anthropic, OpenAI and Google have set up offices in the business-friendly country, alongside Chinese companies like TikTok-owner ByteDance and Tencent.

But Singapore's openness to business has also made it the ideal address to place orders for restricted advanced semiconductors.

Singapore was the second-largest billing centre globally last year for Nvidia, accounting for nearly a fifth of what the chip giant earns.

But notably, only 2% of its earnings were for chips that actually entered Singapore, which acts as a hub for many Asian companies to place orders and send them elsewhere.

Watch: Who is suspected Chinese spy at centre of Nvidia affair investigation?

While Singapore does not necessarily prohibit the transfer of advanced AI chips to companies abroad, such moves can breach US export controls.

Advanced semiconductors, crucial for the development of AI models, are at the centre of the US and China's technological rivalry.

US officials have long viewed the country's technological supremacy as a matter of national security and have expressed concerns that sharing the best chips could give China's economy and military a boost.

Huang has agreed to testify in Congress, including before the House Select Committee on China to discuss whether US national security was compromised by his affair.

Trump said he did not think Huang had put national security at risk. "You know, China plays games with us. So we play games with China," he said.

The FBI is also investigating the affair between the former Nvidia boss and Alice Huang, according to Trump.

Musk claimed that "critical deals" involving Nvidia's technology would be lost following Huang's resignation.

"Jensen drove supply chain brilliantly at Nvidia but many of those deals were a handshake with Jensen, not a written contract," he posted on X., external

"Without Jensen, at least some of those critical deals will be lost."

Fellow tech boss Sam Altman, the chief executive of OpenAI, sidestepped the question of Huang returning to Nvidia at a conference, saying: "I don't think I know enough to answer that question."

But Altman, who himself was reinstated to his job after being fired back in 2023, said Huang was a "hero of mine".

Shareholders in Nvidia also want Huang back at the company's helm.

Hundreds of billions of pounds have been wiped off the firm's stock market value in recent days, but it is still worth some $3.87 trillion. Its share price was down in early Monday trading.

In an open letter to the board, Brad Gerstner, founder of Altimeter Capital and Atreides Management boss Gavin Baker called for management to leave "all options on the table, including the potential return of Jensen Huang".

Lord Sarfraz, a member of the UK's House of Lords, has also written to Nvidia's new chair, Harvey Jones, urging the firm's directors to give "serious consideration" to Huang's return following the investigation.

For the moment, Nvidia's chief financial officer Colette Kress is leading the firm and will present the company's results on Tuesday.

Suspected China spy linked to Nvidia's ex-boss left in 2025, Megaspeed says

Ex-Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang summoned to testify before Congress about affair with alleged Chinese spy

#144. OpenAI sued by California over Hugging Face attack

California Attorney General Rob Bonta sued OpenAI on Monday, alleging the ChatGPT-maker misled and endangered the public by developing artificial intelligence (AI) models without proper safeguards.

In the lawsuit, Bonta cited the July 2026 attack on the AI platform Hugging Face by experimental OpenAI agents which accessed the internet to breach that company's internal systems.

Bonta claimed OpenAI created a public nuisance and violated the state's unfair competition laws.

OpenAI spokesperson Drew Pusateri told the BBC in a statement that Bonta's lawsuit "appears to single out OpenAI over a challenge that's been identified by every major AI lab".

Other major AI developers including Anthropic, Meta, and Google have also said their models have staged similar attacks.

But Bonta's lawsuit, filed in San Francisco, where the company is headquartered, appears to be the first such suit filed by a US state.

Warning shot or publicity stunt - how worried should we be about the OpenAI hack?

OpenAI says its rogue AI tried to hack other companies

Hundreds of OpenAI agents coordinated the Hugging Face attack over two days even though they were supposed to be contained in a safe testing environment.

The incident drew added scrutiny because the agents went as far as attempting to cover their tracks.

Bonta announced an investigation into the incident in September, and served an investigative subpoena on OpenAI as part of its probe.

"The company knowingly tested a dangerous product without proper oversight or safety procedures and misled the public about it - this disregard for public safety violated California law," Bonta said in a statement on Monday.

OpenAI said it has been transparent and responsive to the Attorney General's office.

"We take misalignment seriously, and we have engaged - and will continue to engage - constructively with the Attorney General's office to share information and advance strong, practical safety standards for the entire industry," the company said.

The company added that, since the Hugging Face incident, it has worked to understand and address "misaligned model activity" which industry figures often refer to as rogue agents.

Nathan Calvin, chief strategy officer at AI safety campaign group Encode AI, said the lawsuit "recognises something we at Encode have long raised alarms about".

"OpenAI and other companies developing frontier AI systems have safety and security practices that are insufficient to protect the public from risks that could be catastrophic," he said.

Unexpected chat between OpenAI agents led to Hugging Face hack

#142. Government must act now or lose Royal Mail, former minister warns

Former Business Secretary Peter Kyle has said the government must act urgently to "level the playing field" for Royal Mail or risk the business becoming unviable.

He said he was concerned that the firm was being "undercut" by rival parcel delivery firms who did not employ staff under the same conditions.

Royal Mail, which is owned by International Distribution Services, has struggled in recent years with increased competition, fewer letters being sent and fines from the regulator for missing delivery targets.

A government spokesperson said it would consider whether further reforms are required to the parcel market after the watchdog, Ofcom, publishes its annual monitoring report in December.

Kyle had started to prepare officials at the Department for Business and Trade (DBT) to look at the delivery market for parcels shortly before he was replaced in his job by Jonathan Reynolds as business secretary in July.

He said a "crunch point" had been reached where the government had to make a decision about whether it wants Royal Mail "to be a viable proposition in the future".

He said his review would have looked at where the costs in that market lay and where the profit was being made, to see successful companies upheld "the values of business in Britain today".

"If we leave it another few years then Royal Mail will just become unviable and it will just collapse under the weight of having full-time employees," he said.

According to Royal Mail, 98% of its 130,000 workers are permanent employees, with rights to benefits including pensions, sick pay and holiday pay.

Some other parcel delivery companies use self-employed delivery drivers, with some offering an opt-in service that gives couriers limited additional benefits.

Royal Mail is also legally obliged to offer customers a "one price goes anywhere" service across the UK and to deliver first class letters six days a week to every address.

Kyle's proposed review would not have considered further changes to Royal Mail's universal service obligations or to terms and conditions for its staff.

Former Business Secretary Peter Kyle had planned to launch a review of the parcel delivery market

Royal Mail's performance has improved in recent months but it is still missing its targets for delivering first and second class post.

The firm was fined Â£21m in October 2025 for missing delivery targets in 2024/25.

Ofcom agreed last year to scrap second class deliveries on Saturdays and to reduce the target for delivering first class post within a day of collection to 90%.

A government spokesperson said the current business secretary had met Royal Mail and the Communication Workers Union, which represents postal workers, last week "to seek timely progress on improving their quality of service and to reiterate our commitment to the company's success."

DBT has also launched a consultation on tackling, external what it calls "bogus" self-employment, which it describes as "a practice whereby some businesses mislabel staff as self-employed to avoid statutory obligations".

Royal Mail has welcomed the consultation, and a spokesperson for the company claimed some parts of the delivery industry were "driving a race to the bottom" on employment standards and said it would "continue to push for a level playing field, with strong and enforceable standards across the industry."

Royal Mail misses delivery targets again but hails 'encouraging' signs

Hundreds of Royal Mail staff quitting each week under new efficiency plans

Many MPs across the UK have reported receiving high volumes of complaints from constituents about missed and delayed deliveries.

Shadow business minister Bradley Thomas said that Royal Mail's management needed to address the business's failings.

"Customers are being asked time and time again to pay increased prices, yet the service they're receiving is not improving," he said.

"Be in no doubt, this is not the fault of the hardworking postmen and women, it's the fault of Royal Mail's management.

"Nationalisation isn't the solution to this problem. Instead, we need ministers and Ofcom to bear down on Royal Mail's management to ensure they deliver on the improvements they've promised their customers."

The Communication Workers Union, which represents Royal Mail workers, is also putting pressure on ministers, with General Secretary Dave Ward calling for a "new model of ownership".

#140. 'We slept here': Flight delays linger as airlines recover from ground stop, but schedule improves Monday

U.S. flights delays eased on Monday as airlines scrambled to get back on track after the Federal Aviation Administration grounded departures nationwide for more than four hours on Friday.

Nearly 300 U.S. flights were canceled and close to 1,000 were delayed Monday, a fraction of Sunday's disruptions, according to FlightAware data.

Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy over the weekend tied the issue that prompted officials to call for the ground stop to a router in southern Arkansas and work to update copper wires to a fiber-optic system overseen by L3Harris Technologies. FAA Administrator Bryan Bedford said the upgrades were part of "routine" maintenance to switch over to fiber optic.

Officials ordered the ground stop before 4 p.m. ET on Friday and it lasted until after 8 p.m. ET, marking the longest halt to U.S. flight departures since 9/11.

Hundreds of thousands of travelers were stranded and about 18,000 U.S. flights were canceled from Friday through Monday morning, according to FlightAware.

Over that period, American Airlines had the most cancellations with 5,367 flights, close to 22% of its schedule, followed by Delta Air Lines with 3,434 cancellations, or 17% of its schedule, according to aviation data firm Cirium. Southwest Airlines and United Airlines each canceled more than 16% of their schedules. Bad weather on Thursday and Friday had also contributed to disruptions before the ground stop.

On Sunday, disruptions lingered but were starting to improve.

"We slept here," said Beth Martin, who was trying to travel home to Birmingham, Alabama, from New York's LaGuardia Airport on Sunday at the end of her daughter's birthday trip. The two were booked for Saturday night, and when delay announcements started rolling in from Delta, they decided to make good use of the extra time: After strolling through Central Park, they went window shopping on Fifth Avenue.

But when the flight was canceled, the standby list for a backup flight looked grim and their original midtown hotel was charging about $700 for an additional night, the two slept at LaGuardia on Saturday. She said they found wheelchairs to sleep in outside security.

Airlines waived change fees and fare differences and offered pilots and flight attendants extra pay to ease the delays.

Customers told CNBC they were booked on several flights before getting on board.

Justine Fritz, who works in sales for a consumer goods company, was in New York for a business event on Friday. She tried five different flights before getting on one to Houston, then flew four hours out of her way to get back to Minneapolis after her Saturday flight was canceled. Fritz said she had so many boarding passes that the Transportation Security Administration wouldn't let her through security at first.

"It's been bananas," she told CNBC at the gate. Fritz said she made the best of it by shopping and getting a haircut, but she's still disappointed.

"I'm missing the first hour of the Vikings game," she said.

Martin said her trip home with her daughter will take even longer. Delta booked them to Memphis, and her husband will drive the four hours from Birmingham to pick them up.

Officials are investigating the root cause that led to what the FAA described as "instability" in data feeds. Duffy said Sunday that two routers, the original one detected as a problem in southern Arkansas, and another in Amarillo, Texas, are in an L3Harris lab in Florida.

"We are supporting the FAA in its investigation to understand the conditions that led to the Nov. 13 ground stop of our nation's air travel," L3Harris said in a statement Saturday afternoon.

President Donald Trump said Monday he didn't "take any responsibility" for the ground stop.

Shortly after the ground stop, Duffy said he was asking Congress for as much as $50 billion for upgrades to the aging U.S. air traffic control system. A day later, Democratic lawmakers pushed back, citing unspent money from existing funding, which includes Congress' approval last year of $12.5 billion for airport and air traffic control improvements over five years.

"Our committee, on behalf of the flying public, will hold the administration accountable for what happened to make sure this chaos does not happen again," said Reps. Rick Larsen, D.-Wash., ranking member of the House Committee on Transportation and Infrastructure, and Andre Carson, D.-Ind., ranking member of the aviation subcommittee, in a joint statement.

#139. Lisa Cook lawyer signals Fed gov won't attend Trump committee's hearing

Federal Reserve Governor Lisa Cook's attorney on Tuesday gave the strongest signal yet that his client will not attend a hearing at the White House with a "committee of inquiry" investigating mortgage fraud allegations against Cook.

The committee established last month by President Donald Trump, who has previously tried to remove Cook from the central bank's board, is pretextual and poses a threat to the Fed, attorney Abbe Lowell wrote in a letter to presidential counsel Will Scharf.

"Governor Cook has no legal obligation to participate in an unfair hearing — and indeed, has a duty to the Federal Reserve to decline to participate in any proceeding that would undermine the institution's unique status and independence," Lowell wrote in the letter, which is dated Monday and was made public Tuesday.

The latest correspondence came nearly two weeks after the White House committee delayed its proposed hearing with Cook to Dec. 14 from Nov. 5. Lowell said that any hearing with Cook could not take place until at least December because of conflicts with her Fed duties.

While Lowell had raised strong concerns from the outset about the legitimacy of the committee, he initially said Cook "welcomes the opportunity to present the facts so she can clear her name."

But he has continued to cast doubts on the both the committee's purpose and its composition of three executive branch officials, including National Economic Council Director Kevin Hassett.

Lowell's tone sounded more hostile than ever in this week's letter, the first known to be sent by either party in the matter since the Republican Party's devastating losses in the midterm elections on Nov. 3.

He accused Scharf and the committee of seeking "only to manufacture 'cause' for a predetermined removal outcome."

Lowell highlighted two top concerns. First, he asserted that Hassett "is not competent to serve on any factfinding committee" because his prior comments about Cook "demonstrate that he has prejudged the issue."

Second, Lowell called it "fundamentally unfair" that the committee is demanding Cook hand over documents "immediately," while pledging to provide the governor with its own records just seven days before the hearing.

"Your and the Committee's failure to address these legitimate issues, over multiple weeks of back-and-forth correspondence, reinforces my prior observation: President Trump's decision to fire Governor Cook has already been made," Lowell wrote.

"Consequently, this Committee process appears to be a façade designed to obscure a predetermined decision to fire Governor Cook or to unreasonably manufacture a supposed 'adverse inference' if she does not participate in a process that does not comport with the requirements of a fair hearing," the lawyer wrote.

Lowell told Scharf that Cook would still be prepared to appear for a hearing if the White House "implements fair procedures." But the "ultimatums" Scharf gave in his last reply in early November "do not provide a productive path forward to enabling that participation," Lowell wrote.

The White House did not immediately respond to CNBC's request for comment on Lowell's letter.

Trump, who frequently urges the Fed to lower interest rates, has long sought to fire Cook, an appointee of former President Joe Biden.

Cook, the first Black woman to serve on the Fed's Board of Governors, last voted to cut rates in December 2025, siding with the majority. The Fed unanimously voted to raise rates after its most recent meeting in September.

Trump officials sent a criminal referral last year alleging Cook may have committed mortgage fraud by falsely claiming different properties as her primary residence in order to get more favorable terms. Cook adamantly denied wrongdoing, and, after Trump tried to fire her in August 2025, filed a lawsuit to block her removal.

The Supreme Court in June prevented Trump from firing Cook, but gave him a path to try again if additional legal steps were taken. In August, the White House told Cook it was reviving its efforts to fire her on mortgage-related grounds. In October, Trump signed a presidential memo establishing the committee of inquiry to investigate allegations that Cook lied on records related to "one or more mortgage instruments."

#138. US government confirms investigation into tech firm Megaspeed

The United States has confirmed it is investigating computing company Megaspeed, suspecting it of violating US export controls by helping to ship tech giant Nvidia's chips to China.

In a joint statement, the US Department of Justice and Department of Commerce said they had been investigating Singapore-based firm Megaspeed since 2024.

It comes after questions were raised about the possibility high-end AI chips may have ended up in China despite US export restrictions.

Concerns emerged in recent weeks after Nvidia founder Jensen Huang revealed he had an extramarital affair, allegedly with former Megaspeed executive Alice Huang. US authorities suspect her of spying for Beijing, which China denies.

Jensen Huang resigned from his role as chief executive last week following revelations he had two affairs.

Megaspeed is the biggest buyer of Nvidia chips in South East Asia and is suspected of helping divert advanced chips to China. The Singapore-based company said on Sunday it had launched an internal investigation into its business.

The justice and commerce departments said on Monday that "it is in the public interest to provide the facts and address inaccurate information" regarding the investigation.

Laying out a timeline, it said it began investigating Megaspeed in 2024 "for potential export control violations".

In May 2025, Department of Commerce investigators requested records from Nvidia "concerning direct or indirect sales to Megaspeed". In June 2025, Nvidia placed Megaspeed on its 'no sell' list.

Agents from the Bureau of Industry and Security, in conjunction with Singaporean law enforcement officials, then conducted an on-site check at Megaspeed that month and interviewed Alice Huang, who was chief executive at the time.

Alice Huang and Jensen Huang pictured together in Taiwan in June 2024

Following this enforcement action, the Department of Justice took the lead role in the criminal investigation of Megaspeed.

The Nvidia scandal has created tensions between the US Treasury and Commerce departments over shipments of high-end chips to Megaspeed.

On Friday, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent blamed the US Commerce Department, led by Howard Lutnick, for failing to properly oversee the export of the chips.

Lutnick has been called to testify before the Senate Banking Committee next month over his department's role in allowing Nvidia to export its advanced chips.

While Singapore does not prohibit the transfer of advanced AI chips to companies abroad, such moves can breach US export controls.

Huang has agreed to testify in Congress, including before the House Select Committee on China to discuss whether US national security was compromised by his affair.

The White House said on Monday it would further restrict China's access to AI chips from firms like Nvidia.

The reported affairs by Jensen Huang created a crisis for one of the world's most valuable firms - a major company in the AI industry and central to the US as it competes with China for dominance.

Trump downplays warnings of AI risks, citing rivalry with China

In the statement, the US government said it would "bring the full extent of the law against Megaspeed for any criminal conduct that may have occurred".

They added: "Sensitive technologies like semiconductors are among the most valuable and strategic assets of the US economy, and the Department of Justice and the Department of Commerce will remain vigilant in pursuing justice for those who want to compromise America's technological edge."

#136. How new US chip restrictions on South East Asia will hit China

Xi and Trump when they met in Beijing in May

As US President Donald Trump heads to China, his administration has renewed efforts to curb Beijing's access to advanced semiconductors.

On Monday, Washington announced new interim rules that will increase scrutiny of data centre operators in Singapore, Malaysia, Thailand, Indonesia and Brazil.

The move not only restricts the export of advanced chips, but also access to their computing power, which Chinese firms currently rent abroad to train artificial intelligence (AI) models.

South East Asian countries in particular have invested heavily in data centres to power their AI ambitions - and Chinese firms seeking computing power overseas have been a key market.

But now these firms will need a US government licence to buy advanced chips from Nvidia or other American chipmakers. This allows Washington to monitor if those chips are being used to serve Chinese companies, or being smuggled to China.

This poses a fresh challenge for Beijing, which has become deeply dependent on compute from abroad in the wake of US export restrictions. At least 30% of China's advanced AI computing power is projected to come from renting overseas or smuggling in 2027, according to research by the Institute for Progress, external, a US-based think-tank.

China's foreign ministry called the new rules "economic coercion" and said Beijing "will take necessary measures to firmly safeguard its own legitimate rights and interests".

This is also a problem for South East Asian economies where large numbers of data centres have sprung up recently. Some of the firms behind them are accused of helping China evade US export controls on advanced chips.

One of those is Singaporean data centre firm Megaspeed, whose former director Alice Huang is suspected of being a Chinese spy. Calls for tighter export controls have grown after Nvidia's Jensen Huang admited to an affair with her and resigned as CEO.

Megaspeed, the biggest buyer of Nvidia's chips in South East Asia, is accused of illegally smuggling chips to Chinese companies after purchasing them for use in data centres in Malaysia, Indonesia and Thailand.

The company has denied these allegations, defended its business as "legitimate and lawful", and said it has "retained external legal counsel to conduct a thorough internal investigation". It also said Alice Huang stepped down from her role as director in late 2023 and left the firm last year.

The US Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security, however, has said Brazil and the four South East Asian nations "present a higher-than-normal risk of diversion" of the chips that Washington wants to keep out of China's hands.

The new measures are aimed at ensuring sensitive US technology "cannot be circumvented through remote computing arrangements involving data centers in third countries," the bureau added. Licences will be issued on a "case-by-case basis", it said.

Batam in Indonesia is emerging as a major regional data centre hub

Countries like Malaysia and Thailand whose data centre roll out has been heavily driven by Chinese buyers could be hit hardest, says international trade expert Naoise McDonagh from Australia's Edith Cowan University.

But the new rules are unlikely to derail the data centre boom as a whole, says Reema Bhattacharya from advisory firm Verisk Maplecroft, adding that investment backed by major tech firms and clearly identifiable customers should "remain relatively resilient".

Singapore was an early adopter of data centres but when it began limiting new construction, investment flowed to neighbours Malaysia and Thailand. Johor in Malaysia and Batam in Indonesia have also approved billions of dollars worth of data centres to catch the spillover demand.

These countries have been offering incentives such as cheaper land to attract foreign investment, including from Chinese tech giants Alibaba and Tencent.

But now, the region could find it harder to get hold of advanced chips.

The BBC has contacted Indonesia, Malaysia and Thailand for comment.

Singapore said it is "engaging relevant US authorities" to understand the new rules better, and is "exploring how to facilitate trusted access" for local firms.

A spokesperson for Brazil's Ministry of Development, Industry, Trade and Services said it is aware of the US announcement and it "is closely monitoring any potential developments regarding this measure".

Malaysia previously said it welcomes data centre investment but "will not allow itself to be used to get around export controls".

The new rules also come just ahead of Trump's meeting with Chinese leader Xi Jinping in Shenzhen this week.

Beijing has made self-sufficiency in tech a national priority and needs the best chips to build powerful AI models.

Chinese firms DeepSeek and MoonShot have come close to US rivals like Anthropic and OpenAI. So much so that The Institute for Progress estimates top US AI models lead the best Chinese platforms by only about six and half months.

"This is despite US AI companies having significantly better compute access and financing," it said.

Still, China needs advanced US chips to ensure its best AI models are competitive, experts tell the BBC.

The new restrictions on South East Asia and Brazil have taken away "one of the more practical routes Chinese AI developers had to accessing frontier computing power," Bhattacharya said.

But tighter restrictions could also push Chinese firms towards domestic semiconductors and further drive advancement at home.

That has been a longstanding dilemma for Washington, McDonagh said: whether to keep selling chips to China to retain its reliance on the US, or restrict access and risk further spurring Chinese innovation.

#134. Robert Pattinson cast as next James Bond

Robert Pattinson has been cast as the next James Bond in the upcoming film from director Denis Villeneuve. It will be the 26th James Bond film and the first since Amazon MGM took over the iconic franchise.

Pattinson will be the seventh actor to play the role. Monday's announcement — which came in part via an Instagram post — ends a long period of speculation about who would take over as "007" after Daniel Craig.

"James Bond is one of the most legendary characters in cinema, and to be entrusted with 007 is the honour of a lifetime," Pattinson said in a statement. "I couldn't be more excited about this partnership and the work ahead with this fantastic team."

Pattinson has appeared in several big-ticket films this year, including Christopher Nolan's "The Odyssey" and psychological thriller "Primetime," and he'll soon appear in "Dune: Part Three," also directed by Villeneuve. Pattinson, who rose to fame with the Twilight movie series, is also in production for Matt Reeves' "The Batman Part II," a follow-up to the 2022 film based on the DC comic.

The upcoming Bond film will have a script by Steven Knight and will be produced by Amy Pascal, known for her work on the Spider-Man franchise, and David Heyman, who produced all eight Harry Potter films.

"We were surrounded by extraordinary talent, but there can only be one 007," Villeneuve said. "I am overjoyed to welcome Robert Pattinson as our new James Bond. He has the timeless soul of 007 and brings a spirit that is completely new. I cannot wait to begin."

In 2022, Amazon bought MGM Studios, which had long produced the Bond movies, for roughly $8.45 billion. Amazon MGM then acquired creative control of the James Bond intellectual property rights in 2025, when it closed a deal with longtime producers Barbara Broccoli and Michael G. Wilson.

The film is still untitled, and Amazon MGM hasn't announced a projected release date.

#133. Investor Steve Eisman says the AI neocloud stocks are in 'big trouble' from Nvidia fallout

Investor Steve Eisman said the so-called AI neocloud stocks will be the biggest losers from the unfolding Nvidia scandal.

Nvidia was essentially a "virtual vertical holding company and it's unwinding," said Eisman of "Big Short" fame on CNBC's "Squawk Box." "I think neoclouds are all in big trouble though because they are part of that vertical holding company and I think they're about to get ejected."

Neoclouds, which used borrowed funds to build data centers, have been among the hardest-hit stocks during the recent pullback in the artificial intelligence trade. CoreWeave and Nebius are each down more than 50% the last three months.

All eyes on Wall Street are on chipmaker Nvidia as it reports earnings on Tuesday after the bell while grappling with a potential Chinese espionage scandal. Founder and CEO Jensen Huang resigned on Thursday under a cloud of controversary revolving around his affair with a suspected Chinese spy. The Commerce Department has subsequently moved to close a loophole on China's remote use of data centers.

Investors are worried as scrutiny increases on Nvidia's China revenue, it may pull back on its extensive financing that supported the AI industry.

Frontier algorithm company Anthropic is expected to make its public debut on Thursday as concerns about the company's profitability potential linger amid the rapid and ongoing commodification of AI.

Eisman said he doesn't think the much-ballyhooed offering is very important anymore to the AI trade because expectations have come down.

"I don't think anybody really cares," the investor said.

Instead, Eisman is focusing on how much Democrats will restrict data center building when they take control of Congress and various governorships in January.

"I think the biggest issues by far are how much are, how much are local and state governments going to slow AI data center construction? Is it going to be 100% moratorium? That would obviously be — that would put the country into recession overnight. Or is it something like 10% to 30%?" he said.

In the event of a 10% to 30% slowdown in data center construction, Eisman said the group of companies within the AI trade that he favors the most are the hyperscalers.

"The group that makes out the best are the hyperscalers because they go cash flow positive again," he said.

Correction: Nvidia founder and CEO Jensen Huang resigned on Thursday. An earlier version misstated the day.

#132. We couldn't afford a house alone, so we bought one with strangers

The Pullman family, on the left, and the Wilcox family, bought a home together soon after meeting

When Elizabeth and Tom Wilcox realised they could never afford to buy the home they wanted for themselves and their two children, they decided to take a leap of faith.

They'd find another couple with young kids, complete strangers to them, and propose that they go halves on a property and all move in together.

Based in Vancouver, which has long had some of the highest house prices in Canada, the Wilcoxes were desperate to buy a home that had a back garden for their children to play in.

"We wanted a house with a yard that could fit a trampoline, in a walkable neighbourhood, but we couldn't afford it," says Elizabeth, a project manager in the healthcare sector.

With a budget of C$1m ($700,000; Â£500,000) all they could hope for was a small apartment with a balcony at best.

That was when Elizabeth came up with the idea of buying with another family.

They placed an advert in a local estate agent's e-newsletter, but it took two years before they finally met a family whom they clicked with â Ashley Pullman and her husband and two children.

In 2023, the Wilcoxes and the Pullmans co-bought a six-bedroom, detached house, complete with a back garden, for C$1.85m.

The two families split the deposit of C$250,000, and continue to go halves on the monthly mortgage payments of C$3,900. They also divide bills and other expenses equally, such as any necessary home repairs.

Elizabeth says the arrangement has saved both families "tens of thousands of dollars".

Mums Ashley Pullman, left, and Elizabeth Wilcox say the two families get along

While there is no official data available for the number of people buying homes with people they previously didn't know, Parimal Gosai says he has seen a big increase in interest in Toronto as a response to high property prices.

Gosai is the founder and CEO of Husmates, an app and website that connects people in Canada's largest city who are looking to potentially buy a home with a stranger.

It can be seen as "a financial relationship, and that takes emotions out of it", he says.

It's unclear how common this phenomenon is elsewhere in the world. David Hollingworth, from British mortgage broker L&C, says while he hasn't come across it himself, he thinks it also happens in the UK, though is "very niche".

"I remember that before Covid-19 there was a matchmaking event in a London pub, like speed dating, for people looking to buy a house with a stranger," he says. "How successful this was I don' t know."

The Wilcoxes and the Pullmans pooled their resources to buy a detached Vancouver home

Three years on, the Wilcoxes and the Pullmans say their living arrangement is still working out.

While each family has some private living spaces, such as their own kitchens, they share the big basement-level living room, which includes a home gym in one corner, and a shared freezer.

"If someone says they're in the basement to work out, we know not to go there for a bit, and it's never been an issue," says Elizabeth.

Ashley agrees that there are no problems. "Maybe it's arrogance but my husband and I felt like we were the type of people who could live with strangers," she says.

Sabrina Bowman, a freelance business consultant in Toronto, has co-owned a home with two other housemates since 2018. When she signed on the dotted line and moved in she didn't know one of the other two people.

While Sabrina says she isn't comfortable sharing the exact financial details, she says they decided to divide up the deposit and mortgage payments based on each of their levels of income.

She says any household disputes can be addressed early with open discussions. "What is important to us is having structures to communicate about everything that happens in the home."

They have regular house meetings where they discuss chores, financial issues, birthday celebrations, and any disagreements.

"It's great to see people be honest about something they may not be comfortable about and for us to find a solution to that problem," Sabrina says.

Sabrina Bowman says it is important to have open communication

In the case of both Sabrina's house in Toronto and the Wilcox and Pullman property in Vancouver, they have legal agreements in place in case someone wants to sell up and move out.

For both homes, reasonable notice periods are specified, and for Sabrina's, the departing housemate is required to give those remaining the first opportunity to buy their share.

Toronto real estate lawyer Tanya Walker says such legal assurances are vital. "You need something legally binding instead of a bunch of emails and Facebook messages that can be too messy to collect if a dispute comes up," Walker says.

Back in Vancouver, Elizabeth says that neither family has a timeframe for how long they will live in the house. "All of us are really happy with the set-up and location so it would be hard to move.

"I can't put a price on how it felt to watch the kids jump on the trampoline for the first time, or run in circles on our patch of green grass."

#129. Where are the most jobs for 18-24 year-olds?

If you're one of the nearly one million people aged 16-24 not in education, employment or training - otherwise known as "Neet" - then you might be wondering where to start.

Under-25s should be guaranteed job placements or training to help them find permanent work, a major review aimed at cutting the number of Neets will say.

But how many jobs are there where you live? Use our look-up tool to find out.

Burnham wants Neets report to be watershed moment, No 10 says, as Tories criticise welfare and tax proposals

Where you live will often have a big impact on your ability to find a job.

We have used online job adverts to help identify the number of vacancies suitable for young people in the area they live, relative to the size of the local population.

The Institute for Public Policy Research (IPPR) think tank used AI to help identify the jobs, which were broadly defined as roles requiring relatively low experience or education.

According to its analysis, South Oxfordshire had 387 suitable vacancies per 1,000 18 to 24-year-olds in the area - the most of any local authority. Rotherham had the least, at 53 per 1,000.

Use this tool to check out the situation in your area. The data for London is only available for the region as a whole and is not broken down by individual councils.

Even if there are jobs available in your area, you might not find one in the sector you want to work in.

The sector with the highest average number of entry-level vacancies in the year to October was teaching, with most of those jobs being for teaching assistants, learning support and cover supervisor roles, according to jobs website Adzuna.

The next largest was hospitality and catering, traditionally an area where younger people get their first experience of work.

Adzuna, a major online jobs website, includes adverts for full-time roles marked as entry-level, those asking for up to two years' experience, graduate roles, internships and apprenticeships.

Accounting and finance had the third-highest number of job openings.

Figures on salaries for vacancies can be patchy because many employers do not include them in job adverts.

Prime Minister Andy Burnham recently urged businesses to start publishing salaries ahead of plans to make pay details compulsory in adverts.

Only about 40% of Adzuna's jobs data for the year to October stated a salary, although that figure varies widely by sector.

Here are the average salaries for jobs in the sectors with the largest number of entry-level jobs, according to Adzuna. The highest average salary is Â£30,530 in engineering, while social care has the lowest at Â£25,880.

While it can be hard for anyone looking for their first job, the situation is also tough for older jobseekers.

Across the whole economy there were 690,000 jobs available in the August to October period this year, which is the lowest number since early 2021.

The Covid pandemic led to huge swings in the number of job vacancies available as companies reacted to shutdowns and then reopened as restrictions were lifted.

Stripping out the fluctuations seen during the pandemic, the number of vacancies is now at its lowest level for 12 years.

Why Milburn's Neets reforms will only save Â£200m a year by 2036

Apprenticeships have often been a way for people to get their first taste of employment.

In England the number of people starting apprenticeships has risen in recent years, although this has been driven by an increase in those aged 25 and over after schemes for older workers were fully rolled out in 2008.

The number of people aged 24 and under starting apprenticeships in England in 2025 was 172,230, down from a peak of 291,300 in 2012.

#127. Nvidia warns chip buyers of legal risks tied to China AI access, sources tell CNBC

Nvidia warned its customers that new legislation on Chinese companies remotely accessing its artificial-intelligence chips could expose it and them to U.S. "legal action," sources told CNBC.

The chip giant is facing increasing scrutiny from U.S. lawmakers over Chinese companies' access to its advanced chips, which U.S. export controls restrict from going to China.

Nvidia messaged chip buyers in recent months to caution that the proposed Remote Access Security Act, or RASA, would give the U.S. government broader authority to control access to cloud services in the same way as physical hardware, potentially exposing both parties to legal risk, according to two people with direct knowledge of the matter. The people asked not to be named while discussing private communications.

It also said that, for now, it would not sell its chips to customers who rent more than 50% of the graphics processing units, or GPUs, to Chinese companies, the people said.

Separately, the Financial Times reported earlier this month that Nvidia verbally told data-center clients to direct at least 51% of their services to non-Chinese customers under future contracts.

Regulators have had limited powers to control companies' access to those chips through cloud services hosted overseas.

RASA passed the House of Representatives in January but has not yet passed the Senate. Remote access to the chips has provoked the ire of President Donald Trump's administration, which has sought to keep the chips out of China to maintain the U.S. lead in the AI race.

CNBC reported on Nov. 5 that Chinese lab Moonshot AI sought access to Nvidia chips through data centers in Europe. The Trump administration previously singled the company out, accusing it in July of accessing the chips through centers in East Asia.

The U.S. Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security, or BIS, announced on Monday an interim final rule designed to prevent Chinese companies' remote access to AI chips.

Nvidia and BIS did not respond to a request for comment.

On Sunday, U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent called for a "dramatic shakeup" at Nvidia after Jensen Huang's shock departure as CEO, amid reports of an extramarital affair with a woman U.S. authorities reportedly suspect of spying for China.

Bessent suggested other Nvidia executives should face scrutiny over the chip giant's compliance with export controls.

"I want to keep coming back to what was the culture at Nvidia, and were they pressing these seemingly illicit sales to goose their revenues and their earnings?" Bessent told Fox News.

In an August securities filing, Nvidia said it sought to "strictly comply with all applicable export control regulators" but did not have "physical control of our products after sale" and relied on its customers' and partners' compliance programs.

Huang has been asked to testify before the Senate Banking Committee on Dec. 3.

#125. JPMorgan Chase says temporary software bug is affecting consumer banking

JPMorgan Chase reported a "technical issue" that was affecting some consumer banking services Monday.

The bank said a "temporary issue related to a software bug in on-premise hardware systems" was causing some customer transactions to take longer than usual, though it said customers can still access their accounts and funds.

"There is no evidence it is cyber-related," a JPMorgan spokesperson said in a statement to CNBC.

"This is not a systemic issue — other banks may have been impacted by slowness in transactions only to the extent that they were a party to a transaction with us," the spokesperson said. "We're working diligently to resolve this by the end of the day today."

CNBC's Eamon Javers reported there was no indication of bad actors involved.

Still, other major banks were listed in outage reports as transactions between Chase and other institutions appeared to get held up. Users reported errors as early as 11:30 a.m. ET.

A representative for U.S. Bank said that company was "not directly impacted. However, clients attempting to send or receive Zelle payments with another financial institution may experience transaction delays."

Zelle is the peer-to-peer payments system backed by many of the largest U.S. banks, including JPMorgan.

The outage reports come days after separate, widespread issues with Microsoft Outlook and communication systems used by air traffic control centers.

— CNBC's Leslie Picker contributed to this report.

#124. We can't afford to hire under-25s, say firms. Will Milburn plans help?

Ally Fir says if a state scheme was running she'd "jump on it right away"

Lowering employers' National Insurance (NI) payments for those between 21 and 24, and cash incentives to take on some workers are among proposals put forward in Alan Milburn's report to get more 18-24 year-olds working.

Many companies have complained that recent increases in National Insurance and the minimum wage have made it more expensive to take on staff.

We talked to firms and business groups about the issues they face when hiring younger works and what they make of the proposals.

Bournemouth-based cafÃ© owner Ben Blades says he cannot justify the extra cost of training up new staff

Cutting NI costs is "right in the sweet spot of what firms need to hit the hire button", said the Recruitment and Employment Confederation (REC).

The Milburn report proposes NI thresholds could be raised over the next five years, from Â£5,000 to Â£10,000 for those aged 21 to 24, and suggests prioritising the retail and hospitality sectors.

Bournemouth-based cafÃ© owner Ben Blades says the high costs he faces currently make it difficult to employ young people with no work experience.

"My labour costs are already over 45%. We simply cannot justify the extra cost to put on an extra staff member to train someone up for three months," he says.

"In hospitality our margins have been squeezed and squeezed and squeezed"

As a result, he says it currently makes more sense to employ someone who already has experience.

Major report proposes welfare changes, apprenticeships and tax cuts to help 440,000 young people into work

At a glance: Key proposals from Milburn's Neets report

Allen Simpson, chief executive of UKHospitality, says the recommendation to raise the threshold is "the right one" and "would be an immediate shot in the arm for job creation".

But the British Chambers of Commerce (BCC) argues the Milburn report does not go far enough in tackling costs facing businesses.

It says while the NI proposal is welcome, the change should be wider than just retail and hospitality, otherwise it "risks adding complexity and blocking off potential career paths".

Other proposals put forward by the Milburn review include cash incentives to employ younger workers.

Employers who take on any young person who has been Neet - not in education, employment or training - for six months should receive a Â£3,000 payment, and small businesses - with less than 50 employees - that do so should receive an extra Â£2,000

There would also be an extra Â£2,000 payment if the young person has been in care or is disabled.

Ally Fir runs a bookshop in Westbourne, alongside her partner Matt, and thinks more state support is needed.

She says incentivised schemes for small business "would help us to employ more, especially young people. If a scheme like that was going we'd jump on it right away."

Fir says she gets job applications "every single day". She'd like to take more people on but "it is the cost of employing people that makes that impossible".

Tom Parsons has already had experience of using government schemes to employ young people at his design consultancy, Aetha, in Poole, Dorset.

The Kickstart Scheme in 2020 meant he could take on two young people in fully funded six-month job placements offering 25 hours a week.

Tom Parsons says the old Kickstart Scheme was a game-changer for those employed under it

"One of them stayed with us for five years and the other one I think about three years. And it completely changed the game for them", Tom told the BBC "so if there was a scheme like that, again, that would really help us".

He says he wants to employ young people but costs remain a huge barrier.

Where are the most jobs for 18-24 year-olds?

Why Milburn's Neets reforms will only save Â£200m a year by 2036

One of the government's flagship employment rights reforms risks weakening the hiring of young people, according to Milburn's report.

Ministers are abolishing what they call ''exploitative'' zero-hours contracts and replacing them with guaranteed hours contracts which would be based on the number of hours people normally work.

Much of the detail is yet to be finalised but the Employment Rights Act is due to be enacted next year.

Milburn is concerned that the changes could cause problems "at a time when the youth labour market is already weak'.'

The REC said companies were "particularly worried" over the guaranteed hours measure.

It added that addressing the Neets problem gave ministers "the perfect opportunity" to reconsider the issue.

Young people should have a legal right to a route into education, training or work, and would be supported to do so under an "opportunity guarantee", Milburn has proposed.

This could help firms which are struggling to recruit. Thirty-five-year-old Dean Greene, a sales director at waste management facility Williams Environmental, says it's tricky finding staff.

"We didn't get many applicants for it at all," says Greene, referencing an ad for a marketing and sales role.

"There wasn't a huge uptake from that sort of 16-to-24 range," he adds.

He thinks the lack of applicants may be because the waste industry is perceived as "mucky and dirty", but he is confident many would be interested if they knew the job was available.

Pointing to himself as an example of progressing in the industry, he tells me how he started as an admin officer scanning paperwork at 18 before remaining with the company for 17 years.

#122. Speed camera fault may have allowed drivers to escape fines

Further problems with the accuracy of speed cameras on English roads have been uncovered which may have allowed drivers to slip through the cracks and escape fines.

National Highways discovered issues with speed cameras last year, affecting 154 out of 400 cameras but, following an investigation, said it had found another fault on units monitoring roads including those running between Huntingdon and Cambridge.

The Home Office has revoked approval that allows footage from the cameras to be used as evidence in legal proceedings. US firm Verra Mobility operates the cameras but National Highways said it will now end the contract.

Roads minister Justin Madders said: "We can't take chances with this.

"If we're going to prosecute people, we need to be confident the system is working as it should. That's why we've taken the action we have."

The fault affected all of the variable speed cameras on smart motorways, and a section of the A14 between Huntingdon and Cambridge as well as the A1 approach junction to the A14.

A spokesperson for National Highways said that Verra's camera system had been selected on the basis that it maintained Home Office approval.

The agency said that since that was no longer the case "we are ending our contractual relationship to use the cameras for enforcement".

It added: "We are considering all further commercial and legal options that may be appropriate."

The BBC has contacted Verra Mobility for comment.

Madders said that the enforcement of speed limits continues to be conducted through a range of different tactics, including mobile cameras as well as marked and unmarked police patrols.

But he admitted that the camera system will not be fully fixed until sometime next year.

Rod Dennis, senior policy officer at the RAC motoring organisation, said: "Malfunctioning speed cameras on smart motorways do more than just let speeding drivers get away with breaking the law and endangering lives â they further undermine confidence in the very types of road we know drivers already have serious misgivings about."

There are 4,000 cameras across the entire road network.

In December 2025, National Highways, which runs England's motorways, blamed an "anomaly" in how variable speed cameras were interacting with speed limit signs on some A roads and motorways.

For example, on a road where the speed limit increases, a driver may see a sign showing 60mph but the camera recording it may still be working on the basis of a previous 40mph speed limit.

The fault led to 2,650 wrongful speed camera activations between 2021 and the end of last year.

Those who had been wrongfully fined have been reimbursed.

As National Highways continued to test the system, it discovered a further problem "relating to how some of these cameras may activate in specific and rare circumstances".

It said that according to its calculations, "the circumstances in which the latest issue could occur are extremely rare, and there is no evidence to suggest it has impacted enforcement".

Nevertheless, it said it was "taking immediate steps to ensure compliance is maintained at relevant locations".

It said it would out further traffic officer patrols in place to monitor the sites.

#119. United Airlines resolves system outage that affected departing flights

United Airlines on Monday said it had resolved a "widespread system outage" that briefly affected departing flights.

"We have resolved a technology issue that delayed flight departures," the airline said in a statement. "While some flights were delayed, there was no impact on flights in progress, or on United Express flights.

A United spokesperson said there was no indication that the outage was a cyberattack.

The Air Line Pilots Association, which represents pilots for United and other carriers, said in an earlier message to members seen by CNBC that the operational outage was "affecting multiple pilot facing applications" and that United could not "provide outbound flights with final weights."

United's outage came three days after a nationwide ground stop halted departures across the country for more than four hours, snarling travel and leading to widespread delays and cancellations over the weekend.

Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy blamed the issues that led to Friday's ground stop on a router in Arkansas that overwhelmed systems that feed radar and other data for air traffic control. Federal Aviation Administration Administrator Bryan Bedford said the upgrades were part of "routine" maintenance to switch over from copper wires to fiber optic.

There were significantly fewer flight delays and cancellations Monday than had been reported Sunday, according to FlightAware data.

There was no indication that United's outage Monday was related to the issues that caused Friday's ground stop.

— CNBC's Leslie Josephs and Phil LeBeau contributed to this report.

#117. Aschenbrenner’s Situational Awareness made AI bets again before October rout

Leopold Aschenbrenner's artificial intelligence-themed hedge fund Situational Awareness, which nearly collapsed in July, loaded back up on AI stocks as it returned to the financial markets at the end of the third quarter. But all of its holdings are down big the last month and a half.

According to a new regulatory filing, as the fund got back on its feet Aschenbrenner took an equity position in Australian neocloud company SharonAI valued at more than $440 million, along with equity stakes in AI-centric plays Cleanspark, IREN, Bitdeer Technologies and Fermi. The Cleanspark stake is worth almost $39 million, while Iren and Bitdeer stakes are more than $21 million and more than $20 million, respectively. Fermi, meanwhile, has a value of more than $11 million.

The fund also owned call options at the end of September in neocloud Nebius, memory chip name Sandisk, the Roundhill Memory ETF (DRAM) and data center power play Bloom Energy. Sandisk and Nebius were key holdings that almost took out his fund over the summer.

All of these Situational Awareness holdings have been routed since Sept. 30. SharonAI is at the top of that list, seeing a 58% loss in October. It's also fallen 11% so far this month. IREN was down 41% in October, while Nebius declined 35%. Last week, Nebius plummeted more than 16% in a single day after the company issued a weak full-year forecast.

It's not known whether the hedge fund manager got out of those positions during the October AI selloff. The 13F filing is just a snapshot of what the hedge fund held at the end of the quarter.

Aschenbrenner — who launched the fund in 2024 after rising to fame that same year with his AI manifesto also entitled Situational Awareness — re-entered financial markets a bit in September, sources told CNBC at the time.

The Monday filing shows he picked up right where he left off.

The near-collapse of his hedge fund over the summer forced him to sell all of its public equity positions to Ken Griffin's Citadel. The fund had $45 billion in assets at its peak, but that tumbled to around $10 billion, consisting mostly of private stakes.

Stocked tethered to the AI trade took a hit in the most recent quarter as investors grew concerned about a slowdown in development of the technology. Those fears were spurred by comments from industry leaders such as Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei and OpenAI CEO Sam Altman back in September, with the latter saying that the company going public this year would be "ill-advised."

Since then, pressure on the trade has only continued.

In recent weeks, investors have worried about how Democratic wins in the midterm elections could hinder data center development, whether some technology companies are using too much debt to finance the AI buildout and what role Nvidia will play in that financing with Jensen Huang now out as CEO at the company.

Losses in Nvidia as well as other AI-related stocks dragged down the three leading U.S. indexes during Monday's session as investors continued to assess the fallout of Huang's departure.

#113. Trump’s accounts traded up to $97.5 million in Nvidia as Huang gained White House access

President Donald Trump has had plenty of reason to cheer the rise of Nvidia.

The chipmaker helped propel the stock market that Trump is fond of touting. Its billionaire co-founder, Jensen Huang, became one of the president's favorite businessmen. And by the end of last year, Nvidia had become one of Trump's three largest individual stock holdings, worth between $12.5 million and $58 million, up from between $615,000 and $1.3 million a year earlier.

Since returning to the White House, Trump's investment accounts have bought or sold Nvidia shares at least 103 times — transactions worth a combined $27 million to $97.5 million, according to a new CNBC analysis of his annual and periodic financial disclosures, which were most recently updated through August.

By midday Monday, Trump's Nvidia stake, based on the value he reported holding in August, would be worth $4.5 million to $57.7 million with stock priced around $157. But the filings give broad dollar ranges for holdings rather than the number of shares, leaving his current stake unclear.

Now, Huang's resignation as Nvidia's CEO on Thursday, following revelations about his relationship with a woman suspected of spying for China and reports of affairs with company employees, has put those trades back in the spotlight.

Among them was a sale of $500,001 to $1 million on May 18, the first Monday after Trump returned from a state visit to China with Huang. Five days earlier, before Huang boarded Air Force One during a refueling stop in Alaska, Secretary of State Marco Rubio had briefed Trump on intelligence concerning Huang's relationship with the suspected spy, The New York Times reported.

Trump on Monday denied being briefed. Asked about the reported briefing from Rubio, Trump said, "I haven't heard that," adding that he did not know whether the woman, Alice Huang, was a Chinese spy.

The president's financial accounts also bought smaller amounts of Nvidia shares in the days immediately before and after the May 18 sale, part of a broader trading blitz. Such flurries have defined his second term: Trump disclosed more than 21,000 trades in 2025 alone, compared with roughly 500 during his entire first term, according to CNBC's analysis.

A White House spokesperson told CNBC independent advisors manage Trump's portfolio without his input. Trump Organization spokesperson Kimberly Benza said the arrangement is designed to "avoid even the appearance of a conflict of interest." The disclosures offer no evidence Trump directed the trades or that his advisors had access to classified intelligence.

Trump has long used the stock market as a scorecard for the economy, touting its record highs — sometimes when highs have not actually been reached — despite the turbulence caused by his tariffs and the Iran war.

"Look, we have the highest stock market we've ever had," Trump told ESPN commentator and political talk show host Stephen A. Smith on Thursday when pressed about Republicans' midterm losses.

Nvidia alone accounted for about 13.5% of the S&P 500's 2026 gains through September, according to an analysis by Choate Wealth that looked at how chip stocks have driven the year's performance.

Huang's rise in Trump's Washington has been nearly as striking as Nvidia's on Wall Street. The two have met or appeared together at least 15 times since Trump returned to office, according to a CNBC review of public records and news reports. Huang in June told The Associated Press that Trump even "calls me in the middle of the night."

At Trump's Jan. 20, 2025, inauguration, several Silicon Valley titans occupied prime seats, but Huang wasn't there. The two men had never met.

That changed on Jan. 31, 2025, when Huang visited the White House, capping a bruising week for Nvidia: Chinese AI startup DeepSeek had wiped $593 billion off the company's market value in a single day, according to LSEG data cited by Reuters.

Two days before the meeting, Trump's money managers bought between $1 million and $5 million in Nvidia stock.

For Huang, much of the relationship hinged on access to China. In April 2025, the administration effectively halted sales of Nvidia's H20 chips to China, prompting a $4.5 billion charge, according to an SEC filing. Huang argued that withholding U.S. chips would only accelerate China's development of its own.

The administration eventually relented. After another meeting with Trump in July 2025, Huang announced from Beijing that Nvidia expected to resume H20 sales. A week after Trump confirmed an August 2025 deal calling for 15% of the revenue from the company's chip sales to China to go to the U.S. government, his accounts also bought between $5 million and $25 million in Nvidia stock, one of several large tech investments that day.

Huang was also becoming a fixture of Trump's international diplomacy, joining him in Saudi Arabia and the U.K. Nvidia, already a $1 million donor to Trump's inaugural fund, would pledge another $25 million to his planned White House ballroom on condition of anonymity, The Washington Post first reported.

But behind the scenes, intelligence officials first briefed Trump last fall on Huang's relationship with the woman suspected of spying for China, The New York Times reported.

Trump nevertheless continued to ease restrictions on Nvidia's business in China, announcing Dec. 8 that the company could sell its more powerful H200 chips. His accounts had sold as much as $1 million in Nvidia stock four days earlier and bought more the day after.

In the first eight months of 2026, Trump's accounts made another 38 Nvidia trades worth a combined $9.4 million to $28.3 million, according to CNBC's analysis. A disclosure released Oct. 8 — the day Trump awarded Huang the National Medal of Science — showed purchases of up to $1.6 million and sales of up to $1 million in August alone.

Trump stood by Huang as Nvidia's board weighed his future last week, urging him to fight to keep his job, the Times reported.

With Huang out and Nvidia's shares tumbling, Trump described him Thursday as a "tremendous talent" who was "going through hell" — in the same interview in which he boasted of the market's record highs.

Trump is already floating a comeback for Huang, telling reporters Monday that reinstating him "certainly isn't a bad idea." But Nvidia's board is not considering his return, a person familiar with the matter told CNBC. The person asked not to be named because they were discussing internal matters."

#110. Disney beats fourth-quarter estimates, targets $3 billion in savings through cost-cutting efforts

Disney on Tuesday reported quarterly earnings and revenue that exceeded analysts' expectations and announced it's targeting $3 billion in cost savings by fiscal 2029.

Those cost-cutting efforts include several rounds of layoffs announced in recent months, Disney's chief financial officer, Hugh Johnston, told CNBC. The most recent round, which seeks to reduce costs by up to 20%, was reported earlier this month.

Future cost-saving measures will include reductions in administrative and other general expenses, Johnston said, while adding, "We do expect more layoffs."

Disney CEO Josh D'Amaro told investors on the company's earnings call that the job cuts are "not easy."

"They affect people who care deeply about Disney and have contributed so much to this company," he said. "At the same time, these steps are important if we want to be a company that moves faster, adapts to a rapidly changing environment, and keeps investing in the content and technology that will drive our growth."

The cuts will help Disney fund its $26 billion in projected content spend for fiscal 2027, a 9% increase from fiscal 2026.

The entertainment giant is also forecasting double-digit growth in earnings per share in the next fiscal year, as well as segment operating income growth of around 8%.

Disney noted that the majority of that growth will be concentrated in the first half of the year due to the theatrical debut of "Avengers: Doomsday" and a boost from the Super Bowl, which will air on Disney's ESPN and ABC networks.

"The growth is really going to come in the first half of this year," Johnston said. "That's the opposite of what we saw in '26, where the growth was backloaded."

The company will also increase its annual dividend to $2 a share, a 33% increase from the previously declared dividend. It will be payable in two installments of $1 per share, one in January and one in July.

Here's how Disney performed in its fiscal fourth quarter ended Oct. 3, compared with what Wall Street expected, according to LSEG:

Disney reported net income for the fiscal fourth quarter of $2.61 billion, or $1.51 per share, compared with $1.31 billion, or 73 cents per share, during the same period the year prior.

Adjusting for one-time items, including severance and other restructuring charges as well as the value of intangible assets, Disney reported earnings per share of $1.77.

The company's revenue for the quarter was 16% higher year over year, and results were once again lifted by its parks and streaming divisions.

Disney's experiences segment, which includes its theme parks and cruises, reported nearly $10.11 billion in revenue, up 15% year over year. Operating income for the unit increased 20% to $2.25 billion.

The division is already a top performer for the company, but it benefited in the fiscal fourth quarter because of an extra calendar week compared with the previous fiscal year.

While international attendance continues to lag at Disney's domestic parks, it is being offset by domestic attendance, especially at the Walt Disney World resort in Orlando, Florida, Johnston said.

Growth at Disneyland Paris was driven by recent investments including an expansion area called World of Frozen. Attendance at the Hong Kong and Shanghai parks remained soft because of broad economic challenges in those regions, according to Disney.

Still, D'Amaro said he believes the growth in parks will be sustainable through next year, especially with new additions, including the Monsters, Inc.-themed land "Monstropolis" opening at Walt Disney World in 2027.

"Q1 forward resort bookings at Walt Disney World are pacing up compared to the prior year," he said. "And new capacity and new experiences will start coming online in 2027. We keep investing to make the experience fresh, and what we see is that drives repeat visits."

The company expects operating income for the experiences segment to grow in the high-single digits in fiscal 2027. Growth is expected to be front-loaded as the company laps the launch of the Disney Adventure cruise ship, which set sail in March.

Over at its entertainment division, Disney saw revenue increase 17% to $11.89 billion. Operating income nearly doubled to $1.33 billion.

Revenue from streaming, specifically, rose 19% to $6.1 billion, Disney said.

The segment benefited from the extra week on the fiscal calendar as well as from growth in subscription and affiliate fees. Additionally, the company saw higher theatrical distribution revenue from titles like "Toy Story 5."

Disney said Tuesday it expects operating income for the entertainment division to grow in the high-single digits in the coming fiscal year.

The company's sports division saw revenue increase 16% in the fiscal fourth quarter, reaching $4.61 billion. Operating income for the segment surged more than 50% to $1.39 billion.

Subscription and affiliate fees fueled this growth alongside an increase in advertising revenue.

The company expects its sports division's operating income to grow by double digits in fiscal 2027. It noted that the fiscal first quarter of the year will see some declines because of the timing of College Football Playoff games and the New Year's window. However, the fiscal second quarter will benefit from the college championship as well as the broadcast of Super Bowl 61.

Disney's outlook for overall and segment results for fiscal 2027 excludes the impact of a 53rd week in fiscal 2026.

D'Amaro has made advancing technology a pillar of the company's growth strategy. In September, Disney hired Karandeep Anand in the newly created position of chief technology officer.

"Technology is central to how we connect fans to more of Disney and drive returns on our investment," D'Amaro said on the earnings call. "[Anand has] been energized by how much opportunity he sees to move faster and go further in product and technology. Done right, that means serving our fans even better, working more efficiently and being more innovative across every one of our businesses."

Disney announced a partnership with Adobe earlier this month to develop generative artificial intelligence models.

D'Amaro told investors that the team is exploring ways to use AI to make booking trips to Disney parks easier.

"There's actually a vacation planning tool that's in beta right now at Walt Disney World. We built that with our partners at Google, and I've had a chance to experiment with it myself," he said. "It'll be incredibly helpful for our guests who are booking trips. With these AI features, it can feel like you've got someone sitting right by your side, like a personal concierge, that plans and responds to the way that you want to actually book your trip to Walt Disney World."

The entertainment company has also expanded its short-form content, adding a vertical video channel within the Disney+ mobile app. The company had previously announced a partnership with TikTok to bring creator content to the streaming app.
