#109. Anthropic likely to price IPO above the current range of $70 to $78, sources tell CNBC

Anthropic is likely to price its IPO above the current range of $70 to $78, multiple sources told CNBC's David Faber on Tuesday.

The artificial intelligence company is planning to close the order books and lock in total demand at 4 p.m. ET on Tuesday, according to the people, who asked not to be named because they were not authorized to speak publicly. Anthropic is slated to list on the Nasdaq under the ticker symbol "ANT" and start trading on Nov. 19.

If Anthropic's IPO proceeds as planned, the company will debut roughly five months after SpaceX went public in a record offering that raised $85.7 billion. But Anthropic is facing a public market that's grown increasingly skeptical of the AI trade, as well as mounting and widespread antipathy toward the technology in the U.S.

A group of researchers, including Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei, co-founded the company in 2021 after defecting from OpenAI, now its chief rival. Anthropic is best known for developing a family of AI models called Claude, and it underwent a period of explosive growth in 2026, driven largely by the popularity of its AI coding tool, Claude Code.

For the nine months ended Sept. 30, Anthropic said it generated $33.24 billion in revenue, up roughly 1,200% from the same period last year, according to its prospectus. It reported a $104 billion net loss during the nine-month period, though most of that was due to the change in fair value of convertible notes and derivative liabilities. Anthropic lost $25.1 billion over the same stretch in 2025, the filing said.

Anthropic has long worked to establish a reputation as a safety-conscious AI company, and its prospectus included more than 90 pages of risk factors for investors to consider ahead of its listing.

"I think if we do this right, we can create an age of miracles where humanity is able to solve problems that have eluded us for millennia," Amodei said in Anthropic's roadshow video. "We have to make sure that all the risks, all the ways that things can go wrong with this technology, we very carefully address those risks as we bring the benefits and as we have a viable business at the same time."

"I invite you to join us on the next phase of this journey," Amodei said.

Nvidia general counsel Tim Teter will also appear before the committees on Dec. 2, a statement from the lawmakers said.

#105. Home Depot names Richard McPhail as new CEO, beats earnings expectations

Home Depot on Tuesday announced its Chief Financial Officer Richard McPhail will become CEO of the company, replacing Ted Decker, effective next week.

Alongside McPhail's appointment, Home Depot said Ann-Marie Campbell, currently senior executive vice president of U.S. stores and operations, will become president and chief operating officer. Jordan Broggi has been chosen as CFO to replace McPhail.

Decker, CEO since 2022, had been on temporary medical leave since August. The company said on Tuesday that Decker has returned from leave but is now stepping down from his role. He will continue to serve as chair of the board of directors, the company said.

"I'm glad to be back and grateful for the support I received," Decker told analysts on Tuesday. "The board and I have agreed that now is the right time to move to the next phase of our succession plan."

McPhail has been with the company since 2005, holding multiple executive roles.

"We're not going to change course," McPhail told CNBC's Becky Quick.

He said the company will continue to lean into winning professional shoppers like contractors and using AI tools to encourage sales. McPhail added that while the company faces more challenges now than it did at the start of the year, it is still winning share of the home improvement market.

Home Depot has been pressured by a sluggish housing market, higher mortgage rates and a more cautious consumer. Still, the company has previously said it's seen resilience among its pro shoppers who are still shopping for their home improvement projects.

Shares of Home Depot sank more than 3% following the announcement and the retailer's fiscal third-quarter results.

The CEO change came as the home improvement retailer posted quarterly earnings and revenue that beat analysts' expectations. The company said comparable sales rose 2.2% during the period, compared with estimates of up 1.8%, according to StreetAccount.

Here's how Home Depot performed in its fiscal third quarter compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

The company reported net income of $3.71 billion, or $3.72 per share, compared with $3.60 billion, or $3.62 per share, the year prior. Excluding one-time items, Home Depot reported adjusted earnings per share of $3.86.

Revenue rose 4.9% compared to the year-ago period.

"We also are confident that we are taking material share from the market and from competition," McPhail said on the call with analysts.

In the third quarter, the company said online comparable sales grew around 13%, which marked its sixth straight quarter of double-digit positive growth for the metric.

The company said it saw positive comparable sales numbers in 13 out of 16 of its merchandising departments, including notable strength in the power department, which posted double-digit percentage growth.

For the full year, Home Depot said it expects total sales to rise roughly 4.5%, the top end of its previously provided range. It also anticipates comparable sales will grow around 1.4%, compared with an outlook of flat to a 2% increase.

Home Depot added it expects adjusted earnings per share to rise 2% this year, compared with prior guidance of flat to a 4% increase.

#99. Trump has arrived in Asia. Here’s what he said about Iran and Deutsche Bank

U.S. President Donald Trump has arrived for his high-stakes trip to Asia, and took a range of questions on subjects from Iran to Deutsche Bank leaking details about his tax.

The president landed in Manila, Philippines, for the initial leg of his trip on Tuesday and answered rapid-fire questions from the press while meeting with other leaders.

Asked about a possible Iranian threat to Washington, D.C., he said that, if there were an attack, Iran would "pay a price the likes of which no country has ever paid before."

The question was about CNN reporting that U.S. intelligence suggested the country was planning to target senior members of Trump's administration with a dirty bomb or chemical weapon in the U.S. capital.

"We take it very seriously because they happen to be crazy," Trump said. CNBC has not independently verified CNN's report.

But the president also said he believed his administration was "very close to getting it done" when it came to the Iran war.

While Trump was airborne, the Financial Times reported Deutsche Bank had mistakenly leaked details of his taxes in March 2017, during his first presidential term. The mistake was kept from the White House at the time, per the FT.

Asked about this in Manila, the president said: "I paid a lot of tax."

"They shouldn't have done it. It's illegal for them to do it," he added.

Trump was also asked about Nvidia's announcement on Tuesday of a new National Security and Export Control Oversight Committee. The company, which reports earnings later on Tuesday, is reeling from the sudden departure of CEO Jensen Huang, who resigned last week after having an affair with a woman U.S. intelligence reportedly believes to be a Chinese spy.

"It's a great company. It's a great idea," Trump said.

Trump is due to fly to Shenzhen, China, where he is scheduled to meet Chinese President Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin for a three-way meeting on Thursday, on the sidelines of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit.

He is also due to hold a separate bilateral meeting with Xi. Asked by a reporter if he would have a separate meeting with only Putin, Trump said: "I don't know, but I would, if it's available, I would do that. He's coming. I mean, it's really with the guests. I am the guest of President Xi."

Trump met the leaders of Australia, India and Japan as part of the Quad grouping in the Philippines and the prime minister of Thailand. He is due to China on Wednesday.

Speaking at a dinner with Southeast Asian leaders as the event wrapped up for the day, Trump touted his record during his second term, citing investment into the U.S., trade deals made with countries represented by attendees at the dinner, energy infrastructure buildouts and America's AI industry.

Trump, who has led a campaign to rename artificial intelligence "super intelligence," or "SI," told leaders around the table that the U.S. is "leading the world" in the technology.

"We have the world's most powerful SI ecosystem," he said. "We're leading everybody by a lot, and we hope to keep it that way, and we will keep it that way."

He added that the U.S. has "the strongest and most capable military on the planet," pointing to investments in the armed forces and the country's defense sector.

"We make the greatest equipment. You saw that in Venezuela. You see it in Iran. You see it everywhere," Trump said.

#98. Nike lays out financial goals ahead of first investor day in 9 years

Nike said Tuesday ahead of its first investor day in nine years that it was reaffirming its 2027 financial guidance.

The retailer also laid out a financial framework and said that by the end of fiscal 2031 it's targeting approximately $5 billion in incremental revenue growth from fiscal year 2027, operating margins of 12% to 14% and adjusted earnings per share of $3.00 to $3.50. The company said higher-quality revenue, gross margin expansion and productivity improvements would help it reach its goals.

Nike's 2027 financial guidance includes expectations for revenue to decline in the high-single-digit range, an adjusted operating margin of approximately 6%, and adjusted EPS of $1.15 to $1.35.

The company also introduced a strategy it's calling Full Stride Ahead, which the company said aims "to get closer to athletes and focus resources on the greatest opportunities for long-term growth."

"We're concentrating our resources where we have the greatest potential to win," Nike CEO Elliott Hill said in a statement. "We're also changing how we operate — delivering breakthrough products, building deeper local connections and moving faster to capture demand. Together, these actions are designed to return NIKE to sustainable, profitable growth."

Nike said it would focus on performance sports and sportswear opportunities "with the greatest potential for long-term growth," and said its five priority sports would be running, training, global football, basketball and outdoor.

Nike is holding an investor day at its Oregon headquarters Tuesday as the company aims to prove to investors that its turnaround strategy is yielding success after a yearslong slump.

Last month, the company reported mixed fiscal first-quarter results as it announced a restructuring plan, including layoffs that it said would start next year. It's the company's third round of layoffs to be announced this year.

Nike's net income and revenue both fell during the most recent period compared with a year ago, and the company said it expects revenue to decline by a high single-digit percentage in fiscal 2027.

The new strategy, dubbed Pace, focuses on the company's supply chain and reorganizing into three geographies. Nike said the turnaround plan is expected to deliver roughly $2.5 billion in savings through fiscal 2031.

Nike has been seeing significant sustained declines in its China market, where revenue last quarter dropped 26%. Experts have said the company is losing relevance in the country, while Nike has said it's actively working to win over younger consumers.

At the same time, Nike announced last quarter that it's creating a new global campus in India as part of its new growth strategy, hoping the country will help boost its performance. The company said it was remaining focused on China even as it begins to build out its India presence.

CEO Elliott Hill's turnaround strategy includes improving different parts of the business at different rates based on priority. While it's seen some signs of progress in the North America business, Hill has said that performance isn't enough to offset the pressures on the company elsewhere, including internationally.

The Nike consumer has also been under increased macroeconomic pressure as geopolitical tensions mount and inflation rises.

Shares of Nike have fallen 50% over the past two years.

#93. Republicans outspent Democrats for first time in over a decade in record 2026 midterm election political ad blitz

Political advertisers spent a record $12.06 billion during the 2025-26 elections, making it the first time a midterm cycle outspent a presidential cycle, according to a report from advertising intelligence company AdImpact released Tuesday.

Total spending exceeded AdImpact's June projection by more than $460 million.

The elections also marked the first time in over a decade that Republicans have outspent Democrats on advertisements, according to AdImpact.

Despite Republicans leading spending, John Link, senior vice president of data at AdImpact, said that "Dems still led or were generally even with overall airings and impressions."

The record spending extended across the board, with every type of race reaching a new high. Senate spending hit $3.39 billion, while spending on gubernatorial elections reached $2.61 billion, spending on races in the House of Representatives grew to $2.44 billion and state legislative races totaled $602 million, all records, according to AdImpact.

The 2026 midterm cycle was largely powered by megadonors, with SpaceX and Tesla CEO Elon Musk becoming the largest contributor by funneling more than $366 million into campaigns.

The biggest donors in this year's election disproportionately favored Republicans, according to a CNBC analysis of Federal Election Commission records. Fourteen out of the 20 largest donor groups supported GOP candidates, according to that analysis.

Republicans spent $5.51 billion on political advertisements, compared with $5.11 billion spent by Democrats, according to AdImpact.

While Republican spending increased by 27% from 2024, spending by Democrats fell by 4%.

Fundraisers told CNBC in interviews that Democratic donors' hesitancy largely stems from memories of Kamala Harris' defeat in 2024, unease about the rise of democratic socialism and distrust in party leadership.

Though they were outspent, Democrats made sweeping gains against Republicans in races nationwide, taking control of both the House and the Senate and flipping seats in some of the most competitive places, including Texas and Kansas.

It is not the first time spending did not align with the winners. In 2024, President Donald Trump defeated former Vice President Harris despite Democrats outspending Republicans.

While party majorities in Congress have switched hands, the most popular medium for political ads has not changed.

Broadcast television represented the largest category for spending in this year's election, accounting for 49%, or $5.85 billion, of political ad spending, according to AdImpact. Broadcast's share of political ad dollars is up slightly from 48% in 2024.

Broadcast TV's strength comes despite facing pressure from falling viewership and increasing competition from streaming.

Industry experts have told CNBC that political advertisers continue to spend on broadcast because it reaches local markets and active voting populations. The Associated Press also reported that airtime was so expensive during the midterm elections that some candidates, including Republican Ken Paxton in Texas, bought ads during national sporting events, figuring that reaching enough constituents through a national broadcast might actually be less expensive than buying across the state's local television markets.

The fastest-growing categories in this year's cycle were connected TV, or internet-connected devices like Apple TV or Roku, with $2.66 billion, and digital, with $1.91 billion. Both mediums grew double digits from the 2024 presidential election. Spending on cable and radio declined 7% and 13%, respectively, from 2024.
